# Docs 1501-1600

## Batch 151: Professional WiFi (Connect) 1 (9-12 (High School))

## 1. Project 1501: Professional WiFi (Connect) Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Implement a robust Finite State Machine (FSM) to manage the WiFi connection lifecycle (IDLE, CONNECTING, CONNECTED, FAILED) without blocking the main performance loop.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Finite State Machine (FSM)**: A logic structure where the system exists in one of several discrete states, switching based on events.
*   **Non-Blocking Logic**: Designing code that continues executing other tasks while waiting for slow responses (like network handshakes).
*   **Timeout Management**: Implementing a countdown timer to prevent the system from getting stuck in a "Connecting" loop indefinitely.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Built-in CYW43439 Infineon WiFi chip |
| **Power** | VSYS | Requires stable 5V for RF transmission bursts |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_wlan`** (Activate WiFi chip)
*   **from Network, drag `wlan_connect`** (Start association)
*   **from Network, drag `check_wifi_status`** (Get current link state)
*   **from Time, drag `get_ticks_ms`** (Timestamp tracking)

### 7. Variables
*   **state**: String (Stores current phase: "IDLE", "CONNECTING", etc.).
*   **start_time**: Number (Stores the timestamp when the connection started).
*   **SSID / PASS**: Strings (Wireless credentials).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define States**:
    *   From **Variables**, drag `set variable` block.
    *   Create variables for **IDLE**, **CONNECTING**, **CONNECTED**, and **FAILED**.
2.  **Activate Radio**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `init_wlan` block.
    *   **Snap** into the start of the program.
    *   Set **Interface** to **STA_IF**.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Core State Logic**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever` block.
    *   Inside, drag a **Logic** `if / else if` block.
4.  **Handle IDLE State**:
    *   Check `if state == IDLE`.
    *   From **Network**, drag `wlan_connect`.
    *   **Snap** inside the IDLE condition.
    *   From **Time**, drag `get_ticks_ms` and set **start_time**.
    *   Set **state** to **CONNECTING**.
5.  **Handle CONNECTING State**:
    *   Check `else if state == CONNECTING`.
    *   From **Network**, drag `check_wifi_status`.
    *   If status is **CONNECTED**, set **state** to **CONNECTED**.
    *   If **ticks - start_time > 10000ms**, set **state** to **FAILED**.
6.  **Handle Recovery (FAILED)**:
    *   Check `else if state == FAILED`.
    *   After a **5 second** delay (using a non-blocking timer), set **state** to **IDLE** to retry.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the WiFi hardware and enters the **IDLE** state.
2.  **Process**: The system calls the connect command and immediately switches to **CONNECTING**.
3.  **Check**: The main loop continues to spin, checking the WiFi status every few milliseconds without freezing.
4.  **Decide**: If the router responds within 10 seconds, the IP address is assigned. If not, the system flags a timeout.
5.  **Output**: Upon connection, the system enters the **CONNECTED** state where your application logic runs.
6.  **Repeat**: If the signal is lost, the state machine restarts the cycle.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
import machine

# WiFi Configuration
SSID = "Your_SSID"
PASS = "Your_Password"

# State Constants
IDLE, CONNECTING, CONNECTED, FAILED = 0, 1, 2, 3

# Initialize variables
state = IDLE
start_time = 0
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)

print("WiFi Manager Started...")

while True:
    if state == IDLE:
        print("[IDLE] Connecting...")
        wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)
        start_time = time.ticks_ms()
        state = CONNECTING
        
    elif state == CONNECTING:
        # Non-blocking check for connection
        if wlan.isconnected():
            print(f"[CONNECTED] IP: {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
            state = CONNECTED
        # Check for 10-second timeout
        elif time.ticks_diff(time.ticks_ms(), start_time) > 10000:
            print("[FAILED] Timeout reached")
            wlan.disconnect()
            state = FAILED
            start_time = time.ticks_ms() # Capture time for delay
            
    elif state == CONNECTED:
        # Application logic goes here
        pass
        
    elif state == FAILED:
        # Non-blocking wait of 5 seconds before retrying
        if time.ticks_diff(time.ticks_ms(), start_time) > 5000:
            print("[RETRY] Refreshing connection...")
            state = IDLE

    # Small sleep to yield to background system tasks
    time.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Wait**: Using `time.sleep(10)` during a connection attempt. This makes the Pico unresponsive to buttons or sensors during the wait.
*   **Missing Disconnect**: Forgetting to call `wlan.disconnect()` on a timeout, which can leave the radio in an unstable halfway-connected state.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **LED Indicators**: Use an RGB LED to show the state (Blue for Connecting, Green for Connected, Red for Failed).

## 1. Project 1502: Asynchronous Professional WiFi (Connect) Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a non-blocking background task using `uasyncio` to manage WiFi status, ensuring the main application remains responsive during connection attempts.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Asynchronous Processing**: Running multiple routines concurrently by yielding control during wait periods.
*   **Background Tasks**: Long-running loops that manage hardware (like WiFi) independently of the main user logic.
*   **Awaiting**: Pausing a specific function to wait for an event without stopping the rest of the program.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Infineon CYW43439 WiFi stack |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_wlan`** (Setup radio)
*   **from Functions, drag `async_def`** (Create async routine)
*   **from Functions, drag `create_task`** (Run in background)
*   **from Time, drag `await_sleep`** (Non-blocking delay)

### 7. Variables
*   **wlan**: Object (The WiFi interface handler).
*   **wifi_connected**: Boolean (Status flag shared across tasks).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Initialize Radio**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `init_wlan` block.
    *   **Snap** into the top level of the workspace.
2.  **Define Background Task**:
    *   From **Functions**, drag `async_def` block.
    *   Name it `maintain_wifi`.
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever` and **snap** inside.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Define Main Routine**:
    *   From **Functions**, drag `async_def` block.
    *   Name it `main`.
    *   From **Loops**, drag `pico_forever` and **snap** inside.
4.  **Perform Parallel Work**:
    *   Inside `main`, drag a `print` block.
    *   **Snap** `await_sleep` with **1 second**.
    *   This represents your robot code running while WiFi is handled separately.

**C. Event/Condition Handling**
5.  **Manage Connection in Background**:
    *   Inside `maintain_wifi`, drag if-statement: `if not wlan.isconnected()`.
    *   From **Network**, drag `wlan_connect`.
    *   **Snap** inside the condition.
    *   Use `await_sleep(5)` to poll status without blocking Core 0.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico launches the `asyncio` event loop and spawns two tasks: `main` and `maintain_wifi`.
2.  **Process**: The `main` task starts printing "Reading Sensors..." immediately.
3.  **Action**: In the background, `maintain_wifi` notices the radio is down and initiates a connection.
4.  **Check**: The CPU rapidly switches between both tasks, giving the appearance of simultaneous execution.
5.  **Output**: The terminal shows sensor data *and* connection logs interleaved, proving the system never "froze".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio

# Credentials
SSID = "Your_SSID"
PASS = "Your_Password"

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)

async def maintain_wifi_connection():
    """Background task to ensure the link stays up."""
    while True:
        if not wlan.isconnected():
            print("[AUTO-WIFI] Connection lost. Reconnecting...")
            wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)
            
            # Non-blocking wait for connection
            for _ in range(10):
                if wlan.isconnected():
                    print("[AUTO-WIFI] Back Online!")
                    break
                await asyncio.sleep(1)
        
        # Check link quality every 10 seconds
        await asyncio.sleep(10)

async def main():
    """Main application logic (e.g., sensor reading)."""
    print("[SYSTEM] Starting Main Loop...")
    
    # Spawn the WiFi watchdog as a background task
    asyncio.create_task(maintain_wifi_connection())
    
    while True:
        # Simulate heavy work
        print("[MAIN] Performing sensor sweep...")
        # CRITICAL: Always use await asyncio.sleep in async loops
        await asyncio.sleep(2)

# Start the event loop
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    print("System Shutdown")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Using time.sleep**: Using the standard `time.sleep()` inside an `async def` function will freeze the entire event loop, stopping ALL tasks.
*   **Zombie Tasks**: Forgetting to use `create_task` means the function will be defined but never actually run in the background.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Web Server**: Now that WiFi is handled in the background, try adding a second background task that hosts a tiny web dashboard.

## 1. Project 1503: Object-Oriented Professional WiFi (Connect) Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Encapsulate complex WiFi connection logic, including timeouts and status polling, into a reusable `NetworkManager` class to simplify main software architecture.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Object-Oriented Programming (OOP)**: Building custom "types" (classes) that bundle hardware controls and data into objects.
*   **Encapsulation**: Hiding messy low-level logic (loops, delays) behind clean, simple methods like `.connect()`.
*   **Method Parameters**: Allowing users to pass settings (like SSID or Timeout) into the class to make it flexible.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439 Radio via SPI |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Program, drag `class_definition`** (Define the manager)
*   **from Functions, drag `method_definition`** (Create .connect and .ip)
*   **from Variables, drag `instance_variable`** (Store the wlan object)
*   **from Network, drag `init_wlan`** (Inside constructor)

### 7. Variables
*   **self.wlan**: The internal radio object.
*   **nm**: The "Network Manager" instance used in the main program.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Blueprint**:
    *   From **Program**, drag `def __init__`.
    *   From **Network**, drag `init_wlan`.
    *   **Snap** inside and set to `self.wlan`.
2.  **Create Connection Wrapper**:
    *   From **Functions**, drag `def connect`.
    *   Add parameters: `ssid`, `password`.
    *   **Snap** a `while` loop inside to poll for status.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Instantiate Class**:
    *   At the bottom of your code, drag `nm = NetworkManager()`.
4.  **Execute Connection**:
    *   From **Variables**, drag `if` statement.
    *   **Snap** `nm.connect("SSID", "PASS")` as the condition.
    *   If True, start your app.

**C. Event/Condition Handling**
5.  **Expose Data**:
    *   From **Functions**, drag `def get_ip`.
    *   **Return** `self.wlan.ifconfig()`.
    *   This allows the user to see the IP without understanding how the radio stores it.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The system creates a `NetworkManager` object, which automatically wakes up the WiFi chip.
2.  **Process**: The user calls `nm.connect()`, passing their credentials.
3.  **Action**: Inside the class, the code starts the association and enters a private loop to wait for the signal.
4.  **Result**: The class returns `True` to the main code, signaling that the network is ready.
5.  **Output**: The main code prints the assigned IP address by asking the class for it.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time

class NetworkManager:
    """A clean wrapper for Pico W WiFi operations."""
    
    def __init__(self):
        # Configure hardware on object creation
        self.wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
        self.wlan.active(True)
        print("[SYS] WiFi Hardware Online")

    def connect(self, ssid, password, timeout=15):
        """Attempts connection and blocks until success or timeout."""
        if self.wlan.isconnected():
            return True
            
        print(f"[WIFI] Connecting to {ssid}...")
        self.wlan.connect(ssid, password)
        
        # Internal loop hidden from the main program
        start = time.time()
        while time.time() - start < timeout:
            if self.wlan.isconnected():
                print("[WIFI] Handshake Success!")
                return True
            time.sleep(0.5)
            
        print("[WIFI] Critical Failure: Timeout")
        return False

    def get_ip(self):
        """Returns the current IP address or 'None'."""
        if self.wlan.isconnected():
            return self.wlan.ifconfig()[0]
        return None

# --- Main Application ---
# Main code stays clean and readable
manager = NetworkManager()

if manager.connect("Home_WiFi", "secure_pass"):
    print(f"Internet Ready! IP: {manager.get_ip()}")
else:
    print("Running in Offline Mode.")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Variable Scope**: If you don't use `self.wlan`, the WiFi object won't be accessible by other methods (like `get_ip`) because it will "die" after the first function ends.
*   **Hard-Coding**: Putting your WiFi password inside the Class `__init__`. Classes should be "Generic" so you can use them in multiple projects.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-SSID Class**: Update the `connect` method to take a *list* of known networks and try each one until it finds a signal.

## 1. Project 1504: Robust Professional WiFi (Connect) Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
Manage network congestion and avoid "The Thundering Herd" problem by implementing an exponential backoff algorithm with jitter for connection retries.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Exponential Backoff**: A retry strategy that doubles the wait time between each failure (e.g., 1s, 2s, 4s...) to prevent overloading the network.
*   **Jitter**: Adding a small, random variation to the retry delay so multiple devices don't try to reconnect at the exact same millisecond.
*   **Polite Clients**: Communication patterns that intelligently back off during service outages to conserve resources.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Infineon CYW43439 WiFi stack |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `power_of`** (Calculate 2^n)
*   **from Math, drag `random`** (Add Jitter)
*   **from Logic, drag `while_not`** (Retry until successful)
*   **from Variables, drag `increment`** (Count failed attempts)

### 7. Variables
*   **attempt**: Number (Current failure count).
*   **delay**: Number (Seconds to wait before trying again).
*   **MAX_DELAY**: Constant (Upper bound for wait time).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Limits**:
    *   From **Variables**, drag `set variable` to create **MAX_DELAY**.
    *   Set to **60 seconds**.
2.  **Start Counter**:
    *   Create variable **attempt** and set to **0**.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Create Retry Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `while not` block.
    *   **Snap** condition: `wlan.isconnected()`.
4.  **Perform Connection**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `wlan_connect` and **snap** inside.
    *   Add a **5 second** static wait for the initial handshake.
5.  **Calculate Backoff**:
    *   `If` not connected, use **Math** `2 ** attempt` to find delay.
    *   **Snap** `min(delay, MAX_DELAY)` to cap the wait.
6.  **Add Jitter**:
    *   From **Functions**, add `random.uniform(0.1, 1.0)`.
    *   Add this value to your delay.

**C. Event/Condition Handling**
7.  **Reset or Continue**:
    *   If successful, print "Connected" and reset **attempt** to 0.
    *   If failure, use **Variables** `increment` on **attempt** and call **Time** `sleep(delay)`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico tries to connect to the WiFi.
2.  **Event**: The router is turned off. The first attempt fails.
3.  **Process**: The system waits 2 seconds (2^1).
4.  **Action**: The router remains off. Second failure. Pico waits 4 seconds (2^2) plus a random 0.4 seconds.
5.  **Steady State**: As failures continue, the Pico reaches the 60-second cap and stops increasing the wait time.
6.  **Result**: The network is not flooded, and battery power is saved by sleeping longer between pointless attempts.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
import random

SSID = "MyWiFi"
PASS = "12345678"

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)

def resilient_connect():
    attempt = 0
    MAX_DELAY = 60 # Cap wait at 1 minute
    
    while not wlan.isconnected():
        print(f"[RETRY] attempt {attempt + 1}...")
        wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)
        
        # Initial wait for handshake
        time.sleep(5)
        
        if wlan.isconnected():
            print("[SUCCESS] Online!")
            return True
            
        # Calculate Exponential Backoff: 2^n
        delay = min(2 ** attempt, MAX_DELAY)
        # Add Jitter: random variation between 0 and 1 second
        jitter = random.random()
        total_delay = delay + jitter
        
        print(f"[FAILED] Retrying in {total_delay:.2f}s")
        time.sleep(total_delay)
        
        attempt += 1

# Start the process
resilient_connect()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Integer Overflow**: On some platforms, `2 ** 100` might crash the math unit. Always use `min()` to cap the result.
*   **Missing Jitter**: Without jitter, 1000 Picos in a hotel that lose power at the same time will all "attack" the router at the exact same millisecond when power returns.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Flash Code**: Flash an LED slowly during the "backoff sleep" phase to show the device is not dead, just waiting to try again.

## 1. Project 1505: Professional WiFi (Connect) with Error Handling

### 2. Learning Objective
Diagnose network failures by decoding MicroPython WiFi status codes into human-readable error messages and logging them to a persistent file for maintenance.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Status Code Mapping**: Translating cryptic driver integers (e.g., -3, -1) into actionable text (e.g., "Bad Auth").
*   **Persistent Logging**: Writing diagnostic data to the Pico's internal flash memory so it survives a power cycle.
*   **System Diagnostics**: Developing a "Self-Reporting" system that helps technicians identify issues without opening the case.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Infineon CYW43439 WiFi stack |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `check_wifi_status`** (Get integer code)
*   **from Logic, drag `switch_case`** (Map code to string)
*   **from Storage, drag `write_file`** (Append to log.txt)
*   **from Text, drag `create_string_with`** (Format log line)

### 7. Variables
*   **status**: Number (The raw code from the driver).
*   **error_msg**: String (The translated message).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Status Constants**:
    *   From **Variables**, create constants for **LINK_UP (3)**, **LINK_BADAUTH (-3)**, etc.
2.  **Setup Radio**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `init_wlan` to start.

**B. Main Loop Phase**
3.  **Catch Faults**:
    *   Attempt connection.
    *   From **Network**, drag `check_wifi_status`.
    *   Use a **Logic** `if / else if` block to check the code.
4.  **Translate**:
    *   `If status == -3`, set **error_msg** to "Wrong Password".
    *   `If status == -2`, set **error_msg** to "SSID Not Found".

**C. Event/Condition Handling**
5.  **Record to Flash**:
    *   If **status < 3** (Error), drag **Storage** `append_to_file`.
    *   File: `"error_log.txt"`.
    *   Content: **error_msg**.
6.  **Close & Notify**:
    *   Print the error to the serial console for the user.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Action**: The technician enters the wrong password in the configuration.
2.  **Process**: The Pico tries to connect and fails after 10 seconds.
3.  **Diagnosis**: The driver returns `-3`. The state machine translates this to "Bad Password Detected".
4.  **Logging**: The Pico opens the filesystem and writes "Error: Bad Password" to the log.
5.  **Result**: When the device is plugged into a PC for repair, the technician can read the log and immediately see what went wrong.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time

# Driver Constants
LINK_UP = 3
LINK_BADAUTH = -3
LINK_NONET = -2

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)

def diag_connect(ssid, pw):
    print(f"Dialing {ssid}...")
    wlan.connect(ssid, pw)
    
    # Wait for result
    time.sleep(10)
    
    status = wlan.status()
    
    # Mapper
    errors = {
        LINK_UP: "Connected!",
        LINK_BADAUTH: "ERROR: Wrong Password",
        LINK_NONET: "ERROR: Router Not Found",
        0: "ERROR: Interface Down",
        -1: "ERROR: Connection Failed"
    }
    
    result = errors.get(status, f"Unknown Error ({status})")
    print(result)
    
    # Persistent Logging
    if status != LINK_UP:
        try:
            with open("wifi_log.txt", "a") as f:
                f.write(f"TS:{time.time()} - {result}\n")
        except:
            print("Filesystem locked!")

# Test with bad credentials
diag_connect("Fake_Router", "nopass")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **File Overflow**: If the log grows too large, the Pico will run out of space. Always check file size before writing or implement "Circular Logging" (delete old logs).
*   **Waiting Too Long**: Writing to flash memory is slow. Don't log every second, only log meaningful errors.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Remote Dashboard**: Create a "Hidden" web page on the Pico that reads the `wifi_log.txt` file and displays it in the browser for remote diagnostic.

## 1. Project 1506: Optimized Professional WiFi (Connect) Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Search for and connect to the strongest known network in a multi-AP environment by analyzing Signal Strength (RSSI) from radio scans.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Network Scanning**: Listening to all surrounding radio beacons to identify active Access Points (APs).
*   **RSSI (Received Signal Strength Indicator)**: A measurement (in dBm) of how strong a signal is. Higher numbers (closer to 0) mean better signal.
*   **Sorting Algorithms**: Organizing a list of found networks to prioritize the best connection candidate.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439 Radio Scan Mode |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `wlan_scan`** (Get list of signals)
*   **from Lists, drag `sort_list_by_index`** (Sort by RSSI)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_list_contains`** (Check known networks)
*   **from Network, drag `wlan_connect`** (Join the best one)

### 7. Variables
*   **results**: List (Raw data from the scan).
*   **known_nets**: Dictionary (Your SSID/Password pairs).
*   **best_ssid**: String (The network with the highest RSSI).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Discovery Phase**
1.  **Define Trusted Sources**:
    *   Initialize a dictionary **KNOWN_NETS** with your SSIDs and passwords.
2.  **Scan the Airwaves**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `wlan_scan`.
    *   **Snap** into a variable named `results`.

**B. Filtering & Ranking Phase**
3.  **Identify Viable Targets**:
    *   Create an empty list named `candidates`.
    *   Use a **Loops** `for each item in results` block.
    *   Inside, check: `if item[0] in KNOWN_NETS`.
    *   **Snap** the SSID and its RSSI into the `candidates` list.
4.  **Rank by Quality**:
    *   From **Lists**, drag `sort`.
    *   Choose index **3** (RSSI) and set to **Descending**.
    *   This puts the strongest signal at index 0.

**C. Selection Phase**
5.  **Initiate Handshake**:
    *   If `candidates` is not empty, get the first SSID.
    *   From **Network**, drag `wlan_connect`.
    *   **Snap** using the SSID and password from your dictionary.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico listens to all 2.4GHz channels.
2.  **Process**: It finds "Home_Weak" (-85 dBm), "Home_Strong" (-52 dBm), and "Hacker_NET" (-40 dBm).
3.  **Filter**: It discards "Hacker_NET" because it's not in the trusted dictionary.
4.  **Action**: It compares -52 and -85. Since -52 is higher, it picks "Home_Strong".
5.  **Result**: The device connects to the best possible signal, ensuring data reliability.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)

# Trusted Network Store
KNOWN_NETS = {
    "Home_LivingRoom": "pass123",
    "Home_Bedroom": "pass123",
    "Office_Guest": "guestaccess"
}

def find_best_signal():
    print("[SCAN] Surveying radio landscape...")
    networks = wlan.scan()
    # Format: (ssid, bssid, channel, rssi, authmode, hidden)
    
    candidates = []
    
    for n in networks:
        ssid = n[0].decode('utf-8')
        rssi = n[3]
        
        if ssid in KNOWN_NETS:
            print(f"[FOUND] {ssid} at {rssi} dBm")
            candidates.append({'ssid': ssid, 'rssi': rssi})
            
    if not candidates:
        print("[FAIL] No trusted networks found in range.")
        return None
        
    # Sort by RSSI (Higher is better)
    candidates.sort(key=lambda x: x['rssi'], reverse=True)
    
    winner = candidates[0]['ssid']
    print(f"[WINNER] Strongest signal: {winner}")
    return winner

# Main Execution
target = find_best_signal()
if target:
    wlan.connect(target, KNOWN_NETS[target])
    
    # Wait for completion
    timeout = 15
    while timeout > 0 and not wlan.isconnected():
        timeout -= 1
        time.sleep(1)
        
    if wlan.isconnected():
        print(f"[ONLINE] Connected to {target} with IP {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **SSID Decoding**: Scanned SSIDs are returned as `bytes`. You MUST use `.decode('utf-8')` to compare them with your dictionary strings.
*   **Mesh Problems**: In mesh networks, you might see the same SSID twice. The logic handles this by picking the one with the better RSSI.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Roaming Manager**: Put this logic in an `async` loop that runs every 5 minutes. If a better signal appears (e.g., as you walk through a building), switch to it!

## 1. Project 1507: Professional WiFi (Connect) Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Implement a telemetry pipeline that gathers hardware health data, serializes it into JSON format, and transmits it via the MQTT protocol to a central dashboard.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Telemetry**: The collection and transmission of remote data for monitoring and analysis.
*   **JSON Serialization**: Converting data structures (like dictionaries) into standard strings that can be read by web servers.
*   **MQTT (Message Queuing Telemetry Transport)**: A lightweight messaging protocol designed for high-latency, low-bandwidth IoT environments.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439 Radio via SPI |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_mqtt_client`** (Connect to server)
*   **from Network, drag `mqtt_publish`** (Send data)
*   **from Text, drag `json_encode`** (Prepare packet)
*   **from Network, drag `get_rssi`** (Measure signal)

### 7. Variables
*   **client**: Object (The MQTT connection handle).
*   **payload**: String (The JSON data packet).
*   **stats**: Dictionary (Temporary data container).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Internet Setup**:
    *   Connect to WiFi using Project 1501 logic.
2.  **Establish Broker Connection**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `init_mqtt_client`.
    *   Enter broker address (e.g., `mqtt.eclipseprojects.io`).
    *   **Snap** to variable `client`.

**B. Data Processing Phase**
3.  **Collect Hardware Stats**:
    *   Create a variable `stats` and set to a new Dictionary.
    *   **Snap** `wlan.status('rssi')` into key "signal".
    *   **Snap** `wlan.ifconfig()[0]` into key "ip".
4.  **Serialize for Transmission**:
    *   From **Text**, drag `json_encode`.
    *   **Snap** `stats` inside. Output to variable `payload`.

**C. Transmission Phase**
5.  **Ship the Packet**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `mqtt_publish`.
    *   **Snap** parameters: topic `"pico/health"`, message `payload`.
6.  **Loop & Sleep**:
    *   Use a **Loops** `forever` and **Time** `wait 60 seconds`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W joins the local network and shakes hands with the global MQTT broker.
2.  **Process**: Every minute, the Pico checks its signal strength and gets its own internal clock time.
3.  **Action**: The system converts `{'signal': -60}` into the string `'{"signal": -60}'`.
4.  **Result**: The message is blasted into the cloud where a dashboard (like MQTT Explorer) catches it.
5.  **Output**: You see live updates of your Pico's health from anywhere in the world.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import ujson
import time
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient

# User Settings
SSID = "MyWiFi"
PASS = "12345678"
BROKER = "test.mosquitto.org"
TOPIC = "pico/device1/telemetry"

def start_pipeline():
    # 1. Network Connect
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
    wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)
    
    while not wlan.isconnected():
        time.sleep(1)
    
    print(f"Internet Ready: {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
    
    # 2. Setup MQTT
    client = MQTTClient("PicoMonitor_X", BROKER)
    try:
        client.connect()
        print("Connected to Dashboard")
    except:
        print("Broker Offline")
        return

    # 3. Telemetry Loop
    while True:
        try:
            # Gather Stats
            data = {
                "rssi": wlan.status('rssi'),
                "ip": wlan.ifconfig()[0],
                "uptime": time.ticks_ms() / 1000
            }
            
            # Serialize
            packet = ujson.dumps(data)
            
            # Publish
            print(f"Sending Telemetry: {packet}")
            client.publish(TOPIC, packet)
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Network error: {e}")
            
        time.sleep(60) # Wait 1 minute

# Run the system
start_pipeline()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Unique Topics**: If everyone use `pico/test`, you will see hundreds of other people's data. Always add a unique ID to your topic (like your name).
*   **JSON Keys**: Forgetting to wrap keys in double quotes. `ujson.dumps()` does this for you automatically, so never build JSON strings by hand!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Sensor Injection**: Add a temperature sensor (DHT11) and include the `"temp": 24` reading in your JSON packet for a complete weather station.

## 1. Project 1508: Secure Professional WiFi (Connect) Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Securely manage WiFi credentials by separating sensitive data into a external JSON "Secrets" file, shielding passwords from the main application code.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Secrets Management**: The practice of hiding passwords and API keys from shared source code.
*   **Configuration Files**: Using readable files (like `.json`) to store settings that the main program loads at runtime.
*   **Safe Execution**: Using `try/except` blocks to handle missing configuration files without crashing the device.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439 Radio via SPI |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Storage, drag `open_file`** (Read from disk)
*   **from Text, drag `json_decode`** (Parse the file)
*   **from Logic, drag `try_catch`** (Prevent crashes)
*   **from Network, drag `wlan_connect`** (Using loaded variables)

### 7. Variables
*   **cfg**: Dictionary (Container for all loaded settings).
*   **ssid**: String (Extracted from the config).
*   **password**: String (Extracted from the config).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Storage Phase**
1.  **Prepare the Vault**:
    *   Create a new file on your Pico named `secrets.json`.
    *   Type the following exactly: `{"ssid": "HomeNetwork", "password": "MySecretPassword123"}`.
    *   **Save** it to the root of the device.

**B. Initialization Phase**
2.  **Access the Disk**:
    *   From **Storage**, drag `open_file` block. Set mode to `"r"` (read).
3.  **Decode the Data**:
    *   From **Text**, drag `json_decode`.
    *   **Snap** the file handle inside. Store the result in variable `cfg`.

**C. Handshake Phase**
4.  **Extract & Connect**:
    *   Variable `ssid = cfg['ssid']`.
    *   Variable `password = cfg['password']`.
    *   From **Network**, drag `wlan_connect` and **snap** these variables.
5.  **Clean up**:
    *   From **Storage**, drag `close_file`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico boots and does not contain any passwords in its `main.py` file.
2.  **Process**: It looks for a file named `secrets.json`.
3.  **Action**: It reads the raw text, converts it into a Python dictionary, and isolates the sensitive strings.
4.  **Result**: The WiFi driver connects using the invisible credentials.
5.  **Output**: If you share your project code with a friend, they won't accidentally get your home WiFi password, as they don't have your `secrets.json` file.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import ujson
import time

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)

def load_and_connect():
    # 1. Secured File Access
    try:
        print("[SECURE] Reading vault...")
        with open("secrets.json", "r") as f:
            config = ujson.load(f)
            
        ssid = config['ssid']
        pw = config['password']
        
    except OSError:
        print("[CRITICAL] secrets.json missing! Connect to USB to fix.")
        return
    except KeyError:
        print("[ERROR] JSON formatted incorrectly (check keys).")
        return

    # 2. Connection using secure variables
    print(f"[WIFI] Attempting connection to {ssid}...")
    wlan.connect(ssid, pw)
    
    # 3. Status Watcher
    for i in range(10):
        if wlan.isconnected():
            print(f"[ONLINE] Success! IP: {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
            return
        time.sleep(1)
        
    print("[FAIL] Connection timed out.")

# Run the secure bootloader
load_and_connect()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **JSON Formatting**: Forgetting the curly braces `{}` or using single quotes `'` (JSON ONLY allows double quotes `"`).
*   **File Loss**: Forgetting to upload `secrets.json` to the Pico after writing it. The code will throw an `OSError`.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1509: Multi-Threaded Professional WiFi (Connect)

### 2. Learning Objective
Manage concurrent operations by running a "WiFi Watchdog" on Core 1 while the main application logic runs independently on Core 0.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Multithreading**: Executing multiple lines of code at the same time using different CPU cores.
*   **Core Separation**: Decoupling critical system tasks (like connectivity) from user tasks (like motor control).
*   **Watchdog Pattern**: A process that monitors state and automatically fixes issues (like reconnecting after a crash).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Dual-Core ARM Cortex M0+ |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `run_on_core1`** (Launch parallel task)
*   **from Network, drag `wlan_reconnect`** (Inside the thread)
*   **from Time, drag `wait_seconds`** (Independent timers)
*   **from Logic, drag `forever_loop`** (Keep watchdog alive)

### 7. Variables
*   **IsOnline**: Global Boolean (Shared status between cores).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Watchdog Routine**:
    *   Create a function named `watchdog`.
2.  **Assign to Second Processor**:
    *   From **System**, drag `run_on_core1`.
    *   Name the task `watchdog`.

**B. Define Thread Logic (Core 1)**
3.  **Poll the Radio**:
    *   Inside the `watchdog` loop, drag `if not wlan.isconnected()`.
    *   From **Network**, drag `wlan_connect`.
    *   **Snap** a `wait 10 seconds` block to prevent aggressive retries.

**C. Define Main Logic (Core 0)**
4.  **Execute Primary Task**:
    *   In the main section, drag a **Loops** `forever`.
    *   Include your robot's motor or sensor logic.
5.  **Verify Independence**:
    *   Add a `print("Main App is Running")` block.
    *   Notice that this print continues even when WiFi is disconnected.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W powers on and immediately splits its brain in two.
2.  **Core 0**: Starts running your robot code (blinking lights, moving motors).
3.  **Core 1**: Tries to find the WiFi network in the background.
4.  **Event**: The router is reset. Core 0 continues to drive the robot safely.
5.  **Action**: Core 1 notices the loss of signal and quietly reconnects without freezing the robot's movement.
6.  **Result**: The system is "Hard-Real-Time," meaning critical tasks aren't delayed by networking glitches.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import _thread
import network
import machine
import time

# Credentials
SSID = "MyWiFi"
PASS = "12345678"

# --- CORE 1: BACKGROUND WATCHDOG ---
def wifi_watchdog():
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
    wlan.active(True)
    
    while True:
        if not wlan.isconnected():
            print("[CORE 1] Link Lost! Reconnecting...")
            wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)
            # Give it time to try without blocking Core 0
            time.sleep(10)
        else:
            # Check every 5 seconds if still online
            time.sleep(5)

# Start the watchdog on the second processor core
_thread.start_new_thread(wifi_watchdog, ())


# --- CORE 0: MAIN APPLICATION ---
# This part of the brain is never interrupted by WiFi logic
while True:
    print("[CORE 0] Robot performing safe operations...")
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Resource Collision**: If both cores try to write to the same file (`log.txt`) at the exact same time, the Pico might crash. Always use "Locks" or keep files restricted to one core only.
*   **Watchdog Failure**: If the code inside `wifi_watchdog` has a bug, the thread will die. Core 0 will keep running, but you'll never reconnect until you reboot.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual Status LEDs**: Program a Green LED that only blinks in the Core 0 loop, and a Blue LED that only blinks in the Core 1 loop. Unplug the router and watch the Blue one stop while the Green one keeps going!

## 1. Project 1510: Industrial Professional WiFi (Connect) Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a professional captive portal provisioning system that enables end-users to configure WiFi credentials via a smart web form without ever touching the source code.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Captive Portal**: A system that intercepts network traffic and displays a setup page (provisioning) to the user.
*   **Access Point (AP) Mode**: Turning the Pico W into its own WiFi router that others can connect to.
*   **Socket Programming**: Creating a basic web server (HTTP) that handles incoming requests and serves HTML forms.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439 AP/STA Dual Mode |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_ap_mode`** (Start local wifi)
*   **from Network, drag `open_web_form`** (Serve setup page)
*   **from Storage, drag `save_config`** (Writing credentials)
*   **from System, drag `reboot_device`** (Apply changes)

### 7. Variables
*   **is_configured**: Boolean (Checks if secrets.json exists).
*   **request**: String (The incoming data from the user's phone).
*   **html_form**: String (The actual code for the setup website).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Boot Check Phase**
1.  **Scan for Credentials**:
    *   From **System**, drag `check_file_exists`.
    *   If **False**, enter **Setup Mode**.
    *   If **True**, load credentials and enter **App Mode**.

**B. Provisioning Phase (Setup Mode)**
2.  **Broadcast SSID**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `init_ap_mode`.
    *   Set name to `"Pico_Setup"`.
3.  **Launch Web Server**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `socket_listen` on Port 80.
4.  **Serve Setup Form**:
    *   When a phone connects, send an HTML string containing `<form>` fields for SSID and Password.

**C. Application Phase (Normal Mode)**
5.  **Process Submission**:
    *   If the user submits the form, parse the text.
    *   From **Storage**, drag `write_to_file` ("secrets.json").
6.  **Cycle Power**:
    *   From **System**, drag `reboot`.
    *   The device restarts, finds the file, and joins the real network.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **First Boot**: The device sees it doesn't know any WiFi. It turns on its own WiFi called "Pico_Setup".
2.  **User Action**: You connect your phone to "Pico_Setup". No password is required.
3.  **Form Entry**: Your phone might show "Sign-in to network". You enter your home WiFi name and password into the page.
4.  **Saving**: The Pico takes that data and saves it into its internal memory (Flash).
5.  **Final Step**: The Pico reboots itself. This time it skips the setup and connects directly to your home router.
6.  **Result**: You have a consumer-ready product that anyone can set up in their house.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import ujson
import machine
import time
import os

# --- System Functions ---
def has_config():
    try:
        os.stat("secrets.json")
        return True
    except:
        return False

def provisioning_server():
    print("[SETUP] Entering Provisioning Mode...")
    ap = network.WLAN(network.AP_IF)
    ap.config(essid='Pico_Setup_Node')
    ap.active(True)
    
    # Simple Web Server
    s = socket.socket()
    s.bind(('0.0.0.0', 80))
    s.listen(1)
    
    while True:
        cl, addr = s.accept()
        request = cl.recv(1024).decode('utf-8')
        
        # Handle Save Request
        if "GET /save" in request:
            try:
                # Naive parser for SSID/PASS via URL
                # Example: /save?s=HomeWiFi&p=12345678
                query = request.split('?')[1].split(' ')[0]
                parts = query.split('&')
                ssid = parts[0].split('=')[1]
                pw = parts[1].split('=')[1]
                
                with open("secrets.json", "w") as f:
                    ujson.dump({'ssid': ssid, 'password': pw}, f)
                
                cl.send("HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n\r\nConfig Saved. Rebooting...")
                cl.close()
                time.sleep(2)
                machine.reset()
            except:
                cl.send("HTTP/1.1 400 Bad Request\r\n\r\nFormat Error")
        
        # Serve the page
        else:
            html = """<html><body>
            <h1>Pico W WiFi Setup</h1>
            <form action='/save'>
            SSID: <input name='s'><br>
            PASS: <input name='p'><br>
            <input type='submit' value='Save & Start'>
            </form></body></html>"""
            cl.send('HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Type: text/html\r\n\r\n')
            cl.send(html)
            cl.close()

# --- Main Bootloader ---
if has_config():
    print("[BOOT] Config found. Loading...")
    # Load and run original main_app logic here
    with open("secrets.json", "r") as f:
        cfg = ujson.load(f)
    print(f"Connecting to {cfg['ssid']}...")
else:
    provisioning_server()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Parsing Failures**: If the SSID contains special characters like `&` or `=`, the simple string splitting logic will break. Professional systems use URL decoding.
*   **Zombie AP**: Forgetting to shut down the Access Point after connecting in station mode. This wastes power and creates radio noise.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Signal Indicator**: Blink the LED slowly when in "Setup Mode" and keep it solid when in "App Mode" so the user knows what the device is doing without looking at a screen.

### Project 1511: Professional NTP Time Sync Driver Implementation
**Category:** Communication & Networking  
**Difficulty:** 8  
**Bloom's:** Analyze  

**Learning Objective:**  
Understand the underlying SNTP (Simple Network Time Protocol) by implementing a manual UDP client. Instead of using the "black box" `ntptime` library, we will construct the raw 48-byte packet, send it to a time server, and decode the binary response to handle the 1900-vs-1970 Epoch conversion manually.

**Concepts:**  
*   **UDP (User Datagram Protocol)**: A connectionless protocol ideal for time sync. Unlike TCP, it doesn't waste time establishing a session—we just fire a request and wait.  
*   **Socket**: The fundamental software endpoint for network data. We use `SOCK_DGRAM` for UDP communication.  
*   **Struct & Endianness**: Networks transmit numbers in "Big Endian" (most significant byte first). The Pico (ARM) uses "Little Endian". We must use `struct` to swap the byte order ensuring `0x00000001` doesn't become `0x01000000`.  
*   ## 1. Project 1511: Professional NTP Time Sync Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Communicate with global time servers by constructing and decoding raw UDP packets following the SNTP (Simple Network Time Protocol) specification.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **UDP (User Datagram Protocol)**: A fast, connectionless networking protocol used for time sync where speed is more important than delivery guarantees.
*   **Binary Packing (Struct)**: Converting Python numbers into the exact sequence of bits/bytes required by network protocols.
*   **Epoch Time**: The number of seconds elapsed since a specific date (usually Jan 1, 1970).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439 WiFi Chip |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `WiFi Connect`** (Join internet)
*   **from Network, drag `init_udp_socket`** (Create endpoint)
*   **from Text, drag `pack_binary`** (Build NTP packet)
*   **from Text, drag `unpack_binary`** (Read response)

### 7. Variables
*   **NTP_HOST**: String (e.g., "pool.ntp.org").
*   **packet**: Bytearray (The 48-byte protocol buffer).
*   **raw_time**: Integer (Seconds since 1900).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Join the Web**:
    *   Connect to WiFi using Project 1501 logic.
2.  **Prepare the Radio**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `init_udp_socket`.
    *   Set **Timeout** to 2 seconds.

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Construct the Packet**:
    *   Create a variable `packet` as a bytearray of size 48.
    *   From **Text**, use `pack_into_binary`.
    *   Set the first byte to `0x1B` (NTP version 3, Client mode).
4.  **Send the Request**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `udp_send_to`.
    *   Target: `NTP_HOST`, Port: `123`.

**C. Decoding Phase**
5.  **Listen for Response**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `udp_receive`.
    *   Store the 48-byte result in a variable named `msg`.
6.  **Extract the Data**:
    *   From **Text**, drag `unpack_binary`.
    *   Format: `"!I"` (Big-endian Unsigned Int).
    *   Offset: **40** (The Transmit Timestamp).
7.  **Calculate Local Time**:
    *   Subtract `2,208,988,800` from the result to convert from 1900-epoch to 1970-epoch.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W establishes an internet connection.
2.  **Process**: It builds a specialized 48-byte request packet and fires it into the UDP port 123 of a global server.
3.  **Action**: The server replies with a packet containing the current atomic time.
4.  **Result**: The Pico extracts the 32-bit number, performs the subtraction math, and prints the human-readable time.
5.  **Output**: "Current Time: 14:35:02" (UTC).

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import machine

# --- Server Settings ---
NTP_HOST = "pool.ntp.org"
NTP_PORT = 123
NTP_DELTA = 2208988800  # Offset between 1900 and 1970

def sync_ntp_driver():
    print(f"[NTP] Querying {NTP_HOST}...")
    
    # 1. Open UDP Socket
    s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
    s.settimeout(2.0)
    
    # 2. Build 48-byte packet
    packet = bytearray(48)
    packet[0] = 0x1B # NTP Client version 3
    
    try:
        # 3. Transmit
        addr = socket.getaddrinfo(NTP_HOST, NTP_PORT)[0][-1]
        s.sendto(packet, addr)
        
        # 4. Receive (48 bytes)
        msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
        
        # 5. Decode: The time is a Big-Endian Int at offset 40
        raw_seconds = struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0]
        
        # 6. Adjust Epoch
        actual_time = raw_seconds - NTP_DELTA
        return actual_time
        
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"[ERROR] Sync failure: {e}")
        return None
    finally:
        s.close() # CRITICAL: Free the socket resource

# --- Main App ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("MySSID", "MyPass")

while not wlan.isconnected():
    time.sleep(1)

t = sync_ntp_driver()
if t:
    tm = time.localtime(t)
    print(f"[SUCCESS] Real Time: {tm[3]:02}:{tm[4]:02}:{tm[5]:02}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Port 123 Blocked**: Some school/office networks block UDP Port 123. If code times out, try your phone's mobile hotspot.
*   **Socket Leak**: If you don't call `s.close()`, the Pico will eventually run out of memory and return an `OSError`.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Wall Clock**: Connect an I2C LCD and display the time in large digits for a professional digital clock.

## 1. Project 1512: Asynchronous Professional NTP Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Maintain accurate system time in the background using asynchronous tasks, ensuring that core device functions (like motor control) are never interrupted by network delays.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **AsyncIO (uasyncio)**: A framework for managing multi-tasking on a single CPU core.
*   **Coroutines**: Functions that can "yield" control back to the system, allowing other tasks to run while waiting for I/O.
*   **Periodic Syncing**: The practice of updating a local clock at regular intervals to compensate for hardware "drift."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439 WiFi/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_async_wifi`** (Non-blocking join)
*   **from System, drag `async_create_task`** (Start background job)
*   **from Time, drag `async_sleep`** (Yield control)
*   **from Smart Sensors, drag `update_rtc`** (Set clock)

### 7. Variables
*   **sync_interval**: Integer (Seconds between updates).
*   **last_sync**: Float (Uptime of most recent update).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Boot the Scheduler**:
    *   Using the `uasyncio` library, create a main entry point.
2.  **Establish Connection**:
    *   From **Network**, drag `init_async_wifi`.
    *   Use an `await` block to wait for the link without freezing the script.

**B. Defining Background Tasks**
3.  **Define Watcher**:
    *   Create a coroutine named `time_sync_task`.
    *   **Snap** a `while True` loop inside.
4.  **Perform Sync**:
    *   Inside the loop, call the UDP sync logic from Project 1511.
    *   If successful, update the **RTC**.
    *   **Snap** `await asyncio.sleep(600)` (10 minutes).

**C. Main Application Phase**
5.  **Define Core Logic**:
    *   Create another coroutine named `sensor_loop`.
    *   Blink an LED at high frequency (e.g., every 100ms).
6.  **Concurrent Execution**:
    *   From **System**, drag `async_create_task` for both functions.
    *   Observe that the LED never flickers or stops, even during the NTP network handshake.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W begins two jobs simultaneously using the Async scheduler.
2.  **Process**: Job 1 blinks an LED perfectly every 0.1 seconds.
3.  **Action**: Job 2 wakes up every 10 minutes, reaches out to the internet, and aligns the Pico's clock.
4.  **Result**: The user perceives two programs running at the same time on one chip.
5.  **Output**: "Clock Updated" appears in the console while the LED continues its steady heartbeat.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
import machine
import uasyncio as asyncio
import socket
import struct

NTP_DELTA = 2208988800

async def get_ntp_async():
    # Non-blocking-friendly UDP fetch
    s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
    s.settimeout(1.0)
    try:
        packet = bytearray(48); packet[0] = 0x1B
        addr = socket.getaddrinfo("pool.ntp.org", 123)[0][-1]
        s.sendto(packet, addr)
        msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
        val = struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0]
        return val - NTP_DELTA
    except:
        return None
    finally:
        s.close()

async def sync_task():
    print("[TASK] Time Sync Engine Online")
    rtc = machine.RTC()
    while True:
        print("[NTP] Syncing...")
        t = await get_ntp_async() # In real async sockets, we'd use non-blocking IO
        if t:
            tm = time.localtime(t)
            rtc.datetime((tm[0], tm[1], tm[2], tm[6] + 1, tm[3], tm[4], tm[5], 0))
            print(f"[NTP] Clock Aligned: {tm[3]:02}:{tm[4]:02}")
            await asyncio.sleep(600) # Wait 10 mins
        else:
            print("[WARN] Sync failed, retrying in 30s")
            await asyncio.sleep(30)

async def heartbeat_task():
    led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)
    while True:
        led.toggle()
        await asyncio.sleep(0.1) # Fast flash

async def main():
    # Setup WiFi first
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
    wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
    
    while not wlan.isconnected():
        await asyncio.sleep(1)
        
    print("[SYS] WiFi Linked. Starting Tasks...")
    asyncio.create_task(sync_task())
    asyncio.create_task(heartbeat_task())
    
    while True:
        await asyncio.sleep(1) # Keep main loop alive

# Launch the engine
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    print("System Stopped")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking in Async**: Using `time.sleep()` instead of `await asyncio.sleep()`. This will stop EVERY task on the Pico until the timer finishes.
*   **Missing Task creation**: Forgetting to call `asyncio.create_task()`. The function will exist but never actually start running.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Web Dashboard**: Add a third async task that runs a tiny web server. You can see the current time in your browser without stopping the LED blink!

## 1. Project 1513: Object-Oriented Professional NTP Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Encapsulate complex network time logic into a reusable "TimeManager" class, improving code portability and organization for large-scale IoT applications.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Encapsulation**: Grouping related variables (host, offset) and functions (sync, format) into a single object "package."
*   **Class Properties**: Defining the state of an object (e.g., `self.timezone`).
*   **Abstraction**: Hiding the "dirty" networking code behind clean, easy-to-use methods like `.sync()`.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `new_class`** (Define the manager)
*   **from Logic, drag `class_method`** (Add sync behavior)
*   **from Network, drag `init_ntp`** (The encapsulated logic)
*   **from System, drag `get_rtc_time`** (Read hardware clock)

### 7. Variables
*   **my_clock**: Object (An instance of the TimeManager class).
*   **current_str**: String (The formatted date/time).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Class Definition Phase**
1.  **Initialize the Object**:
    *   Create a class named `TimeManager`.
    *   In the `__init__` constructor, setup the `host` (pool.ntp.org) and internal `rtc` reference.
2.  **Define Sync Logic**:
    *   Create a method named `sync()`.
    *   **Snap** the binary UDP packet logic from Project 1511 inside this method.
    *   Apply a `delta` of 2,208,988,800.

**B. Data Formatting Phase**
3.  **Humanize the Output**:
    *   Create a method named `get_time_string()`.
    *   From **System**, get the current 8-tuple from the RTC.
    *   **Snap** together a string in the format "YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS".

**C. Integration Phase**
4.  **Instantiate & Use**:
    *   Create a new variable `my_clock` as a new `TimeManager()`.
    *   Connect to WiFi.
    *   **Snap** the `my_clock.sync()` call.
    *   In a **Loops** `forever` block, print `my_clock.get_time_string()`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico code initializes a "black box" object that knows how to handle time but hasn't done anything yet.
2.  **Process**: The main program tells the object: "Sync yourself."
3.  **Action**: The object opens its own internal socket, does the math, and silently sets the Pico's hardware clock.
4.  **Result**: The main program stays clean and readable.
5.  **Output**: "Current Time: 2025-05-12 09:30:15" scrolls in the console.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import machine

class TimeManager:
    """A professional wrapper for the Pico W RTC and NTP sync."""
    
    def __init__(self, host="pool.ntp.org"):
        self.host = host
        self.rtc = machine.RTC()
        self.delta = 2208988800 # Seconds between 1900 and 1970
        
    def _fetch_raw(self):
        # Private method for UDP transaction
        s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
        s.settimeout(2.0)
        try:
            packet = bytearray(48)
            packet[0] = 0x1B
            addr = socket.getaddrinfo(self.host, 123)[0][-1]
            s.sendto(packet, addr)
            msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
            return struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0] - self.delta
        except:
            return None
        finally:
            s.close()
            
    def sync(self):
        """Updates the Hardware RTC using NTP"""
        print(f"[OBJ] Syncing with {self.host}...")
        t = self._fetch_raw()
        if t:
            tm = time.localtime(t)
            # (year, month, day, weekday, hours, minutes, seconds, subseconds)
            self.rtc.datetime((tm[0], tm[1], tm[2], tm[6] + 1, tm[3], tm[4], tm[5], 0))
            return True
        return False
        
    def get_time_string(self):
        """Returns standard YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM:SS format"""
        t = time.localtime()
        return "{:04d}-{:02d}-{:02d} {:02d}:{:02d}:{:02d}".format(
            t[0], t[1], t[2], t[3], t[4], t[5]
        )

# --- Application ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("MySSID", "MyPass")

while not wlan.isconnected():
    time.sleep(1)

# Create the object
clock = TimeManager()

# Sync once
if clock.sync():
    print("Initial sync complete!")

# Main loop is very clean
while True:
    print(f"Time: {clock.get_time_string()}")
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Variable Scope**: Forgetting to use `self.` before variables inside classes. For example, `rtc.datetime(...)` will fail, you must use `self.rtc.datetime(...)`.
*   **Object creation**: Writing the class but forgetting to create the instance (`clock = TimeManager()`) before trying to use it.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Drift Monitor**: Extend the class to store the "last successful sync" timestamp and check how many seconds the internal clock drifts every day.

## 1. Project 1514: Robust Professional NTP Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
Implement a "Self-Auditing" time controller that measures and logs Clock Drift (the error in local time) before applying corrections, ensuring high-integrity scheduling.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Clock Drift**: The slow loss of accuracy in a hardware clock over time due to temperature or crystal quality.
*   **Drift Analysis**: Calculating the difference between the "Trusted" (NTP) time and "Local" (RTC) time to assess device health.
*   **Threshold Monitoring**: Setting limits (e.g., 5 seconds) to trigger warnings or emergency resyncs.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_then_else`** (Evaluate drift size)
*   **from System, drag `get_rtc_seconds`** (Capture current time)
*   **from Network, drag `init_ntp_sync`** (Get internet time)
*   **from Math, drag `absolute_value`** (Compare difference)

### 7. Variables
*   **local_ts**: Integer (The seconds currently on the Pico clock).
*   **ntp_ts**: Integer (The seconds received from the server).
*   **drift**: Float (The calculated error in seconds).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Setup Environment**:
    *   Initialize WiFi as per Project 1501.
    *   Create a variable `drift_limit` and set it to `5.0`.

**B. Auditing Phase**
2.  **Capture Baseline**:
    *   From **System**, drag `get_rtc_seconds` and store it in `local_ts`.
3.  **Fetch Truth**:
    *   Call the sync logic from Project 1511 to get `ntp_ts`.
4.  **Calculate Error**:
    *   Create a variable `drift` and set it to `ntp_ts - local_ts`.
    *   Print "Current Drift Detected: [drift]s".

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Evaluate Health**:
    *   From **Logic**, drag `if_then`.
    *   Condition: `abs(drift) > drift_limit`.
6.  **Apply Fix**:
    *   If the condition is met, update the RTC to match `ntp_ts`.
    *   If the drift is small, log "System within tolerance."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico runs for a long period (e.g., 24 hours).
2.  **Process**: It checks its internal clock against a global server.
3.  **Action**: It calculates exactly how many milliseconds its hardware crystal has slipped.
4.  **Result**: If the slip is significant, it resets its clock to stay in perfect sync.
5.  **Output**: "Clock Drift: 0.145s (Normal Operation)" appears in the console.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import machine

class RobustTimeController:
    def __init__(self):
        self.rtc = machine.RTC()
        self.delta = 2208988800
        
    def _get_ntp(self):
        s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
        s.settimeout(2.0)
        try:
            packet = bytearray(48); packet[0] = 0x1B
            addr = socket.getaddrinfo("pool.ntp.org", 123)[0][-1]
            s.sendto(packet, addr)
            msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
            return struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0] - self.delta
        except:
            return None
        finally:
            s.close()

    def audit_and_sync(self):
        print("[AUDIT] Evaluating clock integrity...")
        
        # 1. Fetch Source of Truth
        ntp_ts = self._get_ntp()
        if ntp_ts is None:
            print("[FAIL] Cannot audit: Network offline")
            return
            
        # 2. Capture local state
        local_ts = time.time()
        
        # 3. Analyze
        drift = ntp_ts - local_ts
        print(f"[STAT] Calculated Drift: {drift:.3f} seconds")
        
        if abs(drift) > 1000000: # Usually means 1970 vs 2025
            print("[INFO] Cold boot detected. First sync.")
        elif abs(drift) > 5:
            print("[WARN] Significant time skew! Correcting...")
        else:
            print("[INFO] Tolerance OK (<5s).")
            
        # 4. Correct
        tm = time.localtime(ntp_ts)
        self.rtc.datetime((tm[0], tm[1], tm[2], tm[6] + 1, tm[3], tm[4], tm[5], 0))
        print("[SYS] RTC successfully re-aligned.")

# --- Execution ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)

mgr = RobustTimeController()
mgr.audit_and_sync()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Jitter Misinterpretation**: On slow networks, the "Drift" might look like 0.5s just because the packet took time to travel. This is network noise, not hardware drift (See Project 1516 for the fix).
*   **Missing absolute value**: Forgetting to use `abs()`. If the clock is *ahead*, the drift is negative. A `drift > 5` check would miss a clock that is 10 seconds ahead!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Health Ledger**: Store the drift values in a list and calculate the "Aging Rate" of your Pico's crystal.

---

**11. Common Mistakes:**  
*   **Network Latency as Drift**: If your Ping to the NTP server varies by 50ms, your calculated "Drift" will fluctuate by 50ms. This is "Jitter", not true clock drift.  
*   **Blocking**: Remember this sync is still blocking for 100-500ms. In a robust system, this audit happens in the background (Project 1512).  

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Project 1515**: Handle the case where "pool.ntp.org" is down by implementing a Server Pool (error handling).

## 1. Project 1515: Professional NTP Time Sync with Error Handling

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate single points of failure by implementing a "Server Pool" failover mechanism that automatically switches to secondary time servers if the primary is offline.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Redundancy**: Keeping multiple backup options (Google, NIST, Microsoft) ready to use.
*   **Retry Logic**: Systematically attempting a failed operation multiple times with different parameters.
*   **Iterative Failover**: A standard algorithm that loops through a list until it finds a working resource.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Variables, drag `create_list`** (The server pool)
*   **from Loops, drag `for_each_item`** (Iterate servers)
*   **from Logic, drag `try_except`** (Catch network failures)
*   **from Loops, drag `break`** (Stop on success)

### 7. Variables
*   **SERVER_POOL**: List of Strings (Addresses of 4 global servers).
*   **success**: Boolean (Flags if any server responded).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define the Network**:
    *   Create a variable `SERVER_POOL`.
    *   **Snap** a list containing `"pool.ntp.org"`, `"time.google.com"`, and `"time.nist.gov"`.

**B. Failover Phase**
2.  **Start the Search**:
    *   From **Loops**, drag `for each server in SERVER_POOL`.
3.  **Attempt Connection**:
    *   Inside the loop, attempt the UDP sync from Project 1511 using the current `server` variable.
    *   Wrap this in a `try...except` block.
4.  **Handle Success**:
    *   If the server replies, **snap** a `break` block to stop the loop immediately.
5.  **Handle Failure**:
    *   If it fails (timeout/offline), the loop continues to the next server in the list.

**C. Verification Phase**
6.  **Final Status**:
    *   After the loop, check if time was actually set.
    *   If not, print a "System Error: Total Connectivity Loss."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico wants to update its clock.
2.  **Process**: It tries Server 1. Server 1 is busy and doesn't answer.
3.  **Action**: Pico immediately switches to Server 2 (Google).
4.  **Result**: Server 2 responds in 50ms. The Pico stops searching and updates its clock.
5.  **Output**: "Server 1 timed out. Successfully synced with time.google.com."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import machine

class FailoverTimeManager:
    def __init__(self):
        # A pool of world-class time servers
        self.pool = [
            "pool.ntp.org",
            "time.google.com",
            "time.nist.gov",
            "time.windows.com"
        ]
        self.rtc = machine.RTC()
        self.delta = 2208988800

    def _sync_with_server(self, host):
        """Attempts a single transaction with a standard 1s timeout."""
        print(f"[RETRY] Trying {host}...")
        s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
        s.settimeout(1.0)
        try:
            packet = bytearray(48); packet[0] = 0x1B
            addr = socket.getaddrinfo(host, 123)[0][-1]
            s.sendto(packet, addr)
            msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
            return struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0] - self.delta
        except:
            return None
        finally:
            s.close()
            
    def sync_failover(self):
        print("[SYS] Initializing redundant sync procedure...")
        for server in self.pool:
            t = self._sync_with_server(server)
            if t:
                tm = time.localtime(t)
                self.rtc.datetime((tm[0], tm[1], tm[2], tm[6] + 1, tm[3], tm[4], tm[5], 0))
                print(f"[SUCCESS] Clock set via {server}")
                return True
            print(f"[FAIL] {server} unreachable.")
            
        print("[CRITICAL] All NTP pool servers exhausted!")
        return False

# --- Operation ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)

mgr = FailoverTimeManager()
mgr.sync_failover()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **DNS Latency**: If your internet is completely out, checking 4 servers will take a long time because each "address lookup" (DNS) will time out.
*   **Forgetting `break`**: If you don't `return` or `break` on success, the Pico will keep trying the other 3 servers even if it already has the time!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Priority Sort**: Modify the list to move the most recent "successful" server to the top of the list for the next sync.

---

**11. Common Mistakes:**  
*   **Infinite Loops**: If you put the retry logic inside a `while True`, and your internet is down, the code will hang forever trying servers. Always have an exit condition (iterating the list once).  
*   **DNS Issues**: If DNS is down, `getaddrinfo` might block for a long time (20s+). This code catches "Exception", which includes DNS errors, properly moving to the next (though if DNS is down, all domain names will likely fail).  

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Project 1516**: Improve accuracy by calculating the Round Trip Delay (latency) and adding half of it to the result.  

---

## 1. Project 1516: Optimized Professional NTP Time Sync Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize time precision by calculating and compensating for Network Latency using the Round-Trip Time (RTT) algorithm.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Latency Correction**: Adjusting a measurement based on the time it took to travel.
*   **Network Symmetry**: The assumption that the "upload" trip to the server takes the same amount of time as the "download" trip back.
*   **High-Resolution Timing**: Using millisecond-accurate counters (`time.ticks_ms`) instead of whole seconds.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `get_ticks_ms`** (Start timer)
*   **from Time, drag `ticks_diff`** (Calculate RTT)
*   **from Math, drag `division`** (Halve the latency)
*   **from Math, drag `addition`** (Apply correction)

### 7. Variables
*   **t1 / t2**: Integer (The millisecond "stamp" before and after the request).
*   **latency_sec**: Float (The calculated travel time).
*   **precise_time**: Float (Server time + Latency).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Establish Reference**:
    *   Connect to WiFi.
    *   Initialize external server address (`pool.ntp.org`).

**B. Timing Phase**
2.  **Start the Stopwatch**:
    *   Immediately before sending the UDP packet, **snap** `time.ticks_ms()` into `t1`.
3.  **Fire and Receive**:
    *   Send the NTP request.
    *   Wait for the server's reply.
4.  **Stop the Stopwatch**:
    *   Immediately after the reply is received, **snap** `time.ticks_ms()` into `t2`.

**C. Compensation Phase**
5.  **Calculate Offset**:
    *   `rtt = t2 - t1`.
    *   `latency_sec = (rtt / 2) / 1000`.
6.  **Apply Update**:
    *   Extract the `server_time` from the packet as usual.
    *   `final_time = server_time + latency_sec`.
    *   Update the RTC with this boosted precision.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W prepare to sync.
2.  **Process**: It notes the exact millisecond it sends the request.
3.  **Action**: It notes the exact millisecond it receives the reply (e.g., 200ms later).
4.  **Result**: It realizes the "Time" inside the packet is now 100ms old (half the round trip).
5.  **Output**: "Network Delay: 200ms. Applying +0.1s correction."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import machine

class PrecisionNTP:
    def __init__(self):
        self.rtc = machine.RTC()
        self.delta = 2208988800
        
    def sync_ultra_precise(self):
        s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
        s.settimeout(1.0)
        
        # Pre-resolve to avoid measuring DNS time
        addr = socket.getaddrinfo("pool.ntp.org", 123)[0][-1]
        packet = bytearray(48); packet[0] = 0x1B
        
        try:
            # --- MEASUREMENT ZONE ---
            t1 = time.ticks_ms()
            s.sendto(packet, addr)
            msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
            t2 = time.ticks_ms()
            # ------------------------
            
            # 1. Server time
            raw_val = struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0]
            server_ts = raw_val - self.delta
            
            # 2. Latency Logic
            rtt = time.ticks_diff(t2, t1)
            one_way_latency = (rtt / 2) / 1000 # Convert ms to s
            
            # 3. Final Correction
            precise_ts = server_ts + one_way_latency
            
            # Apply to RTC
            tm = time.localtime(int(precise_ts))
            self.rtc.datetime((tm[0], tm[1], tm[2], tm[6] + 1, tm[3], tm[4], tm[5], 0))
            
            print(f"[RTT] Trip: {rtt}ms. Compensation: +{one_way_latency:.3f}s")
            return True
            
        except:
            return False
        finally:
            s.close()

# --- App ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)

mgr = PrecisionNTP()
mgr.sync_ultra_precise()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **DNS Contamination**: Measuring `getaddrinfo` inside `t1` and `t2`. DNS can take 50ms-2000ms and is NOT part of the time travel trip. Always resolve the domain name *before* starting the timer.
*   **Integer Rounding**: In some languages, `150 / 2` might work, but `/ 1000` requires float math to keep the sub-second detail.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Latency Alert**: If the RTT is greater than 1000ms, blink a red LED to warn that the internet connection is unstable.

---

**11. Common Mistakes:**  
*   **Including DNS in T1-T2**: Do NOT put `getaddrinfo` between `t1` and `t2`. That adds DNS lookup time (which is not part of the packet travel time to the NTP server) to your latency calc. Resolve address *first*, then measure the socket Send/Recv.  
*   **Integer Math**: Remember `rtt / 2` is integer division in some languages, but float in Python 3. `offset_sec` needs to be float.  

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Project 1517**: Create an Audit Log that saves these RTT values to a file `sync_log.txt` so you can visualize your internet quality over 24 hours.
