## 1. Project 1517: Professional NTP Time Sync Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Implement a persistent audit log that records synchronization events, latency, and system health to the Pico W's flash memory for long-term diagnostics.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Persistent Logging**: Writing data to a file that remains accessible even after the power is cut.
*   **File Append (a)**: A specific file mode that adds new data to the end of a file without deleting the existing content.
*   **Telemetry Pipeline**: The process of capturing, formatting, and storing operational data for later analysis.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Onboard Flash Memory |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Storage, drag `append_to_file`** (Save log entry)
*   **from System, drag `get_rtc_time`** (Stamp the event)
*   **from Text, drag `create_string_with`** (Format the log line)
*   **from Storage, drag `read_file`** (Retrieve logs)

### 7. Variables
*   **log_line**: String (The text to be saved).
*   **sync_status**: String ("OK" or "FAIL").

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Prepare Storage**:
    *   From **Storage**, define a variable `filename` and set it to `"sync_log.txt"`.
2.  **Connect WiFi**:
    *   Initialize network as per Project 1501.

**B. Data Capture Phase**
3.  **Execute Sync**:
    *   Perform NTP synchronization using Project 1511 or 1516 logic.
4.  **Format the Record**:
    *   From **System**, get the current 8-tuple time.
    *   Create a string: `"[Date] Sync [Status] (Delay: [ms]ms)"`.
    *   Assign this to `log_line`.

**C. Storage Phase**
5.  **Commit to Flash**:
    *   From **Storage**, drag `append_to_file`.
    *   Target: `filename`.
    *   Content: `log_line`.
6.  **Inspection**:
    *   Create a separate block or script that uses `read_file` to print the entire log to the console for a technician to review.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico successfully gets the time from a server.
2.  **Process**: It builds a professional-looking message containing the date and the network delay.
3.  **Action**: It writes this message into a hidden file on its internal chip.
4.  **Result**: If the power goes out, the log remains safe.
5.  **Output**: "Logging: [2025-01-20 08:00:00] Sync OK (42ms)" appears briefly in the console.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import machine
import os

class AuditedNTP:
    def __init__(self, log_file="sync_log.txt"):
        self.rtc = machine.RTC()
        self.filename = log_file
        self.delta = 2208988800

    def _log(self, message):
        """Standard append-to-file logic."""
        t = time.localtime()
        ts = "{:04d}-{:02d}-{:02d} {:02d}:{:02d}".format(t[0],t[1],t[2],t[3],t[4])
        entry = f"[{ts}] {message}\n"
        
        try:
            with open(self.filename, "a") as f:
                f.write(entry)
            print(f"Archived: {message}")
        except OSError:
            print("Disk Error: Failed to write log.")

    def sync_and_archive(self):
        s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
        s.settimeout(2.0)
        try:
            # 1. Measurement
            t1 = time.ticks_ms()
            addr = socket.getaddrinfo("pool.ntp.org", 123)[0][-1]
            s.sendto(bytearray(48), addr) # Simplified 
            msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
            t2 = time.ticks_ms()
            
            # 2. Calculation
            raw = struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0] - self.delta
            rtt = time.ticks_diff(t2, t1)
            
            # 3. Apply
            tm = time.localtime(raw)
            self.rtc.datetime((tm[0], tm[1], tm[2], tm[6] + 1, tm[3], tm[4], tm[5], 0))
            
            # 4. Log
            self._log(f"SYNC_OK | Server: pool.ntp.org | RTT: {rtt}ms")
            
        except Exception as e:
            self._log(f"SYNC_FAIL | Error: {str(e)}")
        finally:
            s.close()

    def show_history(self):
        print("\n--- Pico Sync History ---")
        try:
            with open(self.filename, "r") as f:
                print(f.read())
        except:
            print("Log is empty.")

# --- Operation ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)

agent = AuditedNTP()
agent.sync_and_archive()
agent.show_history()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Memory Overflow**: If you log every second, the file will eventually fill up the internal memory. Professional systems limit logs to hourly events or check file size before writing.
*   **Overwriting Logs**: Using `"w"` (Write) instead of `"a"` (Append). `"w"` deletes all previous records, making the "history" useless.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Cloud Pipeline**: Use MQTT to send these log entries to a central server instead of just saving them locally.

## 1. Project 1518: Secure Professional NTP Time Sync Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Protect the system from "Time Travel" attacks and server spoofing by implementing rigorous input validation and sanity checking on all external time data.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sanity Check**: Evaluating if a value is physically possible (e.g., a current year of 1970 is impossible).
*   **Input Validation**: A security layer that rejects data that does not meet pre-defined criteria.
*   **System Integrity**: Ensuring that a single bad piece of external data cannot "break" the device's internal logic.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_then`** (Check range)
*   **from Logic, drag `greater_than`** (Upper bound)
*   **from Logic, drag `less_than`** (Lower bound)
*   **from System, drag `init_ntp_sync`** (Encapsulated)

### 7. Variables
*   **MIN_YEAR**: Integer (The earliest acceptable year, e.g., 2024).
*   **MAX_YEAR**: Integer (The latest acceptable year, e.g., 2040).
*   **candidate_year**: Integer (The year extracted from the packet).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Security Guards**:
    *   Create variables `MIN_YEAR` (2024) and `MAX_YEAR` (2040).
2.  **Connect WiFi**:
    *   Follow Project 1501.

**B. Validation Phase**
3.  **Receive Candidate**:
    *   Fetch the raw time from a server as per Project 1511.
4.  **Extract the Year**:
    *   From **System**, convert the raw seconds to a date tuple.
    *   Extract index `0` (the year).
5.  **Run Guards**:
    *   **Snap** an `if...then` block.
    *   If `year < MIN_YEAR` or `year > MAX_YEAR`:
        *   Discard the data.
        *   Print "ALERT: Malicious or Invalid time detected!"

**C. Application Phase**
6.  **Update Only on Success**:
    *   Only if the year passes both checks, apply it to the hardware RTC.
    *   Otherwise, keep the existing (even if slightly drifted) internal time.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A dummy server tries to trick the Pico by sending year "1900".
2.  **Process**: The Pico receives the packet and decodes the year.
3.  **Action**: Its "Security Guard" sees that 1900 is less than 2024.
4.  **Result**: The Pico ignores the packet and logs a security event.
5.  **Output**: "Security Filter: Rejected Bogus Time (1900)."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import machine

class SecureTimeGuard:
    def __init__(self):
        self.rtc = machine.RTC()
        self.delta = 2208988800
        # Hardened boundaries (Update these every decade!)
        self.MIN_SAFE_YEAR = 2024
        self.MAX_SAFE_YEAR = 2040

    def sync_safe(self):
        print("[SECURE] Querying internet for time...")
        s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
        s.settimeout(2.0)
        
        try:
            packet = bytearray(48)
            packet[0] = 0x1B
            addr = socket.getaddrinfo("pool.ntp.org", 123)[0][-1]
            s.sendto(packet, addr)
            msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
            
            # 1. Decode
            val = struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0] - self.delta
            tm = time.localtime(val)
            year = tm[0]
            
            # 2. VALIDATE
            if year < self.MIN_SAFE_YEAR or year > self.MAX_SAFE_YEAR:
                print(f"[REJECT] Bogus Year Detected: {year}. Ignore.")
                return False
                
            # 3. APPLY
            self.rtc.datetime((tm[0], tm[1], tm[2], tm[6] + 1, tm[3], tm[4], tm[5], 0))
            print(f"[ACCEPT] Time validated: {year}-{tm[1]:02d}-{tm[2]:02d}")
            return True
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[FAIL] Connection error: {e}")
            return False
        finally:
            s.close()

# --- Application ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)

guard = SecureTimeGuard()
guard.sync_safe()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Window Too Small**: Setting `MAX_YEAR` to 2025 will cause your device to "stop working" on Jan 1, 2026. Always give your device a "future buffer" of at least 15 years.
*   **Boot Conflict**: Remember that on cold boot, the Pico *thinks* it is 2021. You cannot compare the server time to the *current* Pico time until you have at least one valid sync.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Consensus Check**: Query two different servers (Google and NTP.org). If they disagree by more than 1 second, reject both!

## 1. Project 1519: Multi-Threaded Professional NTP Time Sync

### 2. Learning Objective
Become a network time authority by building an NTP Server that runs on Core 1, answering time queries from multiple devices simultaneously without slowing down the main application on Core 0.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Server Architecture**: Creating a "Listener" that waits for others to ask for data.
*   **True Parallelism**: Using Core 1 (Pico's second processor) to handle "noisy" networking while the main code stays "clean."
*   **Port Binding**: Specifically claiming Port 123 so the chip knows to send all time-sync traffic to your code.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439 AP/STA |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `run_on_core1`** (Parallel processing)
*   **from Network, drag `init_udp_server`** (Listen on port 123)
*   **from Text, drag `pack_binary`** (Build response)
*   **from Network, drag `udp_reply`** (Send back to client)

### 7. Variables
*   **s_server**: Object (The listening UDP socket).
*   **client_addr**: Address (The IP of the device asking for time).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Join the Network**:
    *   Connect to WiFi.
2.  **Sync Local Time**:
    *   Perform a one-time sync with `pool.ntp.org` so your Pico is accurate.

**B. Server Phase (Core 1)**
3.  **Start Listener**:
    *   Create a UDP socket and `bind` it to Port 123 on all interfaces (`0.0.0.0`).
4.  **Wait for Clients**:
    *   Inside a recursive `while True` loop, call `recvfrom(48)`.
5.  **Craft Response**:
    *   Get local time. Add the `NTP_DELTA`.
    *   **Snap** a 48-byte response together.
    *   Set the first byte to `0x1C` (Server mode).
    *   Insert your current time into the "Transmit Timestamp" field.

**C. Launch Phase**
6.  **Activate Core 1**:
    *   From **System**, drag `run_on_core1`.
    *   Attach your server function inside.
7.  **Main Application**:
    *   Run any other task (e.g., Robot control, sensor logging) on the main loop.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W becomes a localized "Time Beacon."
2.  **Process**: An Arduino on the same network sends a request to the Pico.
3.  **Action**: Core 1 immediately replies with the Pico's current time.
4.  **Result**: Core 0 (doing the real work) never even knows a request happened.
5.  **Output**: "NTP Query from 192.168.1.55 answered" appears in the log.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import _thread

NTP_DELTA = 2208988800

def time_authority_server():
    print("[CORE 1] NTP Server Engine Starting...")
    s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
    
    try:
        s.bind(('0.0.0.0', 123))
        print("[CORE 1] Bound to Port 123. Ready for clients.")
    except:
        print("[CORE 1] ERROR: Port 123 Busy. Is another app running?")
        return

    while True:
        try:
            # 1. Listen for 48-byte request
            data, addr = s.recvfrom(48)
            
            # 2. Build Response (48 bytes)
            # Mode 4 (Server), Ver 3 -> 0x1C
            resp = bytearray(48)
            resp[0] = 0x1C
            
            # 3. Insert current system time
            # High quality servers use 8-bytes, we use 4-byte seconds for simplicity
            t = int(time.time() + NTP_DELTA)
            struct.pack_into("!I", resp, 40, t)
            
            # 4. Reply
            s.sendto(resp, addr)
            print(f"[CORE 1] Served {addr}")
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[CORE 1] Server Loop Error: {e}")

# --- Core 0 App ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)

# Launch Background Thread (Core 1)
_thread.start_new_thread(time_authority_server, ())

# Core 0 stays clean
while True:
    print("[CORE 0] Performing industrial automation...")
    time.sleep(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Port Collisions**: If you try to run two NTP servers on one Pico, the second one will crash because Port 123 is already claimed.
*   **Thread Safety**: Be careful when sharing variables between Core 0 and Core 1. It is safest to let the server run entirely on its own data.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Mesh Network**: Deploy 5 Picos; have one sync to the internet and the other four sync to the first one!

## 1. Project 1520: Industrial Professional NTP Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
Deploy a mission-critical time synchronization system that handles global deployments by implementing dynamic Timezone Offsets and Daylight Savings Time (DST) logic.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **UTC (Coordinated Universal Time)**: The primary time standard by which the world regulates clocks and time.
*   **Timezone Offset**: The difference in hours and minutes from UTC for a specific location.
*   **Daylight Savings Logic**: An automated rule that shifts the clock by 1 hour during summer months to extend evening daylight.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Variables, drag `create_number`** (The offset value)
*   **from Math, drag `multiplication`** (Hours to seconds)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_then`** (Check DST month)
*   **from System, drag `set_rtc_time`** (Apply final time)

### 7. Variables
*   **TZ_OFFSET**: Integer (e.g., -5 for New York).
*   **dst_active**: Boolean (True if currently in summer).
*   **utc_seconds**: Integer (Raw time from NTP).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Location**:
    *   Create a variable `TZ_OFFSET` and set it to your local offset (e.g., `+5.5` for IST or `-8` for PST).
2.  **Connect WiFi**:
    *   Initialize as per Project 1501.

**B. Calculation Phase**
3.  **Fetch UTC**:
    *   Perform an NTP sync to get the raw `utc_seconds`.
4.  **Apply Offset**:
    *   Multiply `TZ_OFFSET` by `3600` (seconds in an hour).
    *   Add this value to `utc_seconds` to get `local_seconds`.
5.  **DST Audit**:
    *   Convert `local_seconds` to a date tuple.
    *   Check if the month is between March and November (Standard US/EU DST rule).
    *   If yes, add another `3600` seconds.

**C. Application Phase**
6.  **Update Hardware**:
    *   Convert the final `adjusted_seconds` back to a tuple.
    *   Apply to the `machine.RTC()`.
7.  **Maintenance**:
    *   Rerun this entire sync every 12 hours to stay accurate against network drift.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico gets the global UTC time (e.g., 10:00 AM).
2.  **Process**: It calculates its distance from London (e.g., -5 hours for NY).
3.  **Action**: It checks the date and realizes it is July, so it adds +1 hour for DST.
4.  **Result**: The clock correctly displays 6:00 AM local time.
5.  **Output**: "Industrial Sync: UTC 10:00 -> Local 06:00 (DST Active)."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import struct
import time
import machine

class IndustrialTimeNode:
    def __init__(self, tz_offset_hours=0):
        self.rtc = machine.RTC()
        self.delta = 2208988800
        self.tz_offset = tz_offset_hours * 3600

    def _is_dst(self, tm):
        """Simple DST approximation (March to October)."""
        # month is index 1 in the time tuple
        month = tm[1]
        if 3 < month < 11:
            return True
        return False

    def sync_industrial(self):
        print(f"[BOOT] Syncing with offset {self.tz_offset/3600} hours...")
        s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_DGRAM)
        s.settimeout(3.0)
        
        try:
            addr = socket.getaddrinfo("pool.ntp.org", 123)[0][-1]
            s.sendto(bytearray(48), addr)
            msg, _ = s.recvfrom(48)
            
            # 1. Base UTC
            utc_ts = struct.unpack("!I", msg[40:44])[0] - self.delta
            
            # 2. Timezone Correction
            local_ts = utc_ts + self.tz_offset
            
            # 3. DST Correction
            temp_tm = time.localtime(int(local_ts))
            if self._is_dst(temp_tm):
                print("[INFO] Daylight Savings Active: Adding 1 hour.")
                local_ts += 3600
                
            # 4. Final Application
            tm = time.localtime(int(local_ts))
            self.rtc.datetime((tm[0], tm[1], tm[2], tm[6] + 1, tm[3], tm[4], tm[5], 0))
            
            print(f"[ONLINE] System Clock set to: {tm[3]:02d}:{tm[4]:02d}")
            return True
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERROR] Industrial Sync Failed: {e}")
            return False
        finally:
            s.close()

# --- Startup ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)

# Initialize for New York (-5 Offset)
node = IndustrialTimeNode(tz_offset_hours=-5)
node.sync_industrial()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Integer math with floats**: If your timezone is `+5.5` (India), you must use float math. `5.5 * 3600 = 19800`. If you use integer `5`, you will be 30 minutes off!
*   **DST Precision**: Real DST rules change on specific Sundays of the month (e.g., 2nd Sunday of March). This code uses a "Month only" approximation for simplicity. For high-precision medical or legal apps, use a full DST library.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Auto-Location**: Use a Geo-IP API (Project 1505 concept) to automatically detect your timezone offset based on your internet connection!

## Batch 153: Professional HTTP Server (1521-1530)

## 1. Project 1521: Professional HTTP Server Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Understand the fundamental mechanics of the World Wide Web by building a raw **HTTP Server** from scratch using TCP sockets, establishing the foundation for all modern websites.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **TCP (Transmission Control Protocol)**: A reliable binary communication protocol that ensures data arrives in the correct order without loss.
*   **Request/Response Cycle**: The standard conversation between a browser (Client) and the Pico (Server).
*   **Port 80**: The universal doorway for unencrypted web traffic.
*   **Binary Formatting**: Using `\r\n` (CRLF) to separate technical headers from the visual HTML content.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/TCP Stack |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_tcp_server`** (Setup Port 80)
*   **from Network, drag `accept_connection`** (Wait for visitor)
*   **from Text, drag `parse_text`** (Read request)
*   **from Network, drag `send_binary`** (Transmit HTML)

### 7. Variables
*   **s_server**: Object (The master socket listening for users).
*   **conn**: Object (The temporary connection to a specific visitor).
*   **visitor_ip**: String (The address of the phone or computer).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Prepare the Radio**:
    *   Initialize WiFi using the Project 1501 template.
2.  **Open the Gate**:
    *   From **Network**, create a socket `s_server` using `AF_INET` and `SOCK_STREAM`.
    *   **Snap** a `bind` block to attach the socket to Port `80`.
    *   **Snap** a `listen` block to allow up to `5` visitors to wait in line.

**B. Event Handling Phase**
3.  **Wait for Guests**:
    *   Inside a recursive `while True` loop, call `s_server.accept()`.
    *   Store the result in `conn` and `visitor_ip`.
4.  **Read the Intent**:
    *   Call `conn.recv(1024)` to see what the browser wants (e.g., "Give me index.html").
5.  **Craft the Response**:
    *   **Snap** a series of `send` blocks.
    *   **First**: Send the status: `"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n"`.
    *   **Second**: Send formatting: `"Content-Type: text/html\r\n"`.
    *   **Third**: Send the mandatory separator: `"\r\n"`.
    *   **Fourth**: Send the visual content (HTML).

**C. Cleanup Phase**
6.  **End the Session**:
    *   From **Network**, call `conn.close()`. This "hangs up" so the browser knows the page is finished.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W prints its IP address to the console.
2.  **Process**: It "blocks" (pauses), waiting for a network event on Port 80.
3.  **Action**: You type the Pico's IP into Chrome on your phone.
4.  **Result**: The Pico detects the connection, sends the raw text of a website, and disconnects.
5.  **Output**: A professional "Hello from Pico" page appears on your phone screen.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import time

def run_driver_server():
    # 1. Network Prep
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
    while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)
    print(f"[ONLINE] Visit: http://{wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")

    # 2. Socket Driver Setup
    s = socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM)
    # Fix "Address already in use" error on reboot
    s.setsockopt(socket.SOL_SOCKET, socket.SO_REUSEADDR, 1)
    s.bind(('0.0.0.0', 80))
    s.listen(5)

    while True:
        try:
            # 3. Acceptance (Execution pauses here)
            conn, addr = s.accept()
            print(f"[VISITOR] Incoming from {addr[0]}")
            
            # 4. Read Request Line
            request = conn.recv(1024)
            print(f"[GET] {request.decode('utf-8').splitlines()[0]}")
            
            # 5. Transmit HTTP/1.1 Packet
            conn.send('HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n')
            conn.send('Content-Type: text/html\r\n')
            conn.send('Connection: close\r\n\r\n') # Double newline is CRITICAL
            
            html = """
            <html>
                <body style='background:#121212; color:#00ff00; font-family:sans-serif;'>
                    <h1>Pico Industrial Server</h1>
                    <p>Status: CORE_LINK_ACTIVE</p>
                    <hr>
                    <small>Raw TCP Stream from Port 80</small>
                </body>
            </html>
            """
            conn.send(html)
            
            # 6. Finalize
            conn.close()
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[FAIL] Socket Error: {e}")

# Run
run_driver_server()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Forbidden Newline**: If you forget the `\r\n\r\n` between the header and the HTML, the browser will wait until it times out. It is the "end of file" signal for the header.
*   **Port Blocking**: Port 80 is sometimes blocked by local firewalls. Ensure your testing phone is on the *exact* same WiFi network.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Live Data**: Insert `time.time()` into the HTML string so the website shows the Pico's current uptime every time you refresh.

---

## 1. Project 1522: Asynchronous Professional HTTP Server Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Upgrade our blocking socket server to a multi-tasking architecture using `uasyncio`, allowing the Pico to serve web pages without interrupting background control loops.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Asynchronous I/O**: The ability to "yield" control when the network is slow, letting the CPU work on other things like blinking LEDs or calculating sensor data.
*   **Streams**: Using high-level "Reader" and "Writer" objects instead of low-level individual byte packets.
*   **Event Loop**: the central manager that decides which task gets to run next based on hardware availability.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `init_async_loop`** (Start the manager)
*   **from System, drag `create_async_task`** (Run tasks in parallel)
*   **from Network, drag `start_async_server`** (High-level listener)
*   **from System, drag `async_wait`** (Non-blocking sleep)

### 7. Variables
*   **reader**: Object (The incoming text stream).
*   **writer**: Object (The outgoing text stream).
*   **blink_task**: Pointer (The address of the parallel code).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Background Task**:
    *   Create an `async def` function that toggles an LED and uses `asyncio.sleep(0.5)`. This represents your "Main Control Loop."
2.  **Define Web Handler**:
    *   Create an `async def` function that takes `reader` and `writer`.
    *   Inside, read the request using `await reader.readline()`.

**B. Response Phase**
3.  **Transmit Without Blocking**:
    *   From **Network**, call `writer.write()` to send the HTML.
    *   **CRITICAL**: Use `await writer.drain()` to wait for the data to physically leave the chip without stopping the LED task.
4.  **Terminate Stream**:
    *   Call `writer.close()` and `await writer.wait_closed()`.

**C. Main Execution Phase**
5.  **Orchestrate**:
    *   Inside your `main` function, call `asyncio.start_server`.
    *   **Snap** a `create_task` block for the blinker.
    *   **Snap** `asyncio.get_event_loop().run_forever()`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W establishes a WiFi connection.
2.  **Process**: The LED begins blinking immediately.
3.  **Action**: A visitor loads the web page.
4.  **Result**: The server processes the request "in the cracks" of time between LED blinks.
5.  **Output**: The web page loads instantly, and the LED never misses a beat or stutters.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
import machine
import uasyncio as asyncio

# --- 1. THE WEB HANDLER ---
async def handle_requests(reader, writer):
    print("[SERVER] Client Connected")
    
    # Read first line: GET / HTTP/1.1
    request = await reader.readline()
    print(f"[RECV] {request.decode('utf-8').strip()}")
    
    # Consumes headers (important to clear buffer)
    while True:
        line = await reader.readline()
        if line == b'\r\n' or line == b'\n': break

    # Send response
    response = "HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Type: text/html\r\n\r\n"
    response += "<h1>Async Power</h1><p>Pico W is multitasking!</p>"
    
    writer.write(response.encode('utf-8'))
    await writer.drain() # Non-blocking wait for Wi-Fi hardware
    
    writer.close()
    await writer.wait_closed()
    print("[SERVER] Client Released")

# --- 2. THE BACKGROUND TASK ---
async def cardiac_blinker():
    led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)
    while True:
        led.toggle()
        await asyncio.sleep(0.2) # Yields to the server

# --- 3. THE ORCHESTRATOR ---
async def main():
    # WiFi Join
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
    while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(1)
    
    # Register Server & Start Tasks
    print(f"[READY] Web Server started on {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
    server = await asyncio.start_server(handle_requests, "0.0.0.0", 80)
    
    asyncio.create_task(cardiac_blinker())
    
    # Run indefinitely
    while True:
        await asyncio.sleep(10)

# Boot
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    print("System Shutdown")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking the Loop**: Using `time.sleep(1)` instead of `await asyncio.sleep(1)`. This will freeze everything, including the web server and the LED.
*   **Forgetting `.wait_closed()`**: If you don't await the close, the Pico will eventually run out of "File Handles" and refuse new connections.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Speed Test**: Change the LED sleep to `0.05` (very fast) and see if the web page still loads smoothly.

## 1. Project 1523: Object-Oriented Professional HTTP Server Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate disorganized "spaghetti code" by building a reusable **Router Class** that maps specific URLs to dedicated Python functions, mirroring professional frameworks like Flask.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Software Abstraction**: Hiding complex socket operations inside a simple `app.add_route("/my-page", func)` command.
*   **Routing**: The logic that analyzes a URL (e.g., `/settings`) and points the code to the correct logic handler.
*   **Dictionaries as Logic Tables**: Using a Key-Value pair where the Key is the "URL" and the Value is the "Function" to execute.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `create_dictionary`** (To store URL mappings)
*   **from Variables, drag `get_dictionary_value`** (Find the handler)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_then_else`** (Handle 404 errors)
*   **from Network, drag `init_web_app`** (Encapsulated class)

### 7. Variables
*   **self.routes**: Dictionary (A table of paths and their associated functions).
*   **requested_path**: String (The URL extracted from the browser).
*   **handler**: Function (The specific code block meant for that URL).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Class Definition Phase**
1.  **Initialize the Store**:
    *   Create a class `PicoWeb`.
    *   In `__init__`, create an empty dictionary called `self.routes`.
2.  **Define Registration Logic**:
    *   Create a method `add_route(path, func)`.
    *   Store the function in the dictionary using the path as the index.

**B. Integration Phase**
3.  **Parse the Browser's Wish**:
    *   Inside the server handler, split the incoming text to extract the path (the part after `GET`).
4.  **Consult the Table**:
    *   From **Logic**, check if `requested_path` is in `self.routes`.
5.  **Execute & Respond**:
    *   If found, run the function and send the result.
    *   If not found, send a "404 Not Found" error page.

**C. Application Phase**
6.  **Build the Infrastructure**:
    *   Create a `PicoWeb` object.
    *   **Snap** routes for `/` and `/data`.
    *   Start the server loop.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The server initializes and holds its routing table in memory.
2.  **Process**: You visit `http://IP/data`.
3.  **Action**: The Pico looks at its dictionary, finds the "Data" entry, and runs that specific sensor-reading function.
4.  **Result**: You see a clean JSON dataset on your screen.
5.  **Output**: Visiting a non-existent page like `/hack` shows a "404 Error" immediately.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import time

class PicoWeb:
    def __init__(self):
        self.routes = {}

    def add_route(self, path, handler):
        """Map a URL path to a Python function."""
        self.routes[path] = handler
        print(f"[CORE] Route Registered: {path}")

    async def _handle_client(self, reader, writer):
        try:
            # 1. Parse Request Line (GET /path HTTP/1.1)
            line = await reader.readline()
            if not line: return
            
            # Decode and find the path index
            req_str = line.decode('utf-8')
            parts = req_str.split(" ")
            if len(parts) < 2: return
            
            path = parts[1]
            print(f"[REQ] {path}")

            # 2. Routing Logic
            if path in self.routes:
                content = self.routes[path]() # RUN THE HANDLER
                response = f"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n\r\n{content}"
            else:
                response = "HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found\r\n\r\n<h1>404 Error</h1>"

            # 3. Transmit
            writer.write(response.encode('utf-8'))
            await writer.drain()
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] {e}")
        finally:
            writer.close()
            await writer.wait_closed()

    async def run(self, port=80):
        print("[SYS] Booting Application Server...")
        server = await asyncio.start_server(self._handle_client, "0.0.0.0", port)
        while True: await asyncio.sleep(10)

# --- USER LAND ---

def home_page():
    return "Welcome to the <b>Elite Dashboard</b>."

def sensor_page():
    # Simulate a sensor read
    return "{\"temp\": 24.5, \"unit\": \"C\"}"

# Execution
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")

app = PicoWeb()
app.add_route("/", home_page)
app.add_route("/sensors", sensor_page)

try:
    asyncio.run(app.run())
except:
    pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **String Mismatches**: Path keys are case-sensitive. `/Data` is not the same as `/data`.
*   **Returning Integers**: Web responses must be strings. If your sensor function returns `25`, you must convert it using `str(25)` or the app will crash when trying to "send" the number.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dynamic Links**: Add an HTML link `<a href='/sensors'>View Sensors</a>` to your home page and use it to navigate between pages on your phone.

## 1. Project 1524: Robust Professional HTTP Server Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
Secure your web application against resource exhaustion by anti-DoS mechanisms like timeout enforcement, ensuring the system remains available even under attack.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **DoS Prevention**: Strategies to stop malicious actors from filling up all available connections (sockets) with fake or slow traffic.
*   **Time-to-Live (TTL)**: A countdown timer that automatically kills a connection if it doesn't send data fast enough.
*   **Atomic Operations**: Ensuring a piece of code (like reading a header) finishes completely or fails cleanly without hanging.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `wait_for_task`** (Enforce the deadline)
*   **from Logic, drag `try_except`** (Catch the timeout event)
*   **from Network, drag `force_close`** (Immediate disconnect)
*   **from Text, drag `log_to_console`** (Security event logging)

### 7. Variables
*   **CONNECTION_TIMEOUT**: Float (Maximum seconds to wait for a header, e.g., 2.0).
*   **request_timer**: Object (The monitor tracking the active connection).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Security Policy**:
    *   Initialize a constant `TIMEOUT` and set it to `3.0` seconds.
2.  **Setup WiFi**:
    *   Follow the standard Project 1501 connection logic.

**B. Monitoring Phase**
3.  **Start the Watchdog**:
    *   Inside the server handler, before reading any data, **snap** an `asyncio.wait_for()` block.
4.  **Execute Protected Read**:
    *   Place your `reader.readline()` block inside the protected zone.
5.  **Catch the Attacker**:
    *   **Snap** a `try...except TimeoutError` block.
    *   If the timer hits the limit, immediately execute **C**.

**C. Enforcement Phase**
6.  **Sever Link**:
    *   From **Network**, call `writer.close()`. Do not send an error page (which costs CPU/RAM); just drop the connection to save energy.
7.  **Log Alert**:
    *   Print "[SECURITY] Killed slow connection from [IP]".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The server starts normally.
2.  **Process**: A device connects but sends data extremely slowly (one letter every 10 seconds).
3.  **Action**: The Pico's internal timer reaches 3.0 seconds since the last letter.
4.  **Result**: The server realizes this is an inefficient or malicious user and "hangs up."
5.  **Output**: The Pico remains fast and responsive for other legitimate users.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import time

class RobustServerController:
    def __init__(self, port=80):
        self.port = port
        self.max_wait = 2.5 # Strictly enforce 2.5s per line

    async def _safe_handler(self, reader, writer):
        addr = writer.get_extra_info('peername')
        try:
            # --- SECURITY SHIELD ---
            # If the browser doesn't send the request line in 2.5s, KILL IT
            line = await asyncio.wait_for(reader.readline(), self.max_wait)
            
            if not line: return
            
            print(f"[AUTH] Valid request from {addr[0]}")
            
            # Send standard response
            writer.write(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n\r\nConnection Secured.")
            await writer.drain()

        except asyncio.TimeoutError:
            # Anti-Slowloris logic: No response, just free the socket
            print(f"[BLOCK] Connection from {addr[0]} timed out. Resources freed.")
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Transport error: {e}")
            
        finally:
            writer.close()
            await writer.wait_closed()

    async def start(self):
        wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
        wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
        while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(1)
        
        print(f"[SYS] Secure listener active on port {self.port}")
        await asyncio.start_server(self._safe_handler, "0.0.0.0", self.port)
        while True: await asyncio.sleep(60)

# Main
controller = RobustServerController()
try:
    asyncio.run(controller.start())
except:
    pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Setting Timeout too low**: If you set the timeout to 0.1s, even fast phones on a slightly weak WiFi won't be able to connect. 2-5 seconds is the "Goldilocks Zone" for IoT.
*   **Forgetting to Close in `finally`**: If the connection times out and you don't call `close`, that socket stays "Open" forever, which is exactly what a DoS attack wants to achieve!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Blacklist**: Create a list of IPs that have timed out more than 3 times and ignore their connection requests entirely for 5 minutes.

## 1. Project 1525: Professional HTTP Server with Error Handling

### 2. Learning Objective
Improve user experience and API reliability by implementing professional HTTP Status Codes (404, 500) to communicate system status clearly to clients.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **HTTP Status Codes**: Numerical codes (e.g., 200, 404, 500) that tell a browser if the request succeeded or why it failed.
*   **Graceful Degradation**: The ability of a system to keep running even when a partial failure (like a coding bug in one page) occurs.
*   **Catch-All Logic**: Using a final "else" or "except" block to capture any unplanned user input or software errors.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/RTC |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `try_except`** (Intercept software bugs)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_then_else`** (Handle missing URLs)
*   **from Text, drag `create_text_with`** (Build the error message)
*   **from System, drag `print_to_console`** (Terminal debug)

### 7. Variables
*   **status_code**: String (e.g., "404 Not Found").
*   **error_msg**: String (The technical description of a crash).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Routing Phase**
1.  **Search the Map**:
    *   Initialize your route dictionary as in Project 1523.
2.  **Evaluate Path**:
    *   **Snap** an `if...else` block. Check if the URL exists.
3.  **Handle Missing Data**:
    *   If index is missing, set `status_code` to `"404 Not Found"`.
    *   Send a friendly "Oops! Page Missing" HTML body.

**B. Defensive Programming Phase**
4.  **Contain the Crash**:
    *   Wrap the actual function call in a `try...except` block.
5.  **Translate to 500**:
    *   If the code inside a page handler crashes (e.g., math error), the `except` block fires.
    *   Set `status_code` to `"500 Internal Server Error"`.

**C. Transmission Phase**
6.  **Deliver the News**:
    *   Send the response to the browser.
7.  **Auto-Cleanup**:
    *   Ensure the connection closes even if an error happened.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico is running its web app.
2.  **Process**: A user visits `http://IP/secret-page` which doesn't exist.
3.  **Action**: The Pico sees the name is missing from its dictionary.
4.  **Result**: It sends a professional 404 header.
5.  **Output**: The browser shows "404 Page Not Found" instead of a blank screen or a crash.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import time

class EliteErrorServer:
    def __init__(self):
        self.routes = {
            "/": lambda: "<h1>Success</h1>",
            "/bug": self._buggy_handler # This will crash
        }

    def _buggy_handler(self):
        # Intentional error: dividing by zero
        return str(10/0)

    async def _handle(self, reader, writer):
        try:
            # 1. Read Request
            line = await reader.readline()
            path = line.decode('utf-8').split(" ")[1]
            
            # 2. Routing with Status Handling
            if path in self.routes:
                try:
                    # PRO CASE: We TRY to run the page
                    body = self.routes[path]()
                    header = "HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n\r\n"
                    writer.write((header + body).encode('utf-8'))
                except Exception as e:
                    # SYSTEM CRASH: Page logic failed
                    print(f"[FATAL] Handler failed: {e}")
                    header = "HTTP/1.1 500 Internal Server Error\r\n\r\n"
                    body = f"<h1>500 Server Error</h1><p>Trace: {e}</p>"
                    writer.write((header + body).encode('utf-8'))
            else:
                # 404 CASE: URL is a typo
                print(f"[WARN] 404 for {path}")
                header = "HTTP/1.1 404 Not Found\r\n\r\n"
                body = "<h1>404 Not Found</h1>"
                writer.write((header + body).encode('utf-8'))

            await writer.drain()
            
        except Exception as transport_err:
            print(f"[ERR] Network lost: {transport_err}")
        finally:
            writer.close()
            await writer.wait_closed()

    async def start(self):
        wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
        wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
        while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(1)
        
        print(f"[SYS] Error-Aware Server on {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
        await asyncio.start_server(self._handle, "0.0.0.0", 80)
        while True: await asyncio.sleep(10)

# Run
app = EliteErrorServer()
try:
    asyncio.run(app.start())
except: pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Vague Errors**: Returning a blank page on error. Always send a 404 or 500 header so the browser knows not to keep trying.
*   **Security Leak**: In production, don't show the `{e}` (error details) to the user. Only print them to the Pico's console. Hackers can use error messages to find weaknesses.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Custom 404**: Instead of just text, send a full HTML page with a "Back to Home" button for your 404 errors.

---

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Project 1526**: We are sending strings. What if we want to serve a 5MB image file? `writer.write(5mb_string)` will crash memory. We need **Streaming**.

## 1. Project 1526: Optimized Professional HTTP Server Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Serve large files (images, logs, databases) without crashing the Pico's memory using the "Streaming Chunk" algorithm to keep RAM usage constant.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Streaming**: Transmitting data in small pieces (chunks) instead of loading the entire file into memory at once.
*   **Memory Fragmentation**: A situation where plenty of RAM is free, but it is in small disjointed bits that cannot hold one large file.
*   **Buffer Control**: Manually managing exactly how much data sits in RAM at any given millisecond.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/Flash Storage |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Storage, drag `open_file_for_read`** (Access the data)
*   **from Loops, drag `while_not_end_of_file`** (The chunker)
*   **from Network, drag `stream_bytes`** (Send bit by bit)
*   **from Memory, drag `calculate_free_ram`** (Monitor health)

### 7. Variables
*   **CHUNK_SIZE**: Integer (Bytes to read per pass, e.g., 1024).
*   **chunk**: Binary (The temporary data slice).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Prepare the Content**:
    *   Ensure a large file (e.g., `data.txt`) exists on the Pico's flash memory.
2.  **Define Memory Guard**:
    *   Set `CHUNK_SIZE` to `1024` (1 Kilobyte).

**B. Streaming Phase**
3.  **Open the Source**:
    *   From **Storage**, open the file in `"rb"` (Read Binary) mode.
4.  **Enter the Chunker Loop**:
    *   From **Loops**, start a loop that runs while the file has data.
5.  **Pass the Data**:
    *   Read exactly `CHUNK_SIZE` into a variable `chunk`.
    *   **Snap** a `writer.write(chunk)` block.
    *   **Snap** an `await writer.drain()` block. This is vital; it ensures the chunk is sent before the next one is read from disk.

**C. Cleanup Phase**
6.  **Close Resources**:
    *   Close the file handle to free hardware resources.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W receives a request for a 500KB log file.
2.  **Process**: Instead of loading 500KB into 260KB of RAM (which fails), it loads only 1KB.
3.  **Action**: It sends 1KB, deletes it from RAM, and loads the *next* 1KB.
4.  **Result**: The process repeats 500 times.
5.  **Output**: The large file arrives perfectly on the browser, and the Pico's memory stays 90% free the entire time.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import time
import os
import gc

class StreamingServer:
    def __init__(self, filename="large_data.txt"):
        self.target = filename
        # Create a dummy large file if it doesn't exist
        try: os.stat(self.target)
        except:
            with open(self.target, "w") as f:
                f.write("A" * 50000) # 50KB dummy file

    async def _stream_handler(self, reader, writer):
        try:
            # Consume request
            await reader.readline()
            
            print(f"[MEM] Initial RAM: {gc.mem_free()} bytes")
            
            # 1. Send HTTP Header
            writer.write(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\nContent-Type: text/plain\r\n\r\n")
            
            # 2. THE STREAMING ENGINE
            with open(self.target, "rb") as f:
                while True:
                    # Read 1KB
                    chunk = f.read(1024)
                    if not chunk: break
                    
                    # Send 1KB
                    writer.write(chunk)
                    
                    # Wait for hardware to finish sending before reading more
                    await writer.drain()
                    
                    # Manually trigger cleanup to be safe
                    gc.collect()
            
            print(f"[MEM] Final RAM: {gc.mem_free()} bytes (Consistent!)")

        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Streaming failed: {e}")
        finally:
            writer.close()
            await writer.wait_closed()

    async def start(self):
        wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
        wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
        while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(1)
        
        print(f"[SYS] Secure listener active on port 80")
        await asyncio.start_server(self._stream_handler, "0.0.0.0", 80)
        while True: await asyncio.sleep(10)

# Run
srv = StreamingServer()
try:
    asyncio.run(srv.start())
except: pass
```

**11. Common Mistakes:**  
*   **Forgot `drain()`**: If you write to the socket faster than the Wi-Fi can send, the internal buffer fills up and consumes all RAM anyway! `await writer.drain()` pauses your code until the buffer is empty, ensuring true memory safety.  

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Project 1527**: Build a formal JSON API pipeline that serializes sensor data on the fly.

## 1. Project 1527: Professional HTTP Server Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Transform the Pico W into a **REST API** by generating structured JSON data pipelines that allow other computers and mobile apps to interact with your sensors.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **REST API**: A design style where web URLs represent data resources (e.g., `/api/temp`).
*   **JSON Serialization**: Converting complex Python data structures (Lists/Dictionaries) into a universal text format.
*   **Application MIME Types**: Defining the payload content using headers so the client knows how to parse it.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/MIME Logic |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `create_dictionary`** (Construct the payload)
*   **from Data, drag `json_serialize`** (Convert to text)
*   **from Network, drag `set_content_type`** (Set to application/json)
*   **from Math, drag `read_internal_temp`** (Source data)

### 7. Variables
*   **packet_data**: Dictionary (The structured sensor information).
*   **json_payload**: String (The serialized outgoing text).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Data Assembly Phase**
1.  **Gather Telemetry**:
    *   Read internal temperature or connected ADC sensors.
2.  **Serialize**:
    *   From **Data**, use `ujson.dumps()` to turn your dictionary into a string.
    *   Store in `json_payload`.

**B. Header Phase**
3.  **Define Format**:
    *   From **Network**, send the header `"Content-Type: application/json"`. This tells phones and servers that this is a data object, not a visual web page.
4.  **Send Status**:
    *   Transmit `"HTTP/1.1 200 OK"`.

**C. Transmission Phase**
5.  **Pipe the Bytes**:
    *   Write the `json_payload` to the `writer`.
6.  **Verify**:
    *   Print "[DATA] API Packet Sent" to the console.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W waits for a data request from a Python script or mobile app.
2.  **Process**: The request arrives at `/api/sensors`.
3.  **Action**: The Pico instantly reads its environment and bundles it into a `{"status": "OK"}` object.
4.  **Result**: The structured text is transmitted over WiFi.
5.  **Output**: The receiving device automatically parses the JSON and can plot the data on a graph.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import ujson
import machine
import time

class SensorAPIHost:
    def __init__(self):
        self.adc = machine.ADC(4) # Internal Temp
        
    def _get_reading(self):
        # Convert raw ADC to Celsius (Simplified)
        raw = self.adc.read_u16() * (3.3 / 65535)
        temp = 27 - (raw - 0.706) / 0.001721
        return round(temp, 2)

    async def _api_handler(self, reader, writer):
        try:
            # 1. Logic Trace
            await reader.readline()
            
            # 2. Build JSON
            data = {
                "pico_id": "STATION_ALPHA",
                "temp_c": self._get_reading(),
                "uptime": time.ticks_ms() // 1000
            }
            payload = ujson.dumps(data)
            
            # 3. Transmit Professional Headers
            header = (
                "HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n"
                "Content-Type: application/json\r\n"
                f"Content-Length: {len(payload)}\r\n"
                "Connection: close\r\n\r\n"
            )
            
            writer.write(header.encode('utf-8'))
            writer.write(payload.encode('utf-8'))
            await writer.drain()
            
            print(f"[API] Served Telemetry: {payload}")

        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] API Failure: {e}")
        finally:
            writer.close()
            await writer.wait_closed()

    async def run(self):
        wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
        wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
        while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(1)
        
        print(f"[SYS] REST API active at http://{wlan.ifconfig()[0]}/api")
        await asyncio.start_server(self._api_handler, "0.0.0.0", 80)
        while True: await asyncio.sleep(10)

# Boot
api = SensorAPIHost()
try:
    asyncio.run(api.run())
except: pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Malformed JSON**: If your sensor reading produces `NaN`, `ujson` might produce invalid text. Always validate that your numbers are finite before serializing.
*   **Missing Content-Length**: Professional APIs should always include the `Content-Length` header so the client knows exactly how much data to expect.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-Point API**: Add more keys to your `data` dictionary, such as free RAM (`gc.mem_free()`) or current RTC time.

## 1. Project 1528: Secure Professional HTTP Server Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Protect your sensitive IoT controls using **HTTP Basic Authentication**, ensuring that only authorized users with the correct credentials can modify your system's state.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Basic Authentication**: A standard web mechanism that triggers a login popup in your browser.
*   **Authorization Header**: A hidden piece of text sent by the browser containing `Base64` encoded credentials.
*   **Base64 Decoding**: Converting a scrambled, space-saving string back into readable "Username:Password" text.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/Auth Logic |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Text, drag `decode_base64`** (Convert credentials)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_then`** (Validate the match)
*   **from Network, drag `send_401_status`** (Trigger the login popup)
*   **from Storage, drag `read_secret`** (Retrieve stored password)

### 7. Variables
*   **correct_auth**: String (The encoded Base64 we expect, e.g., `Basic YWRtaW46cGFzcw==`).
*   **header_line**: String (the raw text being checked from the browser).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Credentials Phase**
1.  **Define the Guard**:
    *   Decide on a username (e.g., `admin`) and password (e.g., `1234`).
    *   Use an online tool or the `ubinascii` library to create the Base64 version of `admin:1234`.
2.  **Initialize the Vault**:
    *   Store this string in a variable `AUTH_KEY`.

**B. Challenge Phase**
3.  **Inspect incoming Request**:
    *   Inside your server loop, read every header line.
4.  **Detect the Key**:
    *   **Snap** an `if...startswith` block to look for `"Authorization: Basic"`.
5.  **Challenge Internal**:
    *   If the header is missing, **Snap** a `send` block with `"HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized"`.
    *   **CRITICAL**: You MUST send the header `"WWW-Authenticate: Basic"` or the browser won't show the login box.

**C. Validation Phase**
6.  **Compare**:
    *   If the key exists, compare it with your `AUTH_KEY`.
7.  **Grant Access**:
    *   If they match, proceed to send the sensitive data. If not, re-send the 401 Challenge.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A user tries to visit the `/control` page on your Pico.
2.  **Process**: The Pico sees there is no password in the request.
3.  **Action**: It sends a 401 Challenge packet.
4.  **Result**: Your browser pops up a box asking for Username and Password.
5.  **Output**: After you type the correct info, the page finally opens securely.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import ubinascii

class MasterGuardServer:
    def __init__(self, user, pw):
        # Create the Base64 credential string once at boot
        raw = f"{user}:{pw}"
        self.key = b"Basic " + ubinascii.b2a_base64(raw).strip()
        print(f"[SEC] Security System Initialized. Key: {self.key}")

    async def _handler(self, reader, writer):
        addr = writer.get_extra_info('peername')
        authenticated = False
        
        try:
            # 1. Skip request line
            await reader.readline()
            
            # 2. SCAN HEADERS FOR AUTH
            while True:
                line = await reader.readline()
                if line == b'\r\n' or line == b'\n': break
                
                if line.lower().startswith(b"authorization:"):
                    client_key = line.split(b":")[1].strip()
                    if client_key == self.key:
                        authenticated = True

            # 3. DECISION ENGINE
            if authenticated:
                writer.write(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n\r\n<h1>VIP ACCESS GRANTED</h1>")
            else:
                # Send the "Challenge" header to trigger browser popup
                writer.write(b"HTTP/1.1 401 Unauthorized\r\n")
                writer.write(b"WWW-Authenticate: Basic realm=\"PicoGuard\"\r\n\r\n")
                writer.write(b"Access Denied: Please Login.")
                print(f"[WARN] Unauthorized entry attempt from {addr[0]}")

            await writer.drain()

        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Auth Error: {e}")
        finally:
            writer.close()
            await writer.wait_closed()

    async def launch(self):
        wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
        wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
        while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(1)
        
        print(f"[SYS] Guard active at http://{wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
        await asyncio.start_server(self._handler, "0.0.0.0", 80)
        while True: await asyncio.sleep(10)

# Run with 'admin' and 'pico789'
guard = MasterGuardServer("admin", "pico789")
try:
    asyncio.run(guard.launch())
except: pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Strip Trap**: `ubinascii.b2a_base64` adds a hidden newline `\n` to the end of your string. If you don't call `.strip()`, the comparison will always fail!
*   **Cleartext Risk**: Basic Auth is not encrypted. Anyone on your local WiFi can see your password with a packet sniffer. Use this for fun/learning, but not for banking!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Admin Panel**: Create a secure page that lets you remotely toggle an LED on Pin 15, but only if you are logged in.

## 1. Project 1529: Multi-Threaded Professional HTTP Server

### 2. Learning Objective
Achieve true parallel processing by offloading the networking stack to the Pico's second processor core, ensuring your critical control loops (like motor PID) never stutter during web requests.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dual-Core Concurrency**: Using the RP2040's two independent ARM processors to do two things at the exact same time.
*   **Inter-Core Communication**: Sharing data between the "Logic Core" (Core 0) and the "Network Core" (Core 1) safely.
*   **Thread Safety**: Designing code so that one core doesn't change a variable while the other core is in the middle of reading it.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Dual Core RP2040 |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `start_core_1`** (Launch the second processor)
*   **from Network, drag `init_wifi_as_station`** (Connect to router)
*   **from Logic, drag `while_true`** (The infinite control loop)
*   **from Output, drag `toggle_pin_state`** (Heartbeat monitor)

### 7. Variables
*   **system_load**: Integer (A dummy value shared between cores).
*   **thread_id**: Object (The reference to the background task).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Core 1 Initialization (The Worker)**
1.  **Define the Server Thread**:
    *   Create a function `launch_server()` that contains your standard AsyncIO setup from Project 1522.
2.  **Activate Background Core**:
    *   From **System**, call `_thread.start_new_thread(launch_server, ())`.
    *   This "wakes up" the second processor and gives it the networking job.

**B. Core 0 Main Loop (The Master)**
3.  **Establish Heartbeat**:
    *   Initialize an LED on Pin 25 (`"LED"`).
4.  **Simulate Heavy Load**:
    *   Create a loop that does a complex calculation (like calculating Pi or just incrementing a large counter).
5.  **Data Sharing**:
    *   Update a global variable `status_value` so Core 1 can show it on the web page.

**C. Verification Phase**
6.  **Test Concurrency**:
    *   Open the Pico's web page while the LED is flickering at high speed.
7.  **Monitor Performance**:
    *   Observe that the LED doesn't skip a single beat, even while the web page is loading 1KB of data!

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Core 0 connects to the internet.
2.  **Process**: Core 0 sends a signal to Core 1 to start the web server.
3.  **Action**: Core 1 begins listening for WiFi visitors.
4.  **Result**: Core 0 enters a 100% CPU usage loop for a robot balancing project.
5.  **Output**: A user visits the website; Core 1 handles the request immediately using its own dedicated processor, leaving Core 0 completely undisturbed.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import _thread
import machine
import time

# --- GLOBAL SHARED STATE ---
# Note: In MicroPython, simple variables are usually safe across cores.
# For complex data, use thread locks.
counter = 0

# --- CORE 1: DEDICATED WEB SERVER ---
def web_server_thread():
    print("[CORE 1] Warming up radio internals...")
    
    async def handle_request(reader, writer):
        global counter
        await reader.read(1024)
        
        response = (
            "HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n\r\n"
            f"<h1>Dual-Core Stats</h1>"
            f"<p>Main Logic Iteration: {counter}</p>"
        )
        writer.write(response)
        await writer.drain()
        writer.close()
        await writer.wait_closed()

    async def main():
        await asyncio.start_server(handle_request, "0.0.0.0", 80)
        while True: await asyncio.sleep(1)

    asyncio.run(main())

# --- CORE 0: HIGH-PRECISION LOGIC ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)
print(f"[CORE 0] System Online at {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")

# KICK OFF SECOND CORE
_thread.start_new_thread(web_server_thread, ())

# CRITICAL APPLICATION LOOP
led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)
while True:
    counter += 1
    # Rapid toggle to prove Core 0 is NOT waiting for WiFi requests
    led.toggle()
    time.sleep_ms(50) 
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Shared Memory Collision**: Do not try to write to the SAME list or dictionary from both cores at the same time. This will crash the Pico or corrupt your data. Use integers for simple flags.
*   **WLAN Conflict**: Always connect to WiFi on Core 0 *before* starting Core 1. The WiFi hardware is shared, and trying to initialize it from both cores simultaneously will cause a lock-up.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Dual Heartbeat**: Put one LED on Core 0 and one on Core 1. See if you can make them blink at different speeds perfectly.

## 1. Project 1530: Industrial Professional HTTP Server Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
Implement **Server-Sent Events (SSE)** to build real-time dashboards that push live sensor updates to the browser without requiring a manual page refresh.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Server-Sent Events (SSE)**: A one-way communication channel where the Pico "pushes" data to the browser as soon as it's ready.
*   **Event Stream Header**: A special mime-type (`text/event-stream`) that tells the browser to keep the connection open forever.
*   **Non-Blocking Updates**: Sending data packets without closing the socket, allowing for a continuous flow of information.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Real-Time Telemetry |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Communication, drag `init_sse_stream`** (Establish headers)
*   **from Communication, drag `push_event_data`** (Emit a value)
*   **from System, drag `repeat_every_1s`** (Timing the updates)
*   **from Text, drag `build_sse_packet`** (Format the message)

### 7. Variables
*   **sensor_val**: Float (The changing data to push).
*   **packet**: String (The formatted SSE message: `data: 24.5\n\n`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Handshake Phase**
1.  **Intercept Stream Request**:
    *   Monitor for a GET request to `/events`.
2.  **Send Persist Headers**:
    *   Transmit `"Content-Type: text/event-stream"`.
    *   Transmit `"Cache-Control: no-cache"`.
    *   Transmit `"Connection: keep-alive"`.
    *   **Snap** an `await writer.drain()` to finalize the handshake.

**B. Streaming Phase**
3.  **Enter Persistence Loop**:
    *   Create a `while True:` loop inside the handler.
4.  **Format the Message**:
    *   SSE requires a specific format. Set `packet` to `"data: " + sensor_value + "\n\n"`.
    *   The double newline `\n\n` is critical; it tells the browser the message is complete.
5.  **Push and Wait**:
    *   Write `packet` to the client.
    *   Call `await writer.drain()` immediately.
    *   Use `await asyncio.sleep(1)` to prevent flooding the network.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
6.  **Catch Disconnects**:
    *   Wrap the loop in a `try...except` block. If the user closes their browser tab, the Pico will see an error; handle this by cleaning up the socket.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W hosts a "Live Dashboard" page.
2.  **Process**: The browser runs a small JavaScript snippet that links to the `/events` URL.
3.  **Action**: The Pico starts sending the current temperature every 1 second.
4.  **Result**: The browser receives these tiny 10-byte packets without refreshing the entire page.
5.  **Output**: The user sees the temperature number changing on their screen in real-time, just like a professional industrial monitor.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import random

class IndustrialDashboard:
    def __init__(self):
        self.html = """
        <html><body>
        <h1>Live Industrial Telemetry</h1>
        <h2 id="val">Connecting...</h2>
        <script>
            var source = new EventSource('/events');
            source.onmessage = function(e) {
                document.getElementById('val').innerText = "Sensor: " + e.data + "%";
            };
        </script>
        </body></html>
        """

    async def _sse_handler(self, reader, writer, is_event_req):
        try:
            if is_event_req:
                # 1. ESTABLISH SSE HANDSHAKE
                print("[SSE] Client Connected.")
                writer.write(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n")
                writer.write(b"Content-Type: text/event-stream\r\n")
                writer.write(b"Cache-Control: no-cache\r\n")
                writer.write(b"Connection: keep-alive\r\n\r\n")
                await writer.drain()

                # 2. DATA PUSH LOOP
                while True:
                    reading = random.randint(40, 60)
                    # SSE Protocol requires: "data: <msg>\n\n"
                    packet = f"data: {reading}\n\n"
                    writer.write(packet.encode('utf-8'))
                    await writer.drain()
                    
                    await asyncio.sleep(1) # Frequency
            else:
                # Normal HTML delivery
                writer.write(b"HTTP/1.1 200 OK\r\n\r\n" + self.html)
                await writer.drain()

        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[SYS] Client disconnected: {e}")
        finally:
            writer.close()
            await writer.wait_closed()

    async def main_server(self, reader, writer):
        # Determine if this is a request for the UI or the Event Data
        line = await reader.readline()
        is_event = b"GET /events" in line
        # Consume rest of headers
        while await reader.readline() != b"\r\n": pass
        
        await self._sse_handler(reader, writer, is_event)

    async def run(self):
        wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
        wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
        while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(1)
        
        print(f"[SYS] Dashboard Live at http://{wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
        await asyncio.start_server(self.main_server, "0.0.0.0", 80)
        while True: await asyncio.sleep(10)

# Execute
dash = IndustrialDashboard()
try:
    asyncio.run(dash.run())
except: pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing Newlines**: If you send `data: 123` but forget the `\n\n`, the browser will wait forever for more data and never update the display.
*   **Blocking Sleep**: Never use `time.sleep()` inside an SSE handler! It will block the entire server for all users. Always use `await asyncio.sleep()`.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Multi-Sensor Stream**: Send different event types (e.g., `event: temp\ndata: 22\n\n` followed by `event: humid\ndata: 40\n\n`) to update multiple parts of your UI independently.

## Batch 154: Professional REST API Client (1531-1540)

## 1. Project 1531: Professional REST API Client Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Transition from being a server to a client by using the `urequests` library to proactively fetch live JSON data from global web APIs, enabling your device to react to real-world events.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **HTTP GET Client**: A role where the Pico initiates requests to remote servers (like a browser).
*   **JSON Deserialization**: The process of turning a web response string into a usable Python dictionary.
*   **Resource Management**: The critical necessity of closing network connections to prevent memory exhaustion (leaks).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/TCP Client |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `http_get_request`** (Fetch data)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_status_200`** (Validate response)
*   **from Data, drag `parse_json`** (Convert text to dict)
*   **from Network, drag `close_connection`** (Free resources)

### 7. Variables
*   **api_url**: String (The target endpoint).
*   **response**: Object (The raw network reply).
*   **weather_val**: Float (The extracted numeric data).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Target**:
    *   Set `api_url` to `"http://worldtimeapi.org/api/timezone/Etc/UTC"`.
2.  **Verify WiFi**:
    *   Ensure the standard Project 1501/1506 connection is established.

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Execute Fetch**:
    *   From **Network**, **snap** a `urequests.get(api_url)` block.
    *   Store the result in the `response` variable.
4.  **Confirm Handshake**:
    *   **Snap** an `if...status_code == 200` block.

**C. Extraction & Cleanup Phase**
5.  **Process Payload**:
    *   From **Data**, call `response.json()`.
    *   Extract the `"datetime"` key and print it.
6.  **Secure Memory**:
    *   **CRITICAL SNAP**: Drag a `response.close()` block into the `finally` or end of the script. This is mandatory for every request.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W joins the local WiFi network.
2.  **Process**: It sends a packet across the internet to `worldtimeapi.org`.
3.  **Action**: The server sends back a text string containing the current world time.
4.  **Result**: The Pico "reads" the text, understands the time format, and prints it to the terminal.
5.  **Output**: "Current Time: 2026-01-07 19:27:07".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
import urequests
import gc

class RestClientDriver:
    def __init__(self, ssid, password):
        self.wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
        self.wlan.active(True)
        self.wlan.connect(ssid, password)
        
    def _wait_for_wifi(self):
        timeout = 15
        while timeout > 0 and not self.wlan.isconnected():
            timeout -= 1
            time.sleep(1)
        return self.wlan.isconnected()

    def fetch_time(self):
        url = "http://worldtimeapi.org/api/timezone/Etc/UTC"
        res = None
        try:
            print(f"[HTTP] Connecting to {url}")
            res = urequests.get(url)
            
            if res.status_code == 200:
                data = res.json()
                print(f"[DATA] UTC Time: {data['datetime']}")
                print(f"[DATA] Unix Stamp: {data['unixtime']}")
            else:
                print(f"[ERR] Server returned {res.status_code}")
                
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Network failure: {e}")
        finally:
            if res:
                res.close() # MANDATORY: Prevent socket leak
                print("[SYS] Connection closed, RAM recovered.")
            gc.collect()

# Main
client = RestClientDriver("SSID", "PASS")
if client._wait_for_wifi():
    client.fetch_time()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Leak**: Forgetting `res.close()`. If you do this in a loop, your Pico will run out of "File Descriptors" after about 5-8 requests and simple stop working.
*   **HTTPS Overhead**: While `urequests` handles SSL, it uses a lot of RAM. If your request is huge, you might hit an `Out of Memory` error before the page even finishes loading.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1532**: Synchronous `get()` blocks everything. Switch to an **Async Client** so you can blink an LED while the web request is in progress.
