## 1. Project 1532: Asynchronous Professional REST API Client Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate system freezing during network calls by implementing non-blocking asynchronous HTTP handlers, ensuring that critical tasks like sensor polling continue while waiting for a web response.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Asynchronous Client Overhead**: Manually managing the HTTP connection handshake without blocking the main event loop.
*   **Non-Blocking I/O Streams**: Using `open_connection` to create a concurrent data pipeline.
*   **Yield Points**: Using `await` to let the Pico handle other jobs (like blinking an LED) during the 500ms network delay.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | CYW43439/Async Tasks |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `await_connection`** (Non-blocking handshake)
*   **from Network, drag `stream_write`** (Send request bytes)
*   **from Network, drag `stream_read_line`** (Fetch response header)
*   **from System, drag `create_task`** (Background process)

### 7. Variables
*   **reader**: Object (The incoming data stream).
*   **writer**: Object (The outgoing request stream).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Target Endpoint**:
    *   Set `host` to `"worldtimeapi.org"` and `path` to `"/api/timezone/Europe/London"`.
2.  **Establish Task Concurrency**:
    *   **Snap** an `asyncio.create_task()` for a simple LED blinker.

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Initiate Socket**:
    *   From **Network**, call `asyncio.open_connection()`.
4.  **Send HTTP GET**:
    *   Manually construct the GET request: `"GET /path HTTP/1.0\r\nHost: host\r\n\r\n"`.
    *   **Snap** `writer.write()` and **snap** `await writer.drain()`.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Listen for Body**:
    *   Skip the headers by loop-reading lines until a blank line is found.
    *   **Snap** `await reader.read()` to get the payload.
6.  **Sever & Free**:
    *   Call `writer.close()` and **snap** `await writer.wait_closed()`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Two tasks start: One blinks the LED every 100ms, the other starts the web request.
2.  **Process**: The web request sends a packet and then *sleeps* while waiting for the server.
3.  **Action**: During this sleep, the event loop keeps the LED blinking perfectly.
4.  **Result**: The web data arrives; the handler wakes up and prints the time.
5.  **Output**: The user sees the LED never stuttering, even during heavy internet traffic.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import machine
import gc

class AsyncAPIHandler:
    def __init__(self, host, path):
        self.host = host
        self.path = path
        self.led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)

    async def _blink_heartbeat(self):
        """Proof that the CPU isn't blocked by the network."""
        while True:
            self.led.toggle()
            await asyncio.sleep(0.1)

    async def run_fetch(self):
        print(f"[TCP] Connecting to {self.host}...")
        try:
            # 1. NON-BLOCKING CONNECTION
            reader, writer = await asyncio.open_connection(self.host, 80)
            
            # 2. SEND RAW HTTP 1.0 (Manual construction)
            request = f"GET {self.path} HTTP/1.0\r\nHost: {self.host}\r\n\r\n"
            writer.write(request.encode('utf-8'))
            await writer.drain()
            
            # 3. HEADER SCANNER
            while True:
                line = await reader.readline()
                if line == b'\r\n' or not line: break # End of headers
            
            # 4. DATA COLLECTION
            body = await reader.read()
            print(f"[RECV] Payload Size: {len(body)} bytes")
            
            # 5. CLEANUP
            writer.close()
            await writer.wait_closed()
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Async Fetch Failed: {e}")

    async def main_loop(self):
        # Background task
        asyncio.create_task(self._blink_heartbeat())
        # Foreground task
        await self.run_fetch()
        print("[SYS] Task sequence complete.")

# Main
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
# Simple block for initial connect, async thereafter
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

app = AsyncAPIHandler("worldtimeapi.org", "/api/timezone/Etc/UTC")
asyncio.run(app.main_loop())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Host Splitting**: Do not pass `http://google.com` to `open_connection`. You must separate the domain (`google.com`) from the protocol and path.
*   **Missing Header Blank Line**: If you don't wait for the `b'\r\n'` blank line, you will try to parse the "Status: 200 OK" text as JSON, causing a crash.

## 1. Project 1533: Object-Oriented Professional REST API Client Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Abstract complex API interactions into reusable **SDK (Software Development Kit) Classes**, allowing your main application to interact with high-level data (like temperature) without worrying about URL construction or JSON parsing.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **API Wrapper**: A class that "wraps" around a web service to make it easier to use.
*   **Encapsulation (Security)**: Storing the API Key inside the class instance so it isn't accidentally exposed in global variables.
*   **Response Abstraction**: Converting a 100-line JSON response into a simple Python Dictionary containing only the data you need.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Software Engineering Logic |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `define_class`** (Structure the wrapper)
*   **from Data, drag `get_dict_value`** (Deep data extraction)
*   **from Network, drag `http_get_with_params`** (Construct the URL)
*   **from Text, drag `combine_text`** (Build the request)

### 7. Variables
*   **client**: Object (The instance of the weather class).
*   **weather_info**: Dictionary (The simplified output: `{'temp': 20.5, 'mood': 'Sunny'}`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Class Definition Phase**
1.  **Initialize Identity**:
    *   Create a constructor `__init__` that takes an `api_key`.
    *   Define the `base_url` for the service (e.g., OpenWeatherMap).
2.  **Define Protocol**:
    *   Create a method `get_city_weather(city)`.

**B. Data Processing Phase**
3.  **Concatenate Parameters**:
    *   **Snap** a block that adds `?q=[CITY]&appid=[KEY]` to the base URL.
4.  **Execute & Parse**:
    *   Call `urequests.get()` inside the method.
    *   Check for common error codes (401 for bad keys, 404 for missing cities).

**C. Application Phase**
5.  **Simplify result**:
    *   Extract `data["main"]["temp"]` and return it.
6.  **Clean Call**:
    *   In the main script, simply write `result = weather.get_city_weather("London")`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The main application creates a "Weather Specialist" object using its secret API Key.
2.  **Process**: The main app asks the specialist for "London".
3.  **Action**: The object handles the URL, the network connection, and the JSON parsing in the background.
4.  **Result**: The object returns a simple number: `23.5`.
5.  **Output**: The application displays the temperature without ever "seeing" a single bracket of JSON.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import urequests
import time
import gc

class OpenWeatherSDK:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.endpoint = "http://api.openweathermap.org/data/2.5/weather"

    def get_temp(self, city):
        # 1. CONSTRUCT URL WITH PARAMS
        url = f"{self.endpoint}?q={city}&appid={self.key}&units=metric"
        
        response = None
        try:
            print(f"[SDK] Requesting data for {city}...")
            response = urequests.get(url)
            
            # 2. VALIDATE STATUS
            if response.status_code == 200:
                full_data = response.json()
                # 3. ABSTRACTION: Return only what matters
                return {
                    "temp": full_data["main"]["temp"],
                    "condition": full_data["weather"][0]["main"]
                }
            elif response.status_code == 401:
                print("[AUTH] Error: Invalid API Key!")
            elif response.status_code == 404:
                print("[SITE] Error: City not found!")
                
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[SYS] API Connection Error: {e}")
        finally:
            if response: response.close()
            gc.collect()
        return None

# --- Application Layer ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): time.sleep(1)

# Initialize with dummy key
weather_bot = OpenWeatherSDK("1234567890abcdef1234567890abcdef")

# Simple, high-level interaction
data = weather_bot.get_temp("London")

if data:
    print(f"Current Stats: {data['temp']}C and {data['condition']}")
else:
    print("Failed to retrieve intelligence.")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Hardcoding Secrets**: Never write your API Key directly in the class file. Always pass it into the constructor so you can change it without editing the logic code.
*   **Dictionary Depth**: If you try to access `data["main"]["temp"]` but the city doesn't exist, you'll get a `KeyError`. Always check the `status_code` first!

## 1. Project 1534: Robust Professional REST API Client Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
Protect your API credentials and the remote server's resources by implementing a **Rate-Limiting Controller** that throttles outbound requests, preventing the Pico from being banned by global web services.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Throttling/Rate Limiting**: The practice of artificially slowing down request frequency to match a service provider's speed limit (e.g., 1 call per 10 seconds).
*   **Delta-Time Logic**: Calculating the time difference (`ticks_diff`) between now and the previous request to decide if a new call is permitted.
*   **Data Caching**: Returning the "last known good" value if a request is blocked by the rate limiter, ensuring the UI always has something to show.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Industrial Control Flow |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `ticks_ms`** (Get current millisecond clock)
*   **from Time, drag `ticks_diff`** (Compare two timestamps)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_interval_exceeded`** (Permission check)
*   **from Output, drag `print_to_console`** (Status logging)

### 7. Variables
*   **interval_ms**: Integer (The mandatory cooldown period).
*   **last_request_time**: Integer (Timestamp of the previous success).
*   **cached_data**: Object (The stored result for use during cooldown).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Controller Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Limits**:
    *   Set `interval_ms` to `10000` (10 seconds).
2.  **Initialize Cache**:
    *   Set `last_request_time` to a very small initial value.

**B. Transmission Control Phase**
3.  **Timestamp Check**:
    *   Calculate `diff = ticks_diff(now, last_request_time)`.
4.  **Permit or Block**:
    *   **Snap** an `if...` block: If `diff < interval_ms`, enter the Cooldown Block.
    *   In the Cooldown Block, simply return the `cached_data`.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Update State**:
    *   If the request is permitted, immediately update `last_request_time = now`.
6.  **Execute & Refresh Cache**:
    *   Run the `urequests.get()` logic from Project 1531.
    *   Update `cached_data` with the new result for future cooldowns.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico application runs a loop that tries to get weather every 1 second.
2.  **Process**: The Controller intercepts the request. For the first call, it allows it.
3.  **Action**: For the next 9 attempts, the Controller blocks the internet call and gives the app the "old" data instantly.
4.  **Result**: The application remains fast and responsive, but the internet usage is cut by 90%.
5.  **Output**: "Rate Limit: Returning CACHED data...".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
import urequests
import machine
import gc

class PollingController:
    def __init__(self, cooldown_sec=10):
        self.cooldown_ms = cooldown_sec * 1000
        self.last_run = time.ticks_ms() - self.cooldown_ms
        self.cache = "No Data Yet"

    def fetch_secured(self):
        now = time.ticks_ms()
        elapsed = time.ticks_diff(now, self.last_run)

        # 1. THROTTLING LOGIC
        if elapsed < self.cooldown_ms:
            wait_remaining = (self.cooldown_ms - elapsed) // 1000
            print(f"[THROTTLE] Cool-down active. {wait_remaining}s left. Using Cache.")
            return self.cache

        # 2. PERMITTED REQUEST
        print("[HTTP] Cooldown expired. Contacting API...")
        self.last_run = now
        
        res = None
        try:
            # Demonstration endpoint (World Time)
            res = urequests.get("http://worldtimeapi.org/api/timezone/Etc/UTC")
            if res.status_code == 200:
                self.cache = res.json()["datetime"]
                print("[CACHE] Updated with fresh data.")
            else:
                print(f"[ERR] API returned {res.status_code}")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Link failure: {e}")
        finally:
            if res: res.close()
            gc.collect()

        return self.cache

# --- Main App ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# Request data every 10s, though we will try every 2s
controller = PollingController(cooldown_sec=10)

for i in range(10):
    val = controller.fetch_secured()
    print(f"Update {i}: {val}")
    time.sleep(2) # App loop is faster than API limit
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **External 429 Errors**: Even with your controller, another device might be using your API key. Always check `if status_code == 429` (Too Many Requests) and force an extra 1-minute wait.
*   **Cache Stale-ness**: If your cooldown is too long (e.g., 2 hours), your data might be dangerously out of date. Balance safety with accuracy.

## 1. Project 1535: Professional REST API Client with Error Handling

### 2. Learning Objective
Build bulletproof network applications by implementing **Defensive Extraction** and multi-stage error trapping, ensuring the Pico never crashes when an API returns malformed data or server errors.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Status Code Gating**: Explicitly checking for 200 (OK) before attempting to read any data.
*   **Malformed JSON Handling**: Catching `ValueError` during the `json()` call to handle HTML error pages that pretend to be JSON.
*   **Schema Safety (`.get()` method)**: Using dictionary lookup defaults so that a missing key (like "price") doesn't cause a `KeyError`.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Software Hardening |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `try_except_value_error`** (Handle bad text)
*   **from Data, drag `get_with_default`** (Safe dictionary lookup)
*   **from System, drag `print_to_error_log`** (Diagnostic tracking)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_status_not_200`** (Server error handling)

### 7. Variables
*   **raw_data**: Object (The parsed JSON dictionary).
*   **safe_val**: Float (The final extracted value or a default).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define Defaults**:
    *   Set `safe_val` to `-1.0` (indicates error).

**B. Multi-Stage Validation Phase**
2.  **Gate 1 (The Handshake)**:
    *   Verify `status_code == 200`. If it's 404 or 500, log the error and exit the function safely.
3.  **Gate 2 (The Parser)**:
    *   **Snap** a `try...except ValueError` block around `res.json()`.
    *   If the server is down and sends a plain text "Gateway Timeout", this block prevents a crash.

**C. Defensive Extraction Phase**
4.  **Gate 3 (The Schema)**:
    *   Instead of `data["temp"]`, use `data.get("temp", None)`.
    *   **Snap** an `if...is None:` check to verify the key exists.
5.  **Final Delivery**:
    *   Return the verified value to the application loop.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W requests a cryptocurrency price.
2.  **Process**: The API server has a glitch and sends back a "Maintenance" HTML page.
3.  **Action**: The Pico sees `status_code 503`.
4.  **Result**: The error logic skips the parsing entirely, prints a warning, and returns the default `-1.0`.
5.  **Output**: "System Error (503). Using safe default: -1.0".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import urequests
import time
import gc

class DefensiveClient:
    def __init__(self, target_url):
        self.url = target_url

    def fetch_data_securely(self):
        res = None
        try:
            print(f"[HTTP] Querying {self.url}...")
            res = urequests.get(self.url)
            
            # STAGE 1: HTTP CHECK
            if res.status_code != 200:
                print(f"[ERR] Server rejection: {res.status_code}")
                return None
            
            # STAGE 2: PARSE CHECK
            try:
                raw_json = res.json()
            except ValueError:
                print("[ERR] Response is not valid JSON! (Likely HTML error page)")
                return None
            
            # STAGE 3: SCHEMA EXTRACTION
            # Defensive .get() prevents KeyError if the API changes
            # We expected 'datetime', but return a fallback if missing
            timestamp = raw_json.get("datetime", "TIMESTAMP_MISSING")
            
            return timestamp

        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Network level crash: {e}")
            return None
        finally:
            if res: res.close()
            gc.collect()

# --- Execution ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

client = DefensiveClient("http://worldtimeapi.org/api/timezone/Etc/UTC")
result = client.fetch_data_securely()

if result:
    print(f"Verified Intelligence: {result}")
else:
    print("Mission Aborted: API unreliable.")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **One-Liner Hubris**: Beginners write `val = urequests.get(url).json()["data"]`. This will crash for at least 4 different reasons (No WiFi, 404 Error, Bad JSON, Missing Key). Always use the 3-stage validation.
*   **Ignoring 404**: A 404 (Not Found) still counts as a "valid" response that sends data. If you don't check the status code, you'll try to parse the 404 page text as JSON.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1536**: If the JSON you are fetching is massive (over 50KB), `res.json()` will crash the RAM. Move to **Streaming Parsing**.

## 1. Project 1536: Optimized Professional REST API Client Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Overcome RAM limitations by implementing **Stream-Based JSON Parsing**, allowing your Pico W to process large web responses (100KB+) without storing the entire file in memory at once.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Socket Streams**: Reading data byte-by-byte directly from the network hardware instead of downloading the whole chunk first.
*   **ujson.load() (vs loads)**: The `load` function takes a "file-like object" (the socket) and "sips" data only as fast as it can parse it.
*   **Buffer Control**: Skipping HTTP headers line-by-line to find the start of the data without filling up the string buffer.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Memory Architecture |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Communication, drag `init_tcp_socket`** (Raw connection)
*   **from Data, drag `stream_json_load`** (Memory-efficient parse)
*   **from Text, drag `readline_from_stream`** (Header skipping)
*   **from Logic, drag `while_until_blank`** (Identify data start)

### 7. Variables
*   **sock**: Object (The active TCP socket).
*   **data_stream**: Object (The socket wrapped in a readable file format).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Resolve Domain**:
    *   Use `socket.getaddrinfo()` to find the IP address of the target host.
2.  **Open Raw Channel**:
    *   Initialize a `socket.socket()` and connect to the IP on Port 80.

**B. Transmission & Buffer Phase**
3.  **Send Byte-String**:
    *   Send the raw GET request string manually.
4.  **Header Filtration**:
    *   Use a `while True:` loop to read lines from the socket.
    *   **Snap** an `if line == b'\r\n': break` block to find the exact point where headers end and JSON begins.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Inject the Stream**:
    *   **Snap** the `ujson.load(sock)` block. This pulls data directly from the network into the dictionary.
6.  **Sever & GC**:
    *   Immediately close the socket and call `gc.collect()` to free pointer memory.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W connects to a server providing a massive list of city global coordinates.
2.  **Process**: The Pico doesn't have 150KB of RAM to store the text file.
3.  **Action**: As data arrives over the air, `ujson.load` converts it into tiny Python objects immediately.
4.  **Result**: The 150KB string never exists in the Pico's memory; only the final 5KB dictionary remains.
5.  **Output**: "Stream Parsing Complete. Cities found: 500".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import ujson
import gc
import time

class StreamProcessor:
    def __init__(self, host, path):
        self.host = host
        self.path = path

    def get_large_json(self):
        print(f"[STREAM] Connecting to {self.host}...")
        
        # 1. RAW ADDRESS RESOLUTION
        addr_info = socket.getaddrinfo(self.host, 80)
        addr = addr_info[0][-1]
        
        sock = socket.socket()
        try:
            sock.connect(addr)
            
            # 2. RAW REQUEST
            request = f"GET {self.path} HTTP/1.0\r\nHost: {self.host}\r\n\r\n"
            sock.send(request.encode())
            
            # 3. WRAP SOCKET AS FILE (Required for ujson.load)
            stream = sock.makefile('rb')
            
            # 4. SKIP HEADERS LINE-BY-LINE
            print("[STREAM] Filtering headers...")
            while True:
                line = stream.readline()
                if line == b'\r\n' or not line: break
            
            # 5. THE MAGIC: STREAMING PARSE
            # This pulls data from network directly into Python objects
            print("[STREAM] " + "="*20)
            data = ujson.load(stream)
            print("[STREAM] Success. JSON structure built.")
            
            return data

        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Stream Failure: {e}")
            return None
        finally:
            sock.close()
            gc.collect()

# --- Usage ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

processor = StreamProcessor("worldtimeapi.org", "/api/timezone/Etc/UTC")
payload = processor.get_large_json()

if payload:
    print(f"Extracted Time: {payload.get('datetime')}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Socket Wrapping**: You cannot pass a raw `socket` to `ujson.load()`. You must first call `sock.makefile('rb')` to turn it into a readable stream.
*   **Buffer Limit**: While streaming saves "Text String" RAM, the final "Python Dictionary" still takes up memory. If the data structure itself is 500KB, the Pico will still crash.

## 1. Project 1537: Professional REST API Client Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Enable your Pico W to proactively push critical system alerts and sensor data to global communication platforms by implementing **HTTP POST Webhooks**, transforming a passive device into an active messenger.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **HTTP POST**: The standard method for sending data (the "Payload") to a server for processing.
*   **Webhooks**: A dedicated URL that acts as a "drop box". Sending data to this URL triggers an action, such as a Discord message or a Slack notification.
*   **MIME Types (application/json)**: Telling the server that the data we are sending is formatted as a JSON string so it can be parsed correctly.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Outbound Telemetry |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Serialization, drag `dict_to_json`** (Prepare payload)
*   **from Network, drag `http_post_request`** (Push data)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_post_success`** (Verify delivery)
*   **from Network, drag `release_socket`** (Memory safety)

### 7. Variables
*   **discord_webhook**: String (Your private API link).
*   **alert_msg**: Dictionary (The structured data: `{'content': 'ALERT'}`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define the Pipe**:
    *   Set `discord_webhook` to your secret URL (or use `"https://httpbin.org/post"` for safe testing).
2.  **Verify WiFi**:
    *   Initialize the connection using the standard Project 1501 method.

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Construct the Payload**:
    *   From **Serialization**, create a dictionary with the key `"content"`.
    *   Set the value to `"Pico Alert: Motion Detected in Lab!"`.
4.  **Execute the Push**:
    *   **Snap** a `urequests.post(url, json=data)` block.
    *   **Parameter**: Using `json=data` automatically sets the "application/json" header for you.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Audit the Result**:
    *   **Snap** an `if...status_code` block. Discord returns `204` (No Content) for success.
6.  **Sever the Link**:
    *   Call `response.close()` immediately to free the network socket for the next alert.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A physical event occurs (e.g., a button is pressed or a PIR sensor triggers).
2.  **Process**: The Pico W constructs a JSON object containing the timestamp and a custom message.
3.  **Action**: The Pico establishes an outbound connection to Discord's servers and "drops off" the data.
4.  **Result**: The Discord cloud captures the data and instantly relays it to your phone/PC app.
5.  **Output**: You receive a push notification from your Pico W!

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import urequests
import time
import gc

class WebhookMessenger:
    def __init__(self, webhook_url):
        self.url = webhook_url

    def send_notification(self, message):
        # 1. PREPARE PAYLOAD
        # Note: Different services use different keys (Discord uses 'content')
        payload = {"content": f"[PICO] {message}"}
        
        response = None
        try:
            print("[WEBHOOK] Sending alert...")
            # 2. TRANSMIT
            # The 'json' parameter handles serialization AND headers automatically
            response = urequests.post(self.url, json=payload)
            
            # 3. VERIFY (Discord 204 = Success)
            if response.status_code in [200, 204]:
                print("[SYS] Notification delivered successfully.")
                return True
            else:
                print(f"[ERR] Server rejection: {response.status_code}")
                
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[ERR] Webhook crash: {e}")
        finally:
            if response: response.close()
            gc.collect()
        return False

# --- Usage ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# Replace with your real URL or httpbin for testing
DISCORD_URL = "https://discord.com/api/webhooks/your_id/your_token"
bot = WebhookMessenger(DISCORD_URL)

bot.send_notification("Laboratory Intrusion Detected!")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Key Mismatch**: If you use the key `"msg"` but the service (like Discord) expects `"content"`, the server will ignore your packet and return a 400 error. Always check the API documentation.
*   **Rate Limits**: Discord and Slack will ban you for 10 minutes if you send more than 30 messages in a few seconds. Use the Project 1534 controller!

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1538**: Currently, your alerts are sent "openly". Learn how to wrap them in **Identity-Verified SSL** for industrial security.

---

## 1. Project 1538: Secure Professional REST API Client Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize IoT security by implementing **SSL/TLS Certificate Verification**, ensuring your Pico W only communicates with authentic servers and is immune to Man-In-The-Middle (MITM) attacks.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Identity Verification**: Checking the server's digital ID card (Certificate) before sending any secret data (like API Keys).
*   **Certificate Pinning**: Hardcoding a Root CA's "Fingerprint" into your script so hackers cannot use fake certificates.
*   **Time-Sensitive Handshakes**: Understanding why NTP synchronization is mandatory for SSL to work (expired dates cause rejection).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Cybersecurity Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Communication, drag `init_ssl_module`** (Load cryptography)
*   **from Security, drag `wrap_socket_securely`** (Encryption)
*   **from Network, drag `check_hostname`** (Verify matching domain)
*   **from Time, drag `sync_ntp_high_precision`** (Mandatory for SSL)

### 7. Variables
*   **trusted_ca**: Bytes (The binary Root Certificate data).
*   **secure_sock**: Object (The encrypted data channel).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Sync Clock**:
    *   **CRITICAL**: Initialize NTP from Project 1511. If your Pico's clock is set to "1970", all web certificates will look like they are "from the future" and fail.
2.  **Define Trusted Root**:
    *   Load the Root CA certificate (in DER format) into the `trusted_ca` variable.

**B. Handshake Phase**
3.  **Prepare Socket**:
    *   Open a standard TCP socket to Port 443 (HTTPS).
4.  **Execute Wrap**:
    *   From **Security**, call `ssl.wrap_socket()`.
    *   **Snap** parameters for `server_hostname` and `cert_reqs=ssl.CERT_REQUIRED`.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Audit Cipher**:
    *   Print `ss.cipher()` to confirm the bit-strength of the encryption.
6.  **Secure Transmission**:
    *   Send and receive data using the new `ss` object instead of the raw socket.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W connects to the internet and updates its local clock via NTP.
2.  **Process**: It initiates a connection to `google.com`.
3.  **Action**: The server presents its ID card. The Pico compares this against its internal `trusted_ca` fingerprints.
4.  **Result**: If it matches, the 256-bit encrypted tunnel is opened. If it doesn't match, the Pico cuts the connection instantly.
5.  **Output**: "Secure Connection Established. Cipher: AES-256-GCM".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import socket
import ssl
import time
import ntptime
import gc

# 1. ROOT CA (Example: ISRG Root X1 for Let's Encrypt)
# In a real app, you would load this .der file from flash
TRUSTED_CA_DER = b"\x30\x82\x05\x0a..." # Truncated for example

def industrial_secure_fetch(host):
    # 2. SYNC TIME (Mandatory for SSL validation)
    try:
        ntptime.settime()
        print(f"[TIME] Updated: {time.gmtime()}")
    except:
        print("[ERR] Time sync failed. SSL will likely reject certs.")

    # 3. OPEN TCP
    print(f"[SEC] Initiating TLS handshake with {host}")
    addr = socket.getaddrinfo(host, 443)[0][-1]
    s = socket.socket()
    s.connect(addr)

    try:
        # 4. WRAP SECURELY
        # server_hostname is required for SNI (hosting multiple sites on one IP)
        ss = ssl.wrap_socket(s, server_hostname=host)
        
        # Verify Cipher Strength
        print(f"[CIPHER] Secure link using: {ss.cipher()}")
        
        # 5. EXECUTE ENCRYPTED GET
        request = f"GET /status/200 HTTP/1.0\r\nHost: {host}\r\n\r\n"
        ss.write(request.encode())
        
        # Read small chunk
        print(f"[RECV] Header Preview: {ss.read(32)}")
        
        ss.close()
        print("[SEC] Session ended safely.")
        
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"[ALERT] SECURITY BREACH OR CERT ERROR: {e}")
    finally:
        s.close()
        gc.collect()

# --- Main ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

industrial_secure_fetch("httpbin.org")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Skipping NTP**: This is the #1 reason SSL fails on microcontrollers. Without the correct year/month/day, the Pico cannot calculate if a certificate has expired.
*   **Leaf Pinning**: Do not hardcode the website's certificate, only the Root CA. Website certs change every few months; Root CAs last 20 years.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1539**: SSL handshakes are slow and CPU-heavy. Move the network stack to **Core 1** to prevent your main robot code from lagging.

## 1. Project 1539: Multi-Threaded Professional REST API Client

### 2. Learning Objective
Utilize the RP2040's dual-core architecture to send background telemetry heartbeats, ensuring your main application loop remains 100% responsive even during slow network handshakes.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Core 1 Concurrency**: Launching the MicroPython interpreter on the second processor core.
*   **Atomic Telemetry**: Sending "Heartbeat" messages (I am alive) every 60 seconds without pausing the main motor or sensor loops.
*   **Inter-Core Thread Safety**: Sharing a global string (Status) across cores so Core 1 knows what Core 0 is currently doing.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Parallel Multi-Processing |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `start_core_1`** (Launch second core)
*   **from Communication, drag `init_heartbeat_loop`** (Telemetry logic)
*   **from Data, drag `global_variable_sync`** (Share data between cores)
*   **from Logic, drag `while_true_background`** (Parallel loop)

### 7. Variables
*   **system_state**: String (Shared between threads: "READY", "WORKING", "ERROR").
*   **thread_id**: Object (Reference to the Core 1 background task).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Core 1 Background Phase**
1.  **Define the Worker**:
    *   Create a function `telemetry_worker()`.
    *   Inside, create a `while True:` loop that posts data to `httpbin.org`.
2.  **Activate Core 1**:
    *   From **System**, call `_thread.start_new_thread(telemetry_worker, ())`.
    *   The Pico will now execute this function on its second processor.

**B. Core 0 Main Phase**
3.  **Establish Primary Logic**:
    *   In the main script, start your complex robot balancing or sensor logic.
4.  **Update Shared State**:
    *   Periodically change the `system_state` variable. Core 1 will "see" this change and send it in the next heartbeat.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Audit Responsiveness**:
    *   Toggle an LED on Core 0 at high speed. Notice it doesn't flicker when the network call happens on Core 1.
6.  **Catch Thread Death**:
    *   Wrap the worker in a `try...except` block so a network crash doesn't permanently kill the background telemetry.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W joins the WiFi. Core 0 launches Core 1.
2.  **Process**: Core 0 begins a heavy math calculation (calculating Pi).
3.  **Action**: Core 1 simultaneously sends a POST request to a cloud logger.
4.  **Result**: The math calculation on Core 0 is never delayed by even a single microsecond.
5.  **Output**: "Main Thread: Iteration 500 | Background: Heartbeat Sent!".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import _thread
import urequests
import machine
import time
import gc

# 1. SHARED STATE (Global variables are accessible by both cores)
device_state = "IDLE"

# --- CORE 1: TELEMETRY WORKER ---
def core_1_telemetry():
    global device_state
    url = "https://httpbin.org/post"
    print("[THREAD] Background Monitor Active.")
    
    while True:
        try:
            # Send current Core 0 state to the cloud
            data = {"device": "PicoW_Industrial", "status": device_state}
            print(f"[THREAD] Syncing State: {device_state}")
            
            res = urequests.post(url, json=data)
            res.close()
            
            # Heavy Sleep on Core 1 doesn't affect Core 0
            time.sleep(30) 
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[THREAD ERR] Network skip: {e}")
            time.sleep(5) # Retry soon
        gc.collect()

# --- CORE 0: CRITICAL LOGIC ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# LAUNCH BACKGROUND CORE
_thread.start_new_thread(core_1_telemetry, ())

# APP LOOP
led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)
while True:
    device_state = "WORKING"
    led.on()
    time.sleep_ms(100) # Fast heartbeat
    
    device_state = "COOLING"
    led.off()
    time.sleep_ms(100)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Crash Propagation**: If your background thread crashes due to a network error and isn't in a `try/except` loop, it will disappear. You won't know it's gone until you check your cloud logs.
*   **Socket Multi-Access**: Never try to use the SAME socket or `urequests` object on both cores at once. The WiFi hardware is shared, but the Python objects are not thread-safe.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1540**: If Core 1 fails to send data, it currently just waits. Implement **Exponential Backoff** to make the retry logic professional.

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Project 1540**: Implement **MQTT**. It's the industry standard for this "Heartbeat + Command" architecture, essentially replacing our custom HTTP loop with a robust protocol.

## 1. Project 1540: Industrial Professional REST API Client Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
Ensure 100% data reliability on unreliable networks by implementing **Exponential Backoff Retries**, preventing the "Thundering Herd" server crash problem and guaranteeing that critical sensor packets are eventually delivered.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Exponential Backoff**: An algorithm where the wait time doubles after each failure (1s, 2s, 4s, 8s, 16s).
*   **Jitter (Randomization)**: Adding a random variation to retry times so that 1000 disconnected devices don't all reconnect at the exact same microsecond.
*   **Retry Exhaustion**: Defining a "Give Up" point after which data is saved to local storage (SD card) instead of the internet.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Industrial Reliability |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Control, drag `repeat_with_backoff`** (Reliable loop)
*   **from Logic, drag `calculate_retry_delay`** (Math formula)
*   **from Serialization, drag `save_to_local_cache`** (Failover storage)
*   **from Network, drag `check_is_connected`** (Pre-flight check)

### 7. Variables
*   **retry_count**: Integer (Current attempt number).
*   **max_attempts**: Integer (Limit before giving up).
*   **wait_timer**: Float (The dynamic delay calculated by the backoff formula).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Handshake Retry Phase**
1.  **Define the Wrapper**:
    *   Create a function `upload_telemetry(data)`.
2.  **Initialize Attempt Tracker**:
    *   Set `retry_count` to 0.

**B. Transmission Retry Phase**
3.  **Enter Attempt Loop**:
    *   **Snap** a `for attempt in range(5):` block.
4.  **Execute & Verify**:
    *   Inside the loop, run `urequests.post()`.
    *   If `status_code == 200`, **snap** a `return True` block to exit the loop immediately.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Calculate Backoff**:
    *   If failure occurs, calculate `delay = (2 ** attempt) + random.uniform(0, 1)`.
    *   **Snap** a `time.sleep(delay)` block.
6.  **Final Failover**:
    *   If the loop finishes 5 times without success, **snap** an `append_to_file("failsafe.txt", data)` block.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A forklift blocks the WiFi signal. The Pico W tries to send sensor data.
2.  **Process**: The first attempt fails. The Pico waits 1.2 seconds.
3.  **Action**: The second attempt fails. The Pico waits 2.5 seconds.
4.  **Action**: The signal clears. The third attempt succeeds.
5.  **Result**: The data is delivered to the cloud server without any user intervention or system crash.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import urequests
import time
import random
import gc

class IndustrialUploader:
    def __init__(self, endpoint):
        self.url = endpoint
        self.max_retries = 5

    def upload_robust(self, data):
        for attempt in range(self.max_retries):
            try:
                print(f"[RETRY] Attempt {attempt + 1}...")
                res = urequests.post(self.url, json=data)
                
                if res.status_code in [200, 204]:
                    res.close()
                    print("[SYS] Data verified in cloud.")
                    return True
                res.close()
            except Exception as e:
                print(f"[ERR] Attempt failed: {e}")

            # CALCULATE BACKOFF & JITTER
            # Formula: (2 ^ attempt) + Random(0-1)
            wait_sec = (2 ** attempt) + random.random()
            print(f"[BACKOFF] Waiting {wait_sec:.2f}s before next try.")
            time.sleep(wait_sec)

        print("[CRITICAL] Cloud unreachable. Saving to local flash.")
        self._save_to_flash(data)
        return False

    def _save_to_flash(self, data):
        with open("offline_log.txt", "a") as f:
            f.write(str(data) + "\n")
        gc.collect()

# --- Main ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

uploader = IndustrialUploader("https://httpbin.org/post")
sensor_data = {"temp": 24.5, "pressure": 1013}

uploader.upload_robust(sensor_data)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Linear Retry**: Do not retry every 1 second. If the server is overloaded, 1000 Picos retrying every second will act like a DDoS attack and keep the server dead. Always use **Exponential Backoff**.
*   **Missing Jitter**: If you don't add the random extra seconds, all your Picos will retry at the *exact same time*, causing a network spike.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1541**: You have mastered HTTP. Now move to **MQTT**, the industrial standard protocol for low-power, high-reliability message queuing.

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Project 1541**: We have mastered HTTP (Request/Response). Now we move to **MQTT**, the protocol built specifically for unreliable, low-bandwidth IoT networks.

## Batch 155: Professional MQTT IoT (1541-1550)

## 1. Project 1541: Professional MQTT IoT Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Establish a high-speed, lightweight message pipeline between your Pico W and the global internet by implementing the **MQTT (Message Queuing Telemetry Transport)** protocol, the gold standard for industrial IoT communication.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Pub/Sub Architecture**: A decoupling mechanism where devices "Publish" data to a "Topic" (like a news feed) and others "Subscribe" to receive it, without knowing each other's IP addresses.
*   **MQTT Broker**: The central hub (e.g., Mosquitto) that acts as the traffic controller, relaying messages between clients.
*   **Topic Hierarchy**: Using slashes to organize data (e.g., `factory/robot1/temp`) allowing for precise data routing.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Wireless IoT Stack |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Communication, drag `init_mqtt_client`** (Connect to broker)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_publish`** (Send data)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_subscribe`** (Listen for commands)
*   **from Logic, drag `on_mqtt_message`** (Event callback)

### 7. Variables
*   **broker_addr**: String (The URL of the MQTT server).
*   **client_id**: String (A unique name for this specific Pico).
*   **data_topic**: String (The destination channel for your data).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Identity Creation**:
    *   Set `client_id` using `ubinascii.hexlify(machine.unique_id())` to ensure it never conflicts with other devices.
2.  **Establish Handshake**:
    *   Initialize the `MQTTClient` with the broker address (e.g., `test.mosquitto.org`).
    *   **Snap** the `.connect()` block to verify the broker accepts the connection.

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Define Callbacks**:
    *   **Snap** an `on_message` callback function. This function runs automatically whenever a message arrives on a subscribed topic.
4.  **Active Listening**:
    *   **Snap** the `.subscribe("pico/cmd")` block.
5.  **Broadcasting**:
    *   **Snap** a `.publish("pico/status", "Hello World")` block to send your first packet.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
6.  **Polling Loop**:
    *   Inside the main `while True:` loop, you MUST call `.check_msg()` or `.wait_msg()` to tell the Pico to look for incoming network packets.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W connects to the local WiFi and then the global MQTT Broker.
2.  **Process**: The Pico announces its presence by publishing an "Online" message.
3.  **Action**: You use a mobile app (like MQTT Dash) to send a "Blink" command to the topic `pico/cmd`.
4.  **Result**: The Pico's callback function triggers immediately, parsing the command and flashing the LED.
5.  **Output**: "Broker Sync: MQTT Link Up | Received: Blink".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient
import machine
import ubinascii

# 1. UNIQUE IDENTITY
# Every MQTT client must have a unique ID or the broker will kick them off
CLIENT_ID = ubinascii.hexlify(machine.unique_id())
BROKER = "test.mosquitto.org" # Public test broker
TOPIC_PUB = b"pico/industrial/telemetry"
TOPIC_SUB = b"pico/industrial/cmd"

# 2. EVENT CALLBACK
def sub_callback(topic, msg):
    print(f"\n[RECEIVED] Topic: {topic.decode()} | Message: {msg.decode()}")
    if msg == b"RESET":
        print("[SYS] Remote reset requested...")
        machine.reset()

# --- Execution ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

try:
    # 3. INITIALIZE LINK
    print(f"[MQTT] Connecting to {BROKER}...")
    client = MQTTClient(CLIENT_ID, BROKER)
    client.set_callback(sub_callback)
    client.connect()
    print("[MQTT] Handshake Complete.")

    # 4. SET SUBSCRIPTIONS
    client.subscribe(TOPIC_SUB)
    
    while True:
        # Check for incoming commands
        client.check_msg()
        
        # Periodic Heartbeat
        print(".", end="")
        client.publish(TOPIC_PUB, b"ALIVE")
        time.sleep(5)

except Exception as e:
    print(f"[ERR] MQTT Failure: {e}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Duplicate Client IDs**: If you copy-paste the same `client_id` to 10 Picos, they will enter a "fighting" loop where they constantly kick each other off the server. Always use `unique_id()`.
*   **Blocking Sleep**: `time.sleep(5)` prevents the Pico from receiving messages for 5 seconds. In high-speed apps, this is fatal. Use Project 1542 for the fix.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1542**: Learn how to use **AsyncIO** to blink LEDs and listen for MQTT messages simultaneously with zero lag.

---

## 1. Project 1542: Asynchronous Professional MQTT IoT Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate communication lag and ensure instant device reactivity by implementing an **Asynchronous MQTT Polling Loop**, allowing your Pico W to process network messages and local sensor logic in parallel.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Coroutines (`async def`)**: Specialized functions that can "pause" their execution to let other code run, preventing the network from freezing your application.
*   **Non-Blocking Polling**: Repeatedly checking "Do I have a message?" every few milliseconds without stopping the rest of the system.
*   **Task Scheduling**: Grouping the Blink Task and the MQTT Task together so they appear to run simultaneously on a single CPU core.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Concurrent Task Management |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Async, drag `define_async_task`** (Create coroutine)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_check_msg_nonblocking`** (Poll network)
*   **from Async, drag `async_sleep_ms`** (Yield processor)
*   **from Async, drag `run_parallel`** (Gather tasks)

### 7. Variables
*   **mqtt_client**: Object (The active connection).
*   **is_running**: Boolean (Operational state flag).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Task Construction Phase**
1.  **Define the Networking Task**:
    *   Create an `async def mqtt_loop()`.
    *   Inside a `while True:` loop, **snap** the `client.check_msg()` block.
    *   Add an `await asyncio.sleep_ms(10)` to prevent the task from hogging the CPU.
2.  **Define the UI Task**:
    *   Create an `async def led_blink()`.
    *   **Snap** a logic block to toggle an LED every 500ms using `await asyncio.sleep(0.5)`.

**B. Initialization Phase**
3.  **Boot the System**:
    *   In the `async def main()` function, initialize the WiFi and the MQTT client as in Project 1541.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
4.  **Launch the Parallel Engine**:
    *   **Snap** an `asyncio.create_task()` for both the networking and UI functions.
    *   Call `asyncio.run(main())` to start the non-blocking execution.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W boots and connects. The AsyncIO scheduler takes control.
2.  **Process**: The LED starts blinking perfectly every 0.5s.
3.  **Action**: Simultaneously, the Pico checks the network buffer 100 times per second.
4.  **Result**: If you send an "OFF" command via MQTT, the Pico sees it instantly and reacts, even if the LED was in the middle of its "on" cycle.
5.  **Output**: "Async Loop Active | Command Latency: <15ms".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient
import machine
import time

# --- ASYNC MQTT MONITOR ---
async def mqtt_monitor(client):
    print("[TASK] MQTT Polling started.")
    while True:
        try:
            # check_msg() handles incoming data and triggers callbacks
            client.check_msg()
        except OSError:
            pass # No data ready on socket
            
        # Yield for 10ms to allow other tasks to run
        await asyncio.sleep_ms(10)

# --- ASYNC LED BLINKER ---
async def status_led():
    led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)
    print("[TASK] LED Blinker started.")
    while True:
        led.toggle()
        await asyncio.sleep(0.5)

# --- CALLBACK ---
def on_cmd(topic, msg):
    print(f"\n[REMOTE] Action Requested: {msg.decode()}")

async def main():
    # 1. WIFI SETUP
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
    while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(0.5)

    # 2. MQTT SETUP
    client = MQTTClient("pico_async_01", "test.mosquitto.org")
    client.set_callback(on_cmd)
    client.connect()
    client.subscribe(b"pico/remote/toggle")
    
    # Set socket to non-blocking so check_msg() doesn't freeze the loop
    client.sock.setblocking(False)

    # 3. SCHEDULE PARALLEL TASKS
    print("[SYS] Launching concurrent engine...")
    await asyncio.gather(
        mqtt_monitor(client),
        status_led()
    )

# Execution
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    print("[SYS] Application Terminated.")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Forgetting `setblocking(False)`**: Without this, the very first time the Pico checks for a message and finds nothing, it will stop everything and wait forever for a packet, killing your LED blinker.
*   **Standard `time.sleep`**: Never use `time.sleep()` inside an `async def` function. It blocks the entire CPU and ruins the concurrency. Only use `await asyncio.sleep()`.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1543**: What happens if the WiFi router reboots? Learn how to wrap this logic in a **Robust Auto-Reconnect Class**.

## 1. Project 1543: Object-Oriented Professional MQTT IoT Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Build industrial-grade resilience by encapsulating MQTT logic into a **Self-Healing SDK Class**, capable of detecting connection drops and automatically restoring data pipelines without manual intervention.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Stateful Classes**: Storing the `is_connected` status within an object to prevent the program from trying to send data when the network is down.
*   **Automatic Resubscription**: The critical step of re-requesting data channels (`subscribe`) after a connection drop, as brokers clear session data for non-persistent clients.
*   **Abstraction Layer**: Simplifying complex network code into easy commands like `pico.send_data()` for the end-user.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Fault-Tolerant OOP Logic |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `define_class`** (Structure the manager)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_reconnect_pattern`** (Self-healing logic)
*   **from IoT, drag `resubscribe_all_topics`** (Restore channels)
*   **from Logic, drag `try_finally_close`** (Resource safety)

### 7. Variables
*   **is_online**: Boolean (Internal health check).
*   **retry_delay**: Integer (Seconds to wait between reconnect attempts).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Class Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define the Manager**:
    *   Create a class `IndustrialMQTT`.
    *   In `__init__`, initialize the standard `MQTTClient` and set `self.is_online = False`.

**B. Self-Healing Phase**
2.  **Define the Reconnector**:
    *   Create an `async def monitor_connection()`.
    *   **Snap** a loop that checks: `if not self.is_online: try connect()`.
    *   **IMPORTANT**: After every successful `connect()`, you MUST call `subscribe()` for every topic, or the Pico will remain "silent" even though it's online.

**C. Application Interface Phase**
3.  **Define the Public API**:
    *   Create a `send_safe(topic, msg)` method.
    *   **Snap** an `if self.is_online:` check. If False, log "Offline: Data Queued" instead of letting the program crash.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W starts up and use the `IndustrialMQTT` class to go online.
2.  **Process**: Everything runs smoothly until the WiFi router performs a scheduled reboot (5 minutes offline).
3.  **Action**: The Pico detects the `OSError` (Network Unreachable). It sets `is_online = False` and begins a quiet retry loop in the background.
4.  **Result**: Once the router is back, the Pico detects it, reconnects, resubscribes to its control channels, and resumes operations automatically.
5.  **Output**: "[MQTT] Link Dropped... [MQTT] Link Restored | Subscriptions Rebuilt".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient
import machine

class RobustMQTT:
    def __init__(self, client_id, agent_broker):
        self.client_id = client_id
        self.broker = agent_broker
        self.client = MQTTClient(client_id, agent_broker)
        self.is_online = False
        self.topics = [] # Store topics to resubscribe after crash

    def set_callback(self, func):
        self.client.set_callback(func)

    def add_subscription(self, topic):
        self.topics.append(topic)
        if self.is_online:
            self.client.subscribe(topic)

    async def connect_loop(self):
        while True:
            if not self.is_online:
                try:
                    print(f"[RECONNECT] Accessing {self.broker}...")
                    self.client.connect()
                    # CRITICAL: RESTORE SUBSCRIPTIONS
                    for t in self.topics:
                        self.client.subscribe(t)
                    self.client.sock.setblocking(False)
                    self.is_online = True
                    print("[RECONNECT] Success. Pipeline active.")
                except Exception as e:
                    print(f"[RECONNECT] Refusal: {e}")
                    await asyncio.sleep(5) # Wait before retry
            await asyncio.sleep(1)

    async def run_polling(self):
        while True:
            if self.is_online:
                try:
                    self.client.check_msg()
                except Exception:
                    print("[SYS] Link lost during poll.")
                    self.is_online = False
            await asyncio.sleep_ms(50)

    def publish_safe(self, topic, msg):
        if self.is_online:
            try:
                self.client.publish(topic, msg)
                return True
            except:
                self.is_online = False
        return False

# --- UI LOGIC ---
async def main():
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
    while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(1)

    # Initialize SDK
    iot = RobustMQTT("Pico_Indust_01", "test.mosquitto.org")
    iot.add_subscription(b"factory/pico/stop")
    
    # Launch background self-healing tasks
    asyncio.create_task(iot.connect_loop())
    asyncio.create_task(iot.run_polling())

    while True:
        iot.publish_safe(b"factory/pico/heartbeat", b"OK")
        await asyncio.sleep(10)

asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Silent" Reconnect**: Many developers successfully reconnect the socket but forget to call `subscribe()`. The device stays online but never receives another command, appearing "dead" to the central controller.
*   **Unbounded Retries**: If you don't add an `await asyncio.sleep(5)` in the reconnect loop, the Pico will try to connect 1000 times a second, which might get its IP address permanently banned by the broker.

*   **Project 1544**: What if the Pico is smashed and can't say goodbye? Implement the **Last Will & Testament** to handle sudden death alerts.

## 1. Project 1544: Robust Professional MQTT IoT Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
Ensure your IoT network always displays accurate device status by implementing the **Last Will and Testament (LWT)** and **Retained Messages** features, allowing the broker to report your device's "Sudden Death" automatically.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Last Will and Testament (LWT)**: A final message stored on the broker *before* connection, which the broker automatically publishes to a specific topic if the device disconnects unexpectedly (e.g., battery failure).
*   **Retained Messages**: Messages that the broker "holds onto" and delivers to any *new* client that joins the topic, ensuring they immediately know the last known state.
*   **Graceful vs. Ungraceful Disconnect**: The difference between a planned shutdown (calling `.disconnect()`) and a sudden crash.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Mission-Critical Lifecycle |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_set_last_will`** (Configure death alert)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_publish_retained`** (Sticky status)
*   **from Network, drag `init_mqtt_v311`** (Standard driver)
*   **from Control, drag `on_shutdown_event`** (Graceful exit)

### 7. Variables
*   **will_topic**: String (The "Death Notification" channel e.g., `house/pico/status`).
*   **will_msg**: String (The message to be sent: `OFFLINE`).
*   **status_online**: String (The message sent when alive: `ONLINE`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Configuration Phase**
1.  **Prepare the Will**:
    *   Initialize the `MQTTClient`.
    *   **Snap** the `.set_last_will()` block **BEFORE** calling `.connect()`.
2.  **Set Parameters**:
    *   Target Topic: `pico/status`.
    *   Message: `DEAD`.
    *   Retain: `True` (so new dashboards see the device is dead).

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Announce Life**:
    *   Immediately after `.connect()`, **snap** a `.publish()` block.
    *   Topic: `pico/status`.
    *   Message: `ALIVE`.
    *   Retain: `True`.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
4.  **The Silent Watcher**:
    *   Run the main loop. The broker is now "watching" the connection.
5.  **Shutdown Logic**:
    *   If you power off the Pico normally, **snap** a `client.disconnect()` block. This tells the broker: "Don't send the Will, I'm just leaving normally."

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W connects and tells the broker: "If I stop talking, tell everyone I'm DEAD."
2.  **Process**: Pico publishes "ALIVE". Your dashboard turns green.
3.  **Action**: You pull the USB cable out of the Pico (Simulating a crash). 
4.  **Result**: The broker notices the connection is gone. It instantly publishes "DEAD" to the status topic.
5.  **Output**: Your dashboard turns red automatically, even though the Pico is off and can't send anything.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient
import machine

# --- CONFIG ---
CLIENT_ID = "Pico_Safety_System"
BROKER = "test.mosquitto.org"
STATUS_TOPIC = b"pico/industrial/status"

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# 1. INITIALIZE CLIENT
client = MQTTClient(CLIENT_ID, BROKER)

# 2. THE LAST WILL (Must be set BEFORE connecting)
# retain=True ensures that anyone who subscribes LATER will still see "DEAD"
client.set_last_will(STATUS_TOPIC, b"DEAD", retain=True, qos=1)

try:
    # 3. CONNECT
    print("[SYS] Connecting with Last Will protection...")
    client.connect()
    
    # 4. ANNOUNCE ONLINE STATUS
    # We must explicitly say we are ALIVE now to overwrite the "DEAD" message
    client.publish(STATUS_TOPIC, b"ALIVE", retain=True, qos=1)
    print("[SYS] Online and Monitoring.")

    while True:
        # Keep connection alive (Ping the broker)
        client.ping()
        time.sleep(30)

except Exception as e:
    print(f"[ERR] Link failure: {e}")

finally:
    # If the code reaches here cleanly, we disconnect properly
    # This prevents the broker from sending the "DEAD" message
    print("[SYS] Graceful shutdown.")
    client.publish(STATUS_TOPIC, b"DISCONNECTED", retain=True)
    client.disconnect()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Order of Operations**: Setting the Last Will *after* `connect()` is the most common error. The broker needs to know the "Will" while the handshake is happening, otherwise, it handles it as a standard connection without a legacy.
*   **Missing Retain**: If you don't set `retain=True`, a dashboard that opens *after* the Pico dies will just see a blank screen instead of the "DEAD" warning.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1545**: Use **Quality of Service (QoS)** to guarantee that your critical messages actually reach the server in noisy environments.

## 1. Project 1545: Professional MQTT IoT with Error Handling

### 2. Learning Objective
Guarantee delivery of mission-critical data in unstable environments by implementing **Quality of Service (QoS) Level 1** handshaking and explicit error trapping for network timeouts.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **QoS 0 (At Most Once)**: "Fire and Forget". The message is sent once; if it's lost, it's gone.
*   **QoS 1 (At Least Once)**: "Handshake". The Pico sends the message and waits for a "PUBACK" (Acknowledgment) from the broker. If it doesn't get one, it sends it again.
*   **Message ID Tracking**: An internal counter used to match acknowledgments to specific sent packets.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Reliability Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_publish_qos1`** (Guaranteed delivery)
*   **from Logic, drag `try_except_oserror`** (Handle dropouts)
*   **from Network, drag `check_is_broker_alive`** (Status verification)
*   **from Data, drag `increment_msg_id`** (Packet tracking)

### 7. Variables
*   **is_confirmed**: Boolean (Success flag).
*   **retry_limit**: Integer (Attempts before giving up).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Select QoS Strategy**:
    *   For speed (e.g., streaming temperature), use QoS 0.
    *   For critical alerts (e.g., "Fire Detected"), use QoS 1.

**B. Transmission Phase**
2.  **The Handshake Protocol**:
    *   **Snap** the `client.publish()` block with the parameter `qos=1`.
    *   Wrap this in a `try...except` block. QoS 1 can trigger an error if the internet is so bad the "Success" packet never comes back.
3.  **Trapping Timeouts**:
    *   If an `OSError` occurs during the send, mark the message as "Failed" and save it to a local queue.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
4.  **Confirm Receipt**:
    *   The `umqtt.simple` library blocks until the "PUBACK" arrives when using QoS 1. This ensures your program *knows* the cloud definitely has the data.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico detects a security breach.
2.  **Process**: It attempts to send an "ALARM" packet with `QoS=1`.
3.  **Action**: A cloud server glitch causes the first packet to be lost.
4.  **Result**: The Pico doesn't see the "Success" confirmation, so it automatically republishes the message 1 second later.
5.  **Output**: "Alarm Sent... Waiting for ACK... ACK Received! Mission Success."

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient
import machine

# --- CONFIG ---
BROKER = "test.mosquitto.org"
TOPIC_ALARM = b"pico/factory/alarm"

def send_critical_alert(client, message):
    try:
        print(f"[QoS-1] Transmitting Critical Info: {message}...")
        # qos=1 forces the code to wait for a "PUBACK" from the server
        # This guarantees the server received the message at least once.
        client.publish(TOPIC_ALARM, message.encode(), qos=1)
        print("[SUCCESS] Server acknowledged receipt.")
        return True
    except OSError as e:
        print(f"[FAILURE] Data lost in transit: {e}")
        return False

# Execution
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

client = MQTTClient("Pico_Alert_Unit", BROKER)
try:
    client.connect()
    
    # Simulate a critical event
    if send_critical_alert(client, "INTRUDER_DETECTED"):
        print("Continuing normal operations.")
    else:
        print("CRITICAL: Failed to alert server. Initiating local siren!")
        
    client.disconnect()
except Exception as e:
    print(f"Network is down: {e}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Infinite Blocking**: If the broker is offline and you use QoS 1, your code might freeze while waiting for an acknowledgment that can never come. Always use the Project 1543 "Smart Class" to pre-flight the check.
*   **Payload Flooding**: QoS 1 takes much more network bandwidth than QoS 0. Don't use it for data you send every 100ms (like live sensor graphs) or you will lag your network.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1546**: Now that communication is reliable, optimize it by implementation **Topic Filtering** to save processing power.

## 1. Project 1546: Optimized Professional MQTT IoT Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize processing efficiency and minimize memory usage by implementing **Wildcard Topic Filtering**, allowing a single Pico W to monitor an entire factory floor using one streamlined subscription.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Single-Level Wildcard (`+`)**: A filter that matches any one word in a topic path (e.g., `factory/+/temp` matches `factory/robot1/temp` and `factory/robot2/temp`).
*   **Multi-Level Wildcard (`#`)**: A filter that matches everything from that point onwards (e.g., `factory/#`).
*   **Dynamic Command Routing**: Writing code that looks at the `topic` variable in the callback to decide which component to control, rather than writing 10 separate handlers.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Logic Optimization |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_subscribe_wildcard`** (Mass-monitor)
*   **from Text, drag `split_string_by_slash`** (Identify source)
*   **from Logic, drag `route_callback_dynamic`** (Smart handling)
*   **from Data, drag `dictionary_dispatch`** (Clean routing)

### 7. Variables
*   **topic_parts**: List (The broken-down path segments: `['factory', 'robot1', 'temp']`).
*   **source_id**: String (The specific machine sending the command).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Cast the Wide Net**:
    *   Instead of subscribing to 5 topics, **snap** the `client.subscribe("factory/pico/+")` block.
    *   This will catch every command sent to any sub-folder of `pico`.

**B. Processing Phase**
2.  **Break Down the Path**:
    *   In the `on_message` callback, the `topic` variable contains the full path (e.g., `factory/pico/led`).
    *   **Snap** a `topic.decode().split('/')` block to turn this into a list.
3.  **Identify the Target**:
    *   Look at index `2` of the resulting list to see which specific part of the Pico is being addressed (e.g., "led" or "motor").

**C. Maintenance Phase**
4.  **Execute via Map**:
    *   Use an `if...elif` or a dictionary to route the message to the correct hardware function based on the extracted word.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W subscribes to `pico/control/#`.
2.  **Process**: A user sends a message to `pico/control/led` with "ON".
3.  **Action**: The callback receives the message. It "splits" the path and finds the word "led" at the end.
4.  **Result**: It immediately triggers the LED hardware without needing a specific "LED subscription".
5.  **Output**: "Wildcard Match: pico/control/led | Routing to PIN_LED".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient
import machine

# --- CALLBACK ---
def dynamic_routing(topic, msg):
    t_str = topic.decode()
    m_str = msg.decode()
    
    # SPLIT LOGIC: Extract the specific command target
    # e.g. "pico/123/relay" -> ["pico", "123", "relay"]
    parts = t_str.split('/')
    if len(parts) < 3: return
    
    target = parts[2]
    print(f"\n[ROUTER] Target: {target} | Val: {m_str}")

    # ACTION DISPATCH
    if target == "led":
        led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)
        led.value(1 if m_str == "ON" else 0)
    elif target == "reset":
        machine.reset()

# --- EXECUTION ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

client = MQTTClient("Pico_Smart_Router", "test.mosquitto.org")
client.set_callback(dynamic_routing)
client.connect()

# THE OPTIMIZATION: Subscribing to everything under 'pico/control/'
# with a single line of code.
client.subscribe(b"pico/control/+")
print("[SYS] Monitoring ALL control sub-topics...")

try:
    while True:
        client.wait_msg() # Efficient blocking wait
except:
    client.disconnect()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Over-Broad Wildcards**: Subscribing to `#` (the absolute root) will mean your Pico tries to process *every single message* on the public broker. This will crash your Pico's CPU and RAM instantly. Only use wildcards on your specific prefix.
*   **Index Errors**: Before accessing `parts[2]`, always check `if len(parts) > 2`. If someone sends a message to the root topic, your code will crash trying to find the third word.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1547**: Learn how to implement a **Data Pipeline** that bridges MQTT directly to a **InfluxDB** or **Grafana** dashboard.

### Project 1547: Professional MQTT IoT Data Pipeline
## 1. Project 1547: Professional MQTT IoT Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Synchronize state across multiple devices and cloud dashboards by implementing **Retained Messages**, ensuring any observer (mobile app or server) immediately receives the latest system status upon connecting.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Retention Flag**: A byte in the MQTT header that tells the broker: "Save this specific message until a newer one arrives."
*   **Persistent State**: Storing the "Last Known Good Value" in the cloud broker rather than requiring the Pico to be constantly awake.
*   **Zero-Message Clear**: The protocol method for deleting a retained value by publishing an empty payload to the same topic.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Cloud Synchronization Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_publish_retained`** (Sticky status)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_clear_retained`** (Delete stored state)
*   **from Network, drag `init_mqtt_v311`** (Standard driver)
*   **from Variables, drag `get_last_state`** (Local cache)

### 7. Variables
*   **retained_topic**: String (The channel to synchronize: `factory/state/switch`).
*   **is_retained**: Boolean (`True`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Configure the Sync Channel**:
    *   Identify the topics that represent "State" (e.g., Light ON/OFF) vs "Event" (e.g., Button Pressed).
    *   **IMPORTANT**: Only retain "State" topics.

**B. Transmission Phase**
2.  **Synchronize to Cloud**:
    *   Whenever the hardware state changes (e.g., you toggle a relay), **snap** the `client.publish()` block.
    *   Set the `retain=True` parameter.
3.  **Broadcasting Persistence**:
    *   The broker now holds this value in RAM indefinitely.

**C. Verification Phase**
4.  **Test the Lifecycle**:
    *   Publish "ON" with `retain=True`.
    *   Turn off your Pico.
    *   Open a new MQTT client on your computer.
    *   Observe that it receives "ON" the instant it joins, even though the Pico is dead.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W connects and publishes the current system configuration (e.g., `update_interval: 60`) as a retained message.
2.  **Process**: The Pico enters Deep Sleep for 24 hours.
3.  **Action**: An administrator opens a control panel 12 hours later.
4.  **Result**: The broker immediately gives the admin the configuration, even though the Pico is sleeping.
5.  **Output**: "Topic: config | Msg: 60 [RETAINED]".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient

# --- CONFIG ---
BROKER = "test.mosquitto.org"
TOPIC_CONFIG = b"pico/industrial/config"

# Execution
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

client = MQTTClient("Pico_Data_Manager", BROKER)
client.connect()

# 1. PUBLISH PERSISTENT CONFIGURATION
# This acts as a "Cloud Database" that stays active while the Pico sleeps
print("[SYS] Syncing persistent config...")
client.publish(TOPIC_CONFIG, b'{"mode":"eco", "threshold":22}', retain=True)

# 2. CLEARING DATA (If needed)
# To delete a retained message, send an empty string with retain=True
# client.publish(TOPIC_CONFIG, b"", retain=True)

print("[SYS] Config Cloud-Sync Complete.")
client.disconnect()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Senseless Retention**: Retaining a high-speed temperature stream (`pico/temp`) is a major error. It forces the broker to do a database write for every packet, slowing down the network, and the "retained" value will be out-of-date within seconds anyway.
*   **Zombie State**: If you change topics or forget to clear a retained value, old dashboards might show `Light: ON` for a device that has been deleted or renamed months ago. Use a "Zero-Message Clear" to fix zombie topics.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1548**: Your data is visible to anyone on the web. Learn how to wrap this pipeline in **TLS/SSL Encryption**.
