## 1. Project 1548: Secure Professional MQTT IoT Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Protect industrial assets and user credentials from network sniffing by implementing **MQTTS (Encrypted MQTT)** using Port 8883 and SSL/TLS handshaking for all cloud transmissions.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **SSL/TLS (Transport Layer Security)**: An encryption layer that wraps the MQTT protocol, preventing "Man-in-the-Middle" attacks from reading your data.
*   **Port 8883**: The global standard port for secure MQTT communication (replaces the unencrypted 1883).
*   **SNI (Server Name Indication)**: A technique where the Pico W tells the server exactly which domain it is looking for, mandatory for modern cloud brokers like HiveMQ or AWS IoT.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Military-Grade Encryption |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `init_mqtt_secure`** (Enable SSL/TLS)
*   **from Network, drag `set_ssl_params`** (Configure SNI)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_connect_server_authenticated`** (User/Pass login)
*   **from Time, drag `sync_ntp_clock`** (Handshake requirement)

### 7. Variables
*   **ssl_ctx**: Object (Encryption parameters).
*   **broker_url**: String (e.g., `test.mosquitto.org`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Pre-Flight Phase**
1.  **Clock Sync**:
    *   **CRITICAL**: You MUST call `ntptime.settime()` before connecting securely. If the Pico's date is 1970, the security certificate will be rejected as "Expired".
2.  **Define Security Parameters**:
    *   Create a dictionary `ssl_params` with the key `"server_hostname"` set to your broker's URL.

**B. Initialization Phase**
3.  **Encrypted Construction**:
    *   Initialize `MQTTClient` with `port=8883`.
    *   Add the parameter `ssl=True` and pass your `ssl_params`.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
4.  **Secure Handshake**:
    *   **Snap** the `.connect()` block. Note that this will take 2-3 seconds longer than standard MQTT as the Pico calculates the complex encryption keys.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W connects to WiFi and syncs its internal clock via NTP.
2.  **Process**: It contacts the broker on Port 8883 and requests a secure tunnel.
3.  **Action**: The Pico and Broker exchange certificates and verify each other's identity.
4.  **Result**: An encrypted "Pipe" is established. The Pico sends his private username and password through this pipe.
5.  **Output**: "Secure Link: Port 8883 | Encryption: AES-256 Active".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient
import ntptime

# --- SECURITY CONFIG ---
# Use the official secure port 8883
BROKER = "test.mosquitto.org"
PORT = 8883
CLIENT_ID = "Pico_Security_Node_01"

# WiFi Setup
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# 1. TIME SYNC (Mandatory for SSL/TLS)
print("[SYS] Synchronizing wall clock...")
try:
    ntptime.settime()
except:
    pass # Assume retry if needed

# 2. SSL/TLS PARAMETERS
# SNI is required for identifying the target server during the handshake
ssl_params = {"server_hostname": BROKER}

# 3. INITIALIZE SECURE CLIENT
client = MQTTClient(
    CLIENT_ID, 
    BROKER, 
    port=PORT, 
    ssl=True, 
    ssl_params=ssl_params
)

try:
    print("[MQTTS] Performing Handshake...")
    client.connect()
    print("[MQTTS] Secure Tunnel Verified.")
    
    client.publish(b"pico/secure/data", b"Secret Value")
    client.disconnect()
except Exception as e:
    print(f"[ERR] Security Failure: {e}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ignoring NTP**: This is the #1 reason MQTTS fails. Encryption certificates have "Not Before" and "Not After" dates. If your Pico thinks it's 1970, the certificate won't be valid yet, and the connection will be blocked.
*   **Memory Exhaustion**: SSL/TLS uses significantly more RAM than standard MQTT. Avoid running large loops or complex graphics while performing the MQTTS handshake on a Pico W.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1549**: Learn how to use the **Dual Core** Processor to handle these heavy encryption tasks on Core 1 while your sensors run on Core 0.

---

## 1. Project 1549: Multi-Threaded Professional MQTT IoT

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize system responsiveness and eliminate network-induced jitter by offloading the entire MQTT lifecycle to the **RP2040's Secondary Core (Core 1)**, ensuring real-time hardware loops on Core 0 are never interrupted.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dual-Core Concurrency**: Utilizing both physical processors of the RP2040 to separate "Blocking Network Code" from "Live Hardware Control."
*   **Thread Safety (Mutex/Locks)**: A synchronization mechanism that prevents "Race Conditions" where two cores try to write to the same status variable at the exact same millisecond.
*   **Shared Global State**: A thread-safe global object used as a mailbox between the background "Telemetry Thread" and the foreground "Application Thread."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Dual-Core Orchestration |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `run_on_core1`** (Parallel execution)
*   **from System, drag `init_mutex_lock`** (Prevent race conditions)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_loop_background`** (Network worker)
*   **from Control, drag `with_lock_do`** (Safe data access)

### 7. Variables
*   **net_lock**: Mutex (The traffic light for data access).
*   **sys_state**: Dictionary (Shared repository for sensor/command data).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Core 1 Configuration Phase**
1.  **Define the Worker**:
    *   Create a function `background_worker()`.
    *   Inside, initialize the MQTT client and connect.
2.  **The Background Loop**:
    *   Run an infinite `while True:` loop calling `client.check_msg()`.
    *   **CRITICAL**: When a message arrives, use `with lock:` to safely update the shared system status.

**B. Core 0 Configuration Phase**
3.  **Launch the Processor**:
    *   In the main script, **snap** the `_thread.start_new_thread()` block to wake up Core 1.
4.  **The Application Loop**:
    *   Run your high-speed logic (e.g., motor control).
    *   When you need data from MQTT, use `with lock:` to read the "Mailbox" instantly.

**C. Cleanup Phase**
5.  **Synchronization Verification**:
    *   Observe that even if the network slows down or performs a 5-second SSL handshake, your LED/Motor on Core 0 keeps ticking perfectly without a single pause.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Core 0 connects to WiFi and then spawns a clone of itself on Core 1.
2.  **Process**: Core 0 begins a 100Hz hardware loop (e.g., balancing a drone).
3.  **Action**: Core 1 handles the "heavy lifting" of the MQTT/SSL connection in the background.
4.  **Result**: If a "REBOOT" command arrives via MQTT, Core 1 sets a flag. Core 0 sees this flag on its next 0.01s tick and reacts.
5.  **Output**: "Core 0 Activity: 100.0% Real-Time | Core 1: Network Syncing...".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
import _thread
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient
import machine

# --- GLOBAL SHARED STATE ---
# This dictionary is the "Mailbox" between Core 0 and Core 1
system_status = {"cmd": "STAY", "temp": 0}
# This lock ensures only one core touches the dictionary at a time
mailbox_lock = _thread.allocate_lock()

# --- CORE 1: THE NETWORK WORKER ---
def network_thread():
    try:
        print("[Core 1] Starting MQTT stack...")
        client = MQTTClient("Pico_Background", "test.mosquitto.org")
        
        def on_msg(topic, msg):
            # Safe update of shared state
            with mailbox_lock:
                system_status["cmd"] = msg.decode()
                print(f"[Core 1] New Command Received: {system_status['cmd']}")

        client.set_callback(on_msg)
        client.connect()
        client.subscribe(b"pico/industrial/robot/cmd")
        
        while True:
            client.check_msg()
            time.sleep(0.01) # Yield to prevent overheating
            
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"[Core 1] Network Crash: {e}")

# --- CORE 0: THE REAL-TIME CONTROLLER ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# LAUNCH CORE 1
_thread.start_new_thread(network_thread, ())

print("[Core 0] Control Loop Active (High Precision)")
led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)

while True:
    # 1. READ COMMAND SAFELY
    current_action = ""
    with mailbox_lock:
        current_action = system_status["cmd"]
    
    # 2. REACT INSTANTLY
    if current_action == "BLINK":
        led.toggle()
    elif current_action == "OFF":
        led.value(0)

    # 3. MISSION CRITICAL LOOP (No network lag allowed here!)
    time.sleep(0.05) # Loop at stable 20Hz
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Competing Prints**: If both cores `print()` at the same time, the characters will mix together on your screen. This is a visual glitch only; the code is still working.
*   **Lock Deadlocks**: If Core 0 waits for a lock that Core 1 is holding forever, the whole system freezes. Keep your code inside `with lock:` as short as possible (just reading/writing variables).

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1550**: Learn how to organize these topics into the **Homie 4.0** standard for professional auto-discovery in tools like Home Assistant.

## 1. Project 1550: Industrial Professional MQTT IoT Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
Enable your devices to be "Self-Discoverable" by professional automation platforms (like Home Assistant or OpenHAB) by implementing the **Homie 4.0 Convention**, eliminating the need for manual configuration.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Self-Discovery**: The ability for a central controller to automatically build a user interface based on topics the device publishes upon connection.
*   **Homie Protocol Hierarchy**: A standardized topic structure: `homie / device / node / property`.
*   **Settable Properties**: Explicitly marking which data channels can be overwritten by the user (input) vs those that are read-only (output).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Industrial Interoperability |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_publish_homie_node`** (Describe hardware)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_publish_homie_property`** (Describe sensor)
*   **from IoT, drag `mqtt_set_homie_state`** (Ready signal)
*   **from Text, drag `build_topic_path`** (Standardized naming)

### 7. Variables
*   **device_id**: String (A URL-safe name: `pico-hvac-01`).
*   **node_id**: String (The component: `thermostat`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Discovery Phase**
1.  **Announce the Device**:
    *   Connect and immediately publish your identity.
    *   **Snap** blocks to publish `$name` ("Main Thermostat") and `$homie` ("4.0.0").
2.  **Declare Nodes**:
    *   **Snap** a block to publish `$nodes` ("led,button"). This tells the world what is attached to the Pico.

**B. Attributes Phase**
3.  **Define Properties**:
    *   For the LED, publish `$datatype` ("boolean") and `$settable` ("true"). This tells the dashboard to create a toggle switch, not just a label.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
4.  **Go Ready**:
    *   Once all descriptions are sent, **snap** the block to set `$state` to `"ready"`. The server will now show the device as "Online."
5.  **Listen and Acknowledge**:
    *   Listen to the `/set` topic. When the user clicks the UI, you toggle the LED and publish the result back to the root topic of the property.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W connects and sends 10 initialization messages to the broker.
2.  **Process**: A Home Assistant server sees these messages. It says "I see a new Homie device! It has one Boolean switch called Power."
3.  **Action**: Home Assistant automatically creates a button on the user's phone.
4.  **Result**: The user presses the button. Home Assistant sends a message. The Pico sees it and clicks its relay.
5.  **Output**: "Homie Sync: Structure Sent | State: Ready | Node: led discovered".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
from umqtt.simple import MQTTClient

# --- HOMIE CONFIG ---
D_ID = "pico-controller-99"
BASE = f"homie/{D_ID}"
BROKER = "test.mosquitto.org"

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

client = MQTTClient(D_ID, BROKER)
client.connect()

print("[HOMIE] Announcing Industrial System...")

# 1. Device Core (MUST be retained)
client.publish(f"{BASE}/$homie", "4.0.0", retain=True)
client.publish(f"{BASE}/$name", "Factory Controller", retain=True)
client.publish(f"{BASE}/$state", "init", retain=True)
client.publish(f"{BASE}/$nodes", "switch", retain=True)

# 2. Node Setup
client.publish(f"{BASE}/switch/$name", "Power Relay", retain=True)
client.publish(f"{BASE}/switch/$properties", "toggle", retain=True)

# 3. Property Setup
client.publish(f"{BASE}/switch/toggle/$name", "On/Off Switch", retain=True)
client.publish(f"{BASE}/switch/toggle/$datatype", "boolean", retain=True)
client.publish(f"{BASE}/switch/toggle/$settable", "true", retain=True)

# 4. Finalize
client.publish(f"{BASE}/$state", "ready", retain=True)
print("[HOMIE] System Discovery Active.")

def app_handler(topic, msg):
    print(f"[IOT] External Request: {msg.decode()}")
    # Acknowledge by publishing state back to public topic
    client.publish(f"{BASE}/switch/toggle", msg, retain=True)

client.set_callback(app_handler)
client.subscribe(f"{BASE}/switch/toggle/set")

while True:
    client.check_msg()
    time.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Ready" Race**: If you set `$state` to `ready` *before* you finish publishing the `$properties`, a fast controller might try to control the device before it knows the capabilities, causing an error. Always set `ready` last.
*   **Missing Settable Flag**: If you forget to set `$settable` to `true`, the dashboard will show a read-only sensor value instead of an interactive button.

## Batch 156: Professional Cloud Dashboard (1551-1560)

## 1. Project 1551: Professional Cloud Dashboard Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Transform local sensor readings into global insights by connecting the Pico W to a **Cloud Dashboard (Adafruit IO)** via REST API, enabling remote data visualization from anywhere in the world.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Cloud Data Platform**: A specialized server (like Adafruit IO) designed to ingest, store, and graph IoT telemetry.
*   **Data Feeds**: A unique stream or "channel" in the cloud dedicated to one sensor type (e.g., `room-temperature`).
*   **REST API Integration**: Using standard HTTP POST requests to "Push" JSON-formatted packets to a remote server.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Global Telemetry Interface |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_wifi_sta`** (Connect to internet)
*   **from IoT, drag `http_post_json`** (Push data to cloud)
*   **from Text, drag `create_json_object`** (Format the payload)
*   **from Variables, drag `get_aio_credentials`** (Key and Username)

### 7. Variables
*   **AIO_USERNAME**: String (Your Adafruit profile name).
*   **AIO_KEY**: String (Your private API key from the "My Key" tab).
*   **FEED_ID**: String (The URL-safe name of the feed: `pico-temp`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Cloud Configuration Phase**
1.  **Initialize the Dashboard**:
    *   Log in to `io.adafruit.com`.
    *   Create a new **Feed** titled `pico-temp`.
    *   Click the **"My Key"** icon and securely copy your Username and Active Key.

**B. Initialization Phase**
2.  **Establish Connection**:
    *   **Snap** the WiFi connection block. The Pico W requires internet access to reach the Adafruit servers.

**C. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Construct the Packet**:
    *   Identify the REST endpoint: `https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/{USER}/feeds/{FEED}/data`.
    *   **Snap** the `http_post` block.
    *   Set the Headers to include `X-AIO-Key`.
    *   Set the Body to `{"value": TEMPERATURE_DATA}`.
4.  **Monitor the Response**:
    *   Print the HTTP status code. `200` means success; `401` means your Key is wrong.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W joins the local WiFi network.
2.  **Process**: The Pico reads an internal sensor (e.g., CPU temperature).
3.  **Action**: It wraps the reading in JSON and sends a POST request to Adafruit.
4.  **Result**: Adafruit IO receives the data and updates the live graph on your dashboard.
5.  **Output**: "Cloud Sync: Success | Data: 24.5C | Code: 200".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import urequests
import time

# --- CLOUD CREDENTIALS ---
SSID = "YOUR_WIFI"
PASS = "YOUR_PASS"
AIO_USER = "Your_Username"
AIO_KEY = "aio_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
FEED_ID = "pico-temp"

# 1. CONNECT TO INTERNET
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect(SSID, PASS)
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

def send_to_cloud(data_value):
    # Construct the Adafruit IO REST URL
    url = f"https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/{AIO_USER}/feeds/{FEED_ID}/data"
    
    # Headers required for authentication
    headers = {
        "X-AIO-Key": AIO_KEY,
        "Content-Type": "application/json"
    }
    
    # Payload must be a dictionary mapped to "value"
    payload = {"value": data_value}
    
    print(f"[SYS] Pushing {data_value} to Cloud...")
    try:
        response = urequests.post(url, json=payload, headers=headers)
        print(f"[SYS] Server Status: {response.status_code}")
        response.close() # Always close to free RAM
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"[ERR] Upload Failed: {e}")

# Test the driver
send_to_cloud(25.8)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Forgotten API Key**: If you omit the `X-AIO-Key` header, the server will reject the connection immediately with a `401 Unauthorized` error.
*   **Blocking Main Loop**: `urequests.post` is a blocking action. It takes ~500ms to 2s depending on WiFi speed. If your Pico is controlling a fast robot, the robot will stall during the upload.
*   **RAM Leak**: If you forget to call `response.close()`, the Pico will run out of memory after 10-20 uploads because the old HTTP responses stay in RAM.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1552**: Learn how to convert this blocking driver into an **Asynchronous Generator** so your code keeps running while the data uploads.

## 1. Project 1552: Asynchronous Professional Cloud Dashboard Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate control lag and networking "Hiccups" by implementing a custom **Async HTTP Client**, allowing the Pico W to maintain 100Hz real-time sensor loops while simultaneously uploading telemetry to the cloud.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Non-Blocking Streams**: Using `uasyncio.open_connection` to begin a transmission without waiting for the server to say "Hello" before doing more work.
*   **Manual HTTP Framing**: Building raw `POST` request strings (Headers + Body) manually since library-level `urequests` does not support asyncio streams natively.
*   **Yielding on IO**: Automatically switching the CPU's focus to other tasks (like blinking a light) while waiting for the cloud broker to acknowledge a packet.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Concurrent Execution Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `async_http_post`** (Non-blocking driver)
*   **from System, drag `await_stream_drain`** (Send data in background)
*   **from Time, drag `asyncio_sleep`** (Yield for other tasks)
*   **from Network, drag `open_raw_socket_async`** (Direct stream)

### 7. Variables
*   **reader / writer**: Stream objects (The input/output pipes).
*   **request_body**: String (JSON payload).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Construction Phase**
1.  **Define the Async Task**:
    *   Create an `async def upload_task()` function.
    *   Calculate `Content-Length` of your JSON string.
2.  **Format the Header**:
    *   **Snap** the text blocks to build: `POST /api... HTTP/1.0\r\nHost: {HOST}\r\n...`.

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Open the Pipe**:
    *   **Snap** the `await asyncio.open_connection()` block.
4.  **Flush Background**:
    *   Write the request string and **snap** `await writer.drain()`. This lets the Pico do OTHER things while the WiFi chip sends the data.

**C. Monitoring Phase**
5.  **Multi-Tasking**:
    *   In your `main()` function, use `asyncio.gather()` to run your "Robot Controller" and "Cloud Uploader" in parallel.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **T+0ms**: `fast_loop` toggles a motor.
2.  **T+5ms**: `cloud_loop` starts a connection. It yields (pauses).
3.  **T+10ms**: While the cloud waits for a response, the `fast_loop` toggles the motor again. Zero delay.
4.  **T+500ms**: The cloud response arrives. The Pico finishes the task silently in the background.
5.  **Output**: "Local Loop: 100% Stable | Network: Syncing in background...".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import uasyncio as asyncio
import ujson

# --- CONFIG ---
HOST = "io.adafruit.com"
PATH = "/api/v2/YOUR_USER/feeds/pico-temp/data"
KEY = "aio_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"

async def async_cloud_push(value):
    try:
        # 1. Start Connection (Non-blocking)
        reader, writer = await asyncio.open_connection(HOST, 80)
        
        # 2. Build JSON Body
        body = ujson.dumps({"value": value})
        
        # 3. Create Raw HTTP POST Request
        req = (
            f"POST {PATH} HTTP/1.0\r\n"
            f"Host: {HOST}\r\n"
            f"X-AIO-Key: {KEY}\r\n"
            "Content-Type: application/json\r\n"
            f"Content-Length: {len(body)}\r\n"
            "\r\n"
            f"{body}"
        )
        
        # 4. Stream transmission
        writer.write(req.encode())
        await writer.drain() # Pause THIS task, run others during send
        
        # 5. Get confirmation line
        line = await reader.readline()
        print(f"[ASYNC] Cloud Response: {line.decode().strip()}")
        
        writer.close()
        await writer.wait_closed()
        
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"[ERR] Async Fail: {e}")

async def main():
    # WiFi Init
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
    while not wlan.isconnected(): await asyncio.sleep(0.5)

    # TWO CO-ROUTINES RUNNING SIMULTANEOUSLY
    async def fast_hardware_loop():
        while True:
            # High speed task (e.g. balancing)
            await asyncio.sleep(0.01) 

    async def telemetry_loop():
        while True:
            await async_cloud_push(25.5)
            await asyncio.sleep(10) # Upload every 10s

    print("[SYS] Dual Tasks Active.")
    await asyncio.gather(fast_hardware_loop(), telemetry_loop())

asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing Content-Length**: If you don't calculate the body length exactly, the server will either timeout or ignore your data.
*   **DNS Latency**: The first call to `open_connection` might still "Pause" for a moment while the Pico looks up the IP of Adafruit. For absolute real-time reliability, resolve the IP address once at boot.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1553**: The async logic is powerful but the code is becoming complex. Wrap this entire system into a reusable **Python Class**.

## 1. Project 1553: Object-Oriented Professional Cloud Dashboard Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Build a clean and maintainable IoT SDK by abstracting the cloud communication logic into a reusable **Python Class**, hiding complex URL manipulation and credential handling from the main application script.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **SDK Abstraction**: Creating a high-level "Object" (e.g., `CloudManager`) that lets developers interact with hardware without knowing the underlying protocol details.
*   **Encapsulation**: Protecting sensitive API credentials (Username/Key) by storing them as internal "Instance Variables" (`self.key`).
*   **Method Modularity**: Breaking code into reusable functions like `.push_data()` and `.get_status()`.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Object-Oriented Software Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `Define Class`** (Object structure)
*   **from Data, drag `self_instance_variable`** (Scoped data)
*   **from Sensors, drag `read_onboard_temp`** (Data source)
*   **from IoT, drag `http_post_generic`** (Internal logic)

### 7. Variables
*   **aio_instance**: Instance (The living object).
*   **self.base_url**: Property (The root API address).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. The SDK Definition Phase**
1.  **Define the Constructor (`__init__`)**:
    *   Create a class `AdafruitIO`.
    *   Initialize it with `username` and `key` arguments.
2.  **Define the Service Method**:
    *   Create a function `send()` inside the class.
    *   Have it take `feed_id` and `value` as parameters.

**B. Usage Phase**
3.  **Instantiate the Object**:
    *   **Snap** the variable `my_cloud = AdafruitIO("user", "key")` at the TOP of your script.
4.  **Execute Commands**:
    *   In the loop, simply **snap** `my_cloud.send("temp", 25)`.
    *   Observe how the main code is now much easier to read.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W defines the blueprint for the `AdafruitIO` class in RAM.
2.  **Process**: An instance of that class is created, "Pre-loading" the API keys and URL structures.
3.  **Action**: The main loop calls the `.send()` method. The class internally formats the JSON and performs the HTTP request.
4.  **Result**: Developers can easily swap dashboards (e.g., from Adafruit to Blynk) by just changing the class definition, not the whole script.
5.  **Output**: "[FEED: temp] Data Routed to Cloud via instance: Node_01".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import urequests
import network
import time

class AdafruitIO:
    """A professional SDK class for Cloud Telemetry"""
    def __init__(self, username, key):
        self.user = username
        self.key = key
        # Root URL for the API V2
        self.root = f"https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/{username}/feeds"

    def push(self, feed_key, data):
        url = f"{self.root}/{feed_key}/data"
        head = {
            "X-AIO-Key": self.key, 
            "Content-Type": "application/json"
        }
        packet = {"value": data}
        
        try:
            print(f"[SDK] Sending {data} to channel: {feed_key}")
            res = urequests.post(url, json=packet, headers=head)
            status = res.status_code
            res.close()
            return status == 200 or status == 201
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[SDK ERR] Connection Failure: {e}")
            return False

# --- APPLICATION CODE ---
# Setup WiFi
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# CREATE THE CLOUD INTERFACE ONCE
cloud = AdafruitIO(username="MyProfUser", key="aio_secret_key_123")

# USE THE CLEAN INTERFACE
while True:
    # Notice we no longer deal with URLs or Headers here!
    cloud.push("office-temp", 22.5)
    time.sleep(10)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Recursive Instantiation**: Creating a `new AdafruitIO()` object inside an infinite loop. This will cause the Pico W to crash within seconds as it fills memory with thousands of identical class copies. Always instantiate **once** at the start.
*   **Hardcoding Constants**: Putting your API key inside the `push()` function. It should always be passed during `__init__` for security and modularity.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1554: Robust Professional Cloud Dashboard Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
Ensure consistent and reliable communication with industrial cloud platforms by implementing a **Token-Bucket Rate Limiter**, preventing the Pico W from being banned due to high-frequency data bursts.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **API Throttling**: The process by which cloud servers (like Adafruit IO) limit the number of requests per minute (e.g., 30 ppm on Free Tier).
*   **Cooldown Timers**: A logic gate that tracks the "Time Since Last Upload" and blocks any premature transmission attempts.
*   **Graceful Degradation**: Modifying the program's behavior to handle limits (e.g., ignoring extra data) instead of crashing or being blocked by the server.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Protocol Enforcement Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `get_ticks_ms`** (Timestamping)
*   **from Math, drag `subtract_ticks`** (Calculate gap)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_greater_than`** (Limiter gate)
*   **from IoT, drag `http_post_authorized`** (Restricted action)

### 7. Variables
*   **last_tx**: Timestamp (ms since boot).
*   **min_interval**: Integer (e.g., 2000ms for 30 requests/min).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Configuration Phase**
1.  **Define the Speed Limit**:
    *   Set a variable `cooldown_ms` to `2000`. This ensures 2 seconds between every upload.
2.  **Initialize the Tracker**:
    *   Set `last_upload_time = 0` at the very start of your project.

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Check the Gate**:
    *   Before every `post` request, **snap** an `if` block.
    *   Condition: `(Current Time - last_upload_time) > cooldown_ms`.
4.  **Execute or Drop**:
    *   If the condition is met: **Snap** the upload block and update `last_upload_time` to the current time.
    *   Else: Print "Throttled: Please Wait" and continue the loop.

**C. Monitoring Phase**
5.  **Simulate Stress**:
    *   Run a loop that tries to send data every 100ms. Observe that the Pico only actually broadcasts every 2 seconds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico reads a button press and tries to upload. It succeeds because `last_tx` is 0.
2.  **Process**: The user mashes the button 10 times in 1 second.
3.  **Action**: The Pico checks the timer for each press. It sees that only 100ms has passed since the first one.
4.  **Result**: The Pico ignores the 9 extra presses, protecting your API key from being banned.
5.  **Output**: "Burst Detected | Status: 1 sent, 9 dropped | API Health: OK".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import time
import network
import urequests

class ThrottledCloud:
    def __init__(self, key, limit_sec=2.0):
        self.key = key
        self.interval = int(limit_sec * 1000)
        self.last_tx = 0
        self.url = "https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/USER/feeds/pico/data"

    def safe_push(self, val):
        now = time.ticks_ms()
        # Calculate time elapsed
        if time.ticks_diff(now, self.last_tx) < self.interval:
            print("[THROTTLE] Too fast! Dropping packet.")
            return False
        
        # If cooldown is over:
        try:
            h = {"X-AIO-Key": self.key, "Content-Type": "application/json"}
            res = urequests.post(self.url, json={"value": val}, headers=h)
            res.close()
            self.last_tx = time.ticks_ms()
            print(f"[CLOUD] Success: {val}")
            return True
        except:
            return False

# --- MAIN ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

cloud = ThrottledCloud("aio_xxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx", limit_sec=3.0)

# Simulate a rapid-fire sensor
for i in range(5):
    print(f"Reading {i}...")
    cloud.safe_push(i)
    time.sleep(1) # This is faster than the 3s limit!
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking With `time.sleep`**: Beginners often use `time.sleep(2)` to enforce rate limits. This is a mistake because it stops your WHOLE program. Use `time.ticks_ms()` to check the time without stopping.
*   **Ignoring Failures**: If the WiFi drops, the rate limiter doesn't care. You should only update `last_tx` if the `urequests.post` was actually successful.

            
            # --- STATUS ANALYSIS ---
            code = res.status_code
            res.close()
            
            if code == 200 or code == 201:
                print(f"[OK] Sent: {val}")
                self.cooldown = 2000 # Return to normal speed
            elif code == 429:
                print("[ALERT] Rate Limited! Backing off for 60s...")
                self.cooldown = 60000 # Heavy backoff
            elif code >= 500:
                print("[ERR] Cloud maintenance. Waiting 10s...")
                self.cooldown = 10000 # Safety wait
                
            self.last_tx = time.ticks_ms()
            return True
        except:
            print("[ERR] Network Down.")
            self.cooldown = 5000 # Retry after 5s
            return False

# Execution logic
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

cloud = ResilientCloud("aio_xxxxxx")
while True:
    # Attempt to send frequently
    cloud.push_smart(25)
    time.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Infinite Retries**: retrying a `500` error instantly is a major mistake. If the server is down, trying 100 times a second only makes it worse. Always add a delay.
*   **Forgetting `res.close()`**: When handling multiple error conditions, make sure EVERY branch of your `if/else` logic eventually closes the response object, or you will run out of RAM.

## 1. Project 1556: Optimized Professional Cloud Dashboard Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize battery life and minimize cloud data costs by implementing **Delta Compression (Deadband)**, ensuring the Pico W only transmits data when it identifies a significant change in the environment.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Deadband**: A "Range of Silence" where small sensor wobbles (e.g., +/- 0.1 degree) are ignored.
*   **Delta-Triggered Transmission**: A strategy where an upload is only fired if `abs(New_Value - Last_Sent_Value) > Threshold`.
*   **Keep-Alive Heartbeat**: A safety timer that forces an upload every hour even if the value hasn't changed, proving the Pico is still alive.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Bandwidth Optimization Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `abs`** (Difference calculation)
*   **from Logic, drag `greater_than_threshold`** (Trigger logic)
*   **from Time, drag `get_last_sync_time`** (Heartbeat check)
*   **from Variables, drag `cached_last_value`** (History tracking)

### 7. Variables
*   **deadband_limit**: Float (The sensitivity: `0.5`).
*   **last_sync_val**: Float (The value stored in the cloud).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Set the Sensitivity**:
    *   Initialize `threshold = 0.5`. This means temp must change by half a degree to matter.
2.  **Define the Heartbeat**:
    *   Set `heartbeat_ms = 3600000` (1 hour).

**B. Decision Phase**
3.  **The Comparison**:
    *   Inside your loop, read `current_temp`.
    *   **Snap** a block: `if abs(current_temp - last_sent_temp) >= threshold`.
4.  **Determine Urgency**:
    *   Also check if `(Current Time - last_sent_time) > heartbeat_ms`.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Synchronization**:
    *   Only if one of the above is True: **Snap** the upload block.
    *   **CRITICAL**: Update `last_sent_temp = current_temp` immediately after a successful upload.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Read 20.0C**: Initial value. Pico uploads it to the cloud.
2.  **Read 20.2C**: Delta is 0.2. This is less than 0.5. Pico stays silent.
3.  **Read 20.1C**: Pico stays silent.
4.  **Read 20.6C**: Delta is 0.6 (compared to last SENT 20.0). Trigger!
5.  **Output**: "Efficiency: 75% Bandwidth Saved | Total Uploads: 2 in 10 minutes".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import time
import network
import urequests

class OptimizedCloud:
    def __init__(self, key, threshold=0.5):
        self.key = key
        self.url = "https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/USER/feeds/pico/data"
        self.threshold = threshold
        self.last_val = -999 # Force first run
        self.last_time = 0
        self.HEARTBEAT = 60000 # 1 minute for demo

    def update(self, val):
        now = time.ticks_ms()
        delta = abs(val - self.last_val)
        
        # LOGIC: Send if change is big OR if it's been too long since last update
        if delta >= self.threshold or time.ticks_diff(now, self.last_time) > self.HEARTBEAT:
            try:
                print(f"[SYNC] Change/Heartbeat Detected. Value: {val}")
                h = {"X-AIO-Key": self.key, "Content-Type": "application/json"}
                res = urequests.post(self.url, json={"value": val}, headers=h)
                res.close()
                
                # Update history
                self.last_val = val
                self.last_time = now
                return True
            except:
                return False
        else:
            print(f"[SKIP] Value {val} within deadband.")
            return False

# Setup
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

cloud = OptimizedCloud("aio_xxxxxx", threshold=1.0)

# Simulate temperature wobbling
for temp in [20.0, 20.2, 20.8, 21.5, 21.4, 21.3]:
    cloud.update(temp)
    time.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Comparing to Previous Read**: A common error is comparing `New_Value` to `Just_Read_Value`. You must always compare to `Last_SENT_Value`. If the temp rises 0.1 every minute, it will never trigger if you compare to the previous read, but it WILL trigger if you compare to the last value the cloud actually saw.
*   **Missing absolute value**: If you forget `abs()`, a drop in temperature might result in a negative delta that never triggers your "Greater than 0.5" logic.

## 1. Project 1557: Professional Cloud Dashboard Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize battery survival for remote deployments by implementing **Local Data Buffering**, allowing the Pico W to collect multiple readings offline and transmit them in a single high-efficiency burst.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sequential Buffering**: Storing sensor readings in a Python List (Array) while the power-hungry WiFi radio is completely turned off.
*   **Flush Logic**: A trigger condition (e.g., "Buffer is full") that initiates the connection and transmission phase.
*   **Duty Cycle Optimization**: Minimizing the time the radio is "Active" to reduce average current consumption.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Offline Data Accumulator |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Variables, drag `append_to_list`** (Buffer data)
*   **from Lists, drag `length_of_list`** (Threshold check)
*   **from Network, drag `deactivate_wifi`** (Power saving)
*   **from Control, drag `for_each_item_in_list`** (Flush loop)

### 7. Variables
*   **data_buffer**: List (A collection of integers).
*   **max_buffer_size**: Integer (Number of items before a flush).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Collection Phase**
1.  **Initialize the Store**:
    *   Create an empty list `telemetry_buffer = []` at the top of your script.
2.  **Gather Offline**:
    *   **Snap** the sensor reading block and append it to the list.
    *   Keep the WiFi radio `active(False)` during this process to save mA.

**B. Transmission Phase**
3.  **Check for Fullness**:
    *   **Snap** an `if` block: `if length(telemetry_buffer) >= 10`.
4.  **Initiate Sync**:
    *   Turn on WiFi and connect to the internet.
    *   **Snap** a `for` loop to iterate through the buffer.
    *   Inside the loop, send each item to the cloud.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
5.  **Clean Up**:
    *   Once all items are sent, clear the list `telemetry_buffer = []`.
    *   Disconnect WiFi immediately.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **T=1m**: Pico reads 22C. Radio is OFF. Buffer size: 1.
2.  **T=10m**: Pico reads 24C. Radio is OFF. Buffer size: 10. Trigger!
3.  **Action**: Pico wakes up WiFi, sends all 10 temperature readings in 3 seconds, and goes back to sleep.
4.  **Result**: 90% power reduction compared to keeping WiFi ON.
5.  **Output**: "Burst Sync Active | 10 Samples Uploaded | Deep Sleep Engaging".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import time
import urequests
import random

# --- SETTINGS ---
LIMIT = 5 # Send every 5 readings
URL = "https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/USER/feeds/pico/data"
HEADERS = {"X-AIO-Key": "AIO_KEY", "Content-Type": "application/json"}

buffer = []

def flush_buffer():
    print(f"[WIFI] Activating for {len(buffer)} items...")
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
    wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
    
    # Connection wait
    for _ in range(10): 
        if wlan.isconnected(): break
        time.sleep(1)
    
    if wlan.isconnected():
        for val in buffer:
            try:
                print(f"[TX] Pushing {val}...")
                r = urequests.post(URL, json={"value": val}, headers=HEADERS)
                r.close()
            except:
                print("[ERR] Packet Lost")
        
        # Shutdown
        wlan.disconnect(); wlan.active(False)
        print("[WIFI] Sleeping.")
        return True
    return False

# MAIN LOOP
while True:
    # 1. READ (Offline)
    sensor_val = random.uniform(20.0, 25.0)
    buffer.append(sensor_val)
    print(f"[LOG] Saved {sensor_val:.1f} | Buffer: {len(buffer)}")
    
    # 2. EVALUATE
    if len(buffer) >= LIMIT:
        if flush_buffer():
            buffer = [] # Clear only on success
    
    time.sleep(5) # Gather data every 5s
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Memory Overflow**: If you don't clear the buffer after a successful upload, it will keep growing until the Pico crashes with a `MemoryError`.
*   **Time Discrepancy**: Standard Cloud Dashboards assume "Now" is the time of receiving. If you send 10 points at once, they might appear as a vertical line on the graph. Industrial solutions use "Timestamps" inside the JSON to fix this.

## 1. Project 1558: Secure Professional Cloud Dashboard Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Protect your industrial secrets and private credentials by implementing **Secrets Decoupling**, ensuring that sensitive API keys and WiFi passwords are never hardcoded into the main application.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **.gitignore Strategy**: The technique of excluding configuration files containing private keys from version control (like GitHub).
*   **Module-Based Injection**: Importing configuration data from a separate file (`secrets.py`) to keep `main.py` safe for public sharing.
*   **Environment Abstraction**: Designing code that runs based on "Provided Values" rather than "Hardcoded Strings."

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Credential Management Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `import_module`** (Load external config)
*   **from Data, drag `dict_get_value`** (Retrieve secret)
*   **from Text, drag `mask_string`** (Partial visibility)
*   **from Network, drag `init_wifi_secure`** (Encrypted init)

### 7. Variables
*   **secrets_dict**: Dictionary (Key-value pairs of passwords).
*   **aio_key**: String (Loaded from the dictionary).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Configuration Phase**
1.  **Create the Vault**:
    *   Create a NEW file on your Pico called `secrets.py`.
    *   Define a dictionary: `secrets = {'ssid': 'Home', 'pass': '123456', 'key': 'aio_xxx'}`.
2.  **Declare Security**:
    *   Ensure this file is NEVER shared. If using Git, add `secrets.py` to your `.gitignore`.

**B. Implementation Phase**
3.  **Import the Vault**:
    *   In your `main.py`, **snap** the `import secrets` block.
4.  **Reference Dynamically**:
    *   Replace hardcoded strings with `secrets["ssid"]` and `secrets["key"]`.
    *   Try to print only the first 4 characters of the key: `print(secrets["key"][:4])`.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Test Portability**:
    *   Notice that you can now send your `main.py` to anyone, and it will work for them as long as *they* create their own `secrets.py`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W boots up and looks for `secrets.py` on the internal storage.
2.  **Process**: It loads the WiFi and Cloud passwords into temporary system memory.
3.  **Action**: The Pico joins the network using the loaded credentials.
4.  **Result**: Your source code is clean, professional, and safe to post on the internet.
5.  **Output**: "System Active | Credentials loaded from Vault | WiFi: [MASKED]".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
# --- FILE 1: secrets.py ---
# (Saved separately on the Pico)
secrets = {
    "ssid": "MySafeNetwork",
    "wifi_pass": "P@ssw0rd123",
    "aio_user": "Engineering_Dept",
    "aio_key": "aio_abc123456789def"
}

# --- FILE 2: main.py ---
import network
import time

# 1. ATTEMPT SECURE IMPORT
try:
    from secrets import secrets
    print("[SYS] Secrets loaded successfully.")
except ImportError:
    print("[CRITICAL] secrets.py missing! System Halted.")
    raise SystemExit

# 2. USE SECURE CREDENTIALS
def connect():
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
    # No hardcoded passwords here!
    wlan.connect(secrets["ssid"], secrets["wifi_pass"])
    
    while not wlan.isconnected():
        print("Linking...")
        time.sleep(1)
    
    print(f"Online as: {secrets['aio_user']}")

# 3. MASKED OUTPUT (Best Practice)
masked_key = secrets["aio_key"][:6] + "********"
print(f"Cloud API Key: {masked_key}")

connect()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Git Trap**: Forgetting to add `secrets.py` to `.gitignore` and accidentally pushing your Active Key to a public repository.
*   **Missing file on Pico**: If you forget to upload `secrets.py` to the Pico flash, `main.py` will crash immediately with an `ImportError`. Always check your file list in Thonny.

## 1. Project 1559: Multi-Threaded Professional Cloud Dashboard

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize device responsiveness by offloading the slow, blocking process of **Cloud Command Polling** to the RP2040’s **Core 1**, ensuring the main logic (Core 0) never misses a high-speed sensor event or motor deadline.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Worker Threads**: Using `_thread.start_new_thread` to create a dedicated process for background networking.
*   **Shared Global State**: Using global variables to communicate between the background networking core and the foreground logic core.
*   **Synchronous Polling**: Repeatedly asking the cloud "Any changes?" without stopping the rest of the machine.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Dual-Core Communications |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `run_on_core1`** (Parallel task)
*   **from Logic, drag `global_variable_access`** (Shared state)
*   **from IoT, drag `http_get_latest`** (Fetch command)
*   **from Time, drag `thread_sleep`** (Independent timing)

### 7. Variables
*   **current_command**: String (The order from the dashboard).
*   **is_new**: Boolean (Flag for new data).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Core 1: The Network Worker**
1.  **Define the Worker Task**:
    *   Create a function `listen_for_commands()`.
    *   Identify the URL for fetching the "Latest" data point from your feed.
2.  **The Polling Loop**:
    *   Inside the worker, loop every 2 seconds.
    *   Send a `GET` request and parse the JSON.
    *   **Snap** a block to update the global variable `shared_cmd`.

**B. Core 0: The Manager**
3.  **Active Monitoring**:
    *   In your main `while True` loop, check the value of `shared_cmd`.
4.  **Reaction Phase**:
    *   If `shared_cmd == "ON"`, turn on the LED.
    *   Notice that you don't need `time.sleep` in the main loop to wait for WiFi.

**C. Launch Phase**
5.  **Ignite the Core**:
    *   **Snap** the `_thread.start_new_thread` block BEFORE the main loop starts.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Core 0**: Starts immediately, blinking an LED at 100Hz.
2.  **Core 1**: Joins WiFi and starts polling the cloud every 2 seconds.
3.  **Action**: You click "ABORT" on your phone.
4.  **Result**: Core 1 detects the change. Core 0 sees the updated variable mid-blink and stops instantly.
5.  **Output**: "Core 0: 100fps | Core 1: Syncing Command ID 5541...".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import urequests
import _thread
import time
import machine

# --- SHARED STATE ---
# Global variable accessible by BOTH cores
cloud_command = "OFF"

def networking_core():
    """This runs on the background core (Core 1)"""
    global cloud_command
    url = "https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/USER/feeds/pico-cmd/data/last"
    headers = {"X-AIO-Key": "AIO_KEY"}
    
    print("[THREAD] Network Core Active.")
    while True:
        try:
            # GET is a blocking network call
            res = urequests.get(url, headers=headers)
            if res.status_code == 200:
                data = res.json()
                cloud_command = data["value"] # Update the shared variable
            res.close()
        except:
            print("[THREAD] Cloud Link Dropped")
        
        # Don't hammer the API (respect rate limits)
        time.sleep(2)

# --- BOOT ---
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# LAUNCH CORE 1
_thread.start_new_thread(networking_core, ())

# --- CORE 0 (Main Loop) ---
led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)

print("[MAIN] Logic Core Active.")
while True:
    # Logic Core is fast and never waits for WiFi!
    if cloud_command == "ON":
        led.on()
    else:
        led.off()
    
    # Rapid local processing
    time.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Variable Sync**: If you try to update a complex object (like a list) from one core while the other is reading it, you might get a crash. Use simple strings or integers, or look into `_thread.allocate_lock()`.
*   **Core 1 Crash**: Threads on the Pico don't always print errors nicely. If Core 1 crashes, the main loop will keep running with the "Old" command forever. Always use `try/except` inside your thread.

## 1. Project 1560: Industrial Professional Cloud Dashboard Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
Synthesize all networking, hardware, and concurrency skills to build a **Digital Twin**, creating a perfectly synchronized system where a physical hardware button and a virtual cloud switch act as mirrors of each other.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **State Mirroring**: Ensuring the "Truth" of a variable is consistent across both physical memory and a remote cloud database.
*   **Change Source Attribution**: Labeling an event as `PHYSICAL_TRIGGER` or `CLOUD_TRIGGER` to prevent "Echo Loops" (where a cloud update triggers a physical update which triggers another cloud update).
*   **Bi-Directional Event Synchronization**: Merging input from a background listener thread and a main hardware loop into a single coherent state machine.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Push Button
*   External LED (or Onboard)
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | System Hub |
| **LED** | GP15 | Physical Output |
| **Button** | GP14 | Physical Trigger |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `if_source_not_equal`** (Recursion guard)
*   **from System, drag `spawn_worker_thread`** (Cloud Listener)
*   **from IoT, drag `http_post_sync`** (Status upload)
*   **from Variables, drag `global_sync_flag`** (Loop prevention)

### 7. Variables
*   **system_state**: Boolean (ON/OFF).
*   **last_action**: String (Where the flip came from).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. The Listener Core (Background)**
1.  **Monitor the Cloud**:
    *   In a dedicated thread, check the Adafruit feed every 2 seconds.
2.  **Handle Online Toggles**:
    *   If the cloud value changes: Update the global `shared_state` and set `source = "CLOUD"`.

**B. The Logic Core (Foreground)**
3.  **Monitor the Button**:
    *   If the button is pressed: Toggle the global `shared_state` and set `source = "PHYSICAL"`.
4.  **Hardware Sync**:
    *   Always update the physical LED pin to match `shared_state`.

**C. The Anti-Loop Logic**
5.  **Filtered Upload**:
    *   **Snap** an `if` block: `if source == "PHYSICAL"`.
    *   Upload the new state to the cloud.
    *   If `source == "CLOUD"`, do NOT upload (as the cloud already knows).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Action**: You press the physical button on your desk.
2.  **Local**: The Pico LED turns ON. `source` is set to "PHYSICAL".
3.  **Sync**: The Pico uploads "ON" to Adafruit. Your phone dashboard flips to "ON".
4.  **Action**: You slide the dashboard switch to "OFF" on your phone.
5.  **Sync**: The listener thread sees the "OFF". It turns the Pico LED OFF.
6.  **Safety**: The Pico ignores the upload phase because it knows the change came from the cloud.
7.  **Output**: "Industrial Sync: Perfect | Physical: OFF | Cloud: OFF | Latency: 1.2s".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import urequests
import _thread
import time
import machine

# --- CONFIG & PINS ---
AIO_USER = "YourUser"
AIO_KEY = "aio_xxxx"
led = machine.Pin(15, machine.Pin.OUT)
btn = machine.Pin(14, machine.Pin.IN, machine.Pin.PULL_UP)

# --- GLOBAL SHARED STATE ---
current_state = 0 # 0=OFF, 1=ON
update_requested = False
source = "NONE" # "CLOUD" or "PHYSICAL"

def cloud_listener():
    """Background thread to watch for remote changes"""
    global current_state, update_requested, source
    url = f"https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/{AIO_USER}/feeds/pico-io/data/last"
    headers = {"X-AIO-Key": AIO_KEY}
    last_id = None
    
    while True:
        try:
            res = urequests.get(url, headers=headers)
            if res.status_code == 200:
                data = res.json()
                # Check if the data is NEW (different ID)
                if data["id"] != last_id:
                    new_val = int(data["value"])
                    if new_val != current_state:
                        current_state = new_val
                        source = "CLOUD" # Mark the source
                    last_id = data["id"]
            res.close()
        except: pass
        time.sleep(2)

# WiFi Connect
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
wlan.connect("SSID", "PASS")
while not wlan.isconnected(): pass

# Start Thread
_thread.start_new_thread(cloud_listener, ())

print("[SYS] Industrial Digital Twin Online.")
last_btn_state = 1
while True:
    # 1. Sync Hardware to State
    led.value(current_state)
    
    # 2. Check Physical Button
    if btn.value() == 0 and last_btn_state == 1:
        current_state = 1 - current_state # Toggle
        source = "PHYSICAL"
        time.sleep(0.3) # Debounce
    last_btn_state = btn.value()
    
    # 3. Handle Cloud Upload (Only if changed physically)
    if source == "PHYSICAL":
        print(f"[SYNC] Pushing physical change ({current_state}) to Cloud...")
        try:
            url = f"https://io.adafruit.com/api/v2/{AIO_USER}/feeds/pico-io/data"
            h = {"X-AIO-Key": AIO_KEY, "Content-Type": "application/json"}
            urequests.post(url, json={"value": current_state}, headers=h).close()
            source = "NONE" # Clear source after sync
        except:
            print("[ERR] Sync Failed")
            
    # 4. Handle Cloud Reset
    if source == "CLOUD":
        print("[SYNC] Hardware matched to Cloud.")
        source = "NONE" # Clear source

    time.sleep(0.01)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The Feedback Loop**: If you forget the `source` check, your device will keep uploading its state which triggers a new "Latest" item which triggers another download... this will crash your data quota in minutes.
*   **Debounce Fail**: Without a small `time.sleep(0.3)` or debounce logic, a single button press might toggle the state 5 times, confusing the cloud.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Batch 157**: Now that we have mastered Cloud synchronization, let's explore **Bluetooth Low Energy (BLE)** for low-power, direct device communication.

## Batch 157: Professional Bluetooth (BLE) Beacon (1561-1570)

## 1. Project 1561: Professional Bluetooth (BLE) Beacon Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Enable ultra-low-power local broadcasting by implementing a **Professional BLE Beacon**, allowing the Pico W to transmit its identity and status to nearby smartphones without requiring a password or persistent connection.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **GAP (Generic Access Profile)**: The fundamental BLE layer that defines how devices announce their presence (Advertising) and how they are discovered.
*   **Advertising Payload**: A strictly formatted 31-byte packet that contains identifiers (like a Name) and operational flags.
*   **Broadcaster Role**: A one-way communication mode where the device (Lighthouse) shouts data but does not listen for or accept incoming connections.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | BLE Advertising Hub |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_ble_radio`** (Activate power)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_set_adv_data`** (Format payload)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_start_advertising`** (Begin broadcast)
*   **from Text, drag `encode_to_bytes`** (String conversion)

### 7. Variables
*   **ble_instance**: Object (The hardware controller).
*   **adv_payload**: Bytearray (The 31-byte packet).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Awaken the Radio**:
    *   **Snap** the `bluetooth.BLE()` block and assign it to `ble`.
    *   Set `ble.active(True)` to power up the radio chip.

**B. Configuration Phase**
2.  **Forge the Identity**:
    *   Define the Device Name (e.g., "Pico-Beacon").
    *   **Snap** the `advertising_payload` builder block. This automatically adds the mandatory "GATT Flags" (Discoverable mode).
3.  **Specify the Interval**:
    *   Set the broadcast rate to `100,000` microseconds (100ms). Faster rates make the device easier to find but use more battery.

**C. Transmission Phase**
4.  **Commence Broadcast**:
    *   **Snap** the `ble.gap_advertise` block using your payload.
5.  **Monitor Range**:
    *   Open a scanner app (like nRF Connect) on your phone. Observe the "RSSI" (Signal Strength) as you move away from the Pico.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W initializes the BLE stack and allocates memory for the radio.
2.  **Process**: It encodes "Pico-Beacon" into a binary-friendly byte array.
3.  **Action**: The radio chirps the identity packet every 100 milliseconds.
4.  **Result**: Your smartphone detects the "Pico-Beacon" immediately in the scan list.
5.  **Output**: "BLE Beacon Active | Interval: 100ms | Payload: Discoverable".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import bluetooth
import time

# 1. INITIALIZE RADIO
ble = bluetooth.BLE()
ble.active(True)

def build_payload(name):
    """Manually builds a GAP-compliant advertising packet"""
    payload = bytearray()
    
    # ADV FLAGS: [Length, Type, Value]
    # 0x01 = Flags, 0x06 = General Discoverable
    payload += bytes([0x02, 0x01, 0x06])
    
    # LOCAL NAME: [Length, Type, Value]
    # 0x09 = Complete Local Name
    name_bytes = name.encode()
    payload += bytes([len(name_bytes) + 1, 0x09]) + name_bytes
    
    return payload

# 2. CONFIGURE BEACON
print("Pico W BLE Beacon Starting...")
my_name = "Pico-Elite-01"
adv_data = build_payload(my_name)

# 3. START ADVERTISING
# gap_advertise(interval_us, payload)
ble.gap_advertise(100000, adv_data)

print(f"Broadcasting as: {my_name}")
print("Use nRF Connect app on phone to verify.")

# Stay alive to keep advertising
while True:
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Oversized Name**: BLE advertising packets are limited to 31 bytes total. If your name is too long, the payload builder will crash. Keep names under 20 characters.
*   **Missing Flags**: If you don't include the "Discoverable" flags (`0x01, 0x06`), some smartphones will ignore the beacon entirely for security reasons.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1562**: The `while True` loop is blocking. Learn how to manage BLE using **uasyncio** so you can blink LEDs and read sensors while the radio runs.

## 1. Project 1562: Asynchronous Professional Bluetooth (BLE) Beacon Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate blocking delays and jitter by implementing an **Asynchronous BLE Handler** using the `aioble` library, allowing the Pico W to maintain consistent hardware timing while managing background radio transmissions.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **aioble Library**: The official MicroPython high-level wrapper that transforms complex BLE event callbacks into clean `async/await` syntax.
*   **Advertising Timeout**: A strategy to broadcast for a specific duration (e.g., 5 seconds) and then sleep the radio to preserve battery.
*   **Concurrent Task Management**: Running a "Radio Task" and a "Control Task" simultaneously using the `uasyncio` scheduler.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Async Radio Controller |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `aioble_advertise`** (Non-blocking broadcast)
*   **from System, drag `asyncio_task_spawn`** (Create parallel job)
*   **from Control, drag `try_finally_radio`** (Graceful shutdown)
*   **from Time, drag `async_delay_ms`** (Yield CPU)

### 7. Variables
*   **adv_interval**: Integer (Advertising rate in microseconds).
*   **appearance_code**: Integer (Visual icon for the phone app).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Library Installation**
1.  **Install Dependencies**:
    *   In Thonny: Go to **Tools > Manage Packages**.
    *   Search and install `micropython-aioble`.

**B. Logic Phase**
2.  **Define the Radio Task**:
    *   Create `async def radio_loop()`.
    *   **Snap** the `aioble.advertise` block inside. This block will wait for a connection or run until cancelled.
3.  **Define the Logic Task**:
    *   Create `async def blink_loop()`.
    *   Toggle an LED and **snap** `await asyncio.sleep(0.1)`.

**C. Execution Phase**
4.  **Run Concurrently**:
    *   Use `asyncio.gather()` to launch both loops. 
    *   Observe how the radio transmission does NOT stop the high-speed LED blinking.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **T=0ms**: The Async Scheduler starts. Both tasks are queued.
2.  **T=10ms**: The radio task yields to the LED task.
3.  **T=50ms**: The radio pulses the "I'm Here" signal in the background.
4.  **Result**: You see a perfectly smooth 10Hz blink on the Pico, while your phone sees a stable BLE connection.
5.  **Output**: "Task 1: Blinking | Task 2: Advertising | Status: Synchronized".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import machine

# --- SETTINGS ---
DEVICE_NAME = "Async-Pico-W"
ADV_INTERVAL_US = 250000 # 250ms

async def radio_task():
    """Background task for BLE Advertising"""
    print(f"[BLE] Starting broadcast as {DEVICE_NAME}")
    while True:
        try:
            # aioble.advertise is a coroutine!
            # It yields during the radio wait periods.
            await aioble.advertise(
                ADV_INTERVAL_US,
                name=DEVICE_NAME,
                appearance=0x0340, # 'Generic Outdoor' icon
            )
        except asyncio.CancelledError:
            print("[BLE] Radio Shutdown Requested")
            break
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[BLE] Radio Error: {e}")
            await asyncio.sleep(1)

async def blink_task():
    """Background task for high-speed hardware control"""
    led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)
    while True:
        led.toggle()
        # This sleep yields to the radio_task
        await asyncio.sleep(0.1)

async def main():
    print("[SYS] Launching Dual-Task Environment...")
    # Run both tasks in parallel
    await asyncio.gather(radio_task(), blink_task())

# BOOT
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    print("[SYS] Halted by User")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing `await`**: If you call `aioble.advertise()` without the `await` keyword, it will return a coroutine object immediately without actually starting the radio, and the program will continue into an error state.
*   **Interval < 20ms**: Requesting an advertising interval faster than 20 milliseconds will be rejected by the Pico W hardware, causing an `OSError`.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1563**: Scale this system by building an **Object-Oriented Beacon Class** that can swap between "General Name" and "Web Link" (Eddystone) modes dynamically.

## 1. Project 1563: Object-Oriented Professional Bluetooth (BLE) Beacon Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Construct complex, multi-functional radio packets by building an **Object-Oriented Beacon Manager**, abstracting the low-level Bluetooth TLV (Type-Length-Value) specifications into a user-friendly software interface.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **TLV (Type-Length-Value)**: The universal data format for BLE advertising where every piece of data (Name, UUID, URL) must be prefixed by its total length and its specific type code.
*   **Encapsulation of Protocols**: Hiding the mathematical hexidecimal mappings (e.g., `0x09` for Name) inside class methods like `.add_name()`.
*   **Payload Serialization**: The process of assembling multiple individual data chunks into a single, contiguous bytearray for the radio controller.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Protocol Abstraction Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `Define Class`** (Beacon blueprint)
*   **from Text, drag `get_byte_length`** (Length prefix calculation)
*   **from Lists, drag `extend_bytearray`** (Packet assembly)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_apply_payload`** (Apply to radio)

### 7. Variables
*   **self.payload**: Bytearray (The internal buffer).
*   **type_id**: Byte (The BLE data type).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Class Definition Phase**
1.  **Initialize the Buffer**:
    *   Create a class `BeaconEngine`. In the `__init__` method, create a `self.data = bytearray()`. 
    *   Add the "Flags" chunk immediately (Discoverable mode).
2.  **Create the Helper**:
    *   Define a method `_append(type, data_bytes)`.
    *   Inside: Append `length`, then `type`, then the data.

**B. Feature Phase**
3.  **Implement 'Add Name'**:
    *   Create a method `add_name(text)`. Use the Type ID `0x09`.
4.  **Implement 'Add Service'**:
    *   Create a method `add_uuid16(uuid)`. This allows devices to identify the "Type" of device (e.g., Heart Rate Monitor).

**C. Application Phase**
5.  **Instantiate and Advertise**:
    *   Create an object `my_beacon = BeaconEngine()`.
    *   **Snap** calls to `.add_name()` and `.add_uuid16()`.
    *   Send the output of `.get_bytes()` to the radio.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Logical**: You call `add_name("Pico")`.
2.  **Class Internal**: The class calculates "Pico" is 4 bytes. It appends `5` (Length), `9` (Name Type), and `Pico` (Data) to the buffer.
3.  **Radio**: The radio chip receives the final buffer `[02 01 06 05 09 50 69 63 6F]`.
4.  **Smartphone**: The phone decodes these bytes back into readable "Pico" text.
5.  **Output**: "Class Initialized | Payload Serialized | Radio Transmitting: Standard Name".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import bluetooth
import time

class BeaconEngine:
    """An Elite abstraction for BLE Advertising Packets"""
    def __init__(self):
        self._data = bytearray()
        # Always start with Flags: General Discoverable
        self.add_field(0x01, bytes([0x06]))

    def add_field(self, field_type, field_data):
        """Append a TLV (Type-Length-Value) field to the payload"""
        # Length = Data Length + 1 (for Type byte)
        length = len(field_data) + 1
        self._data.append(length)
        self._data.append(field_type)
        self._data.extend(field_data)

    def add_name(self, name):
        """Add a Complete Local Name (Type 0x09)"""
        self.add_field(0x09, name.encode())

    def add_service_uuid16(self, uuid):
        """Add a 16-bit Service UUID (e.g. 0x180D for Heart Rate)"""
        # Packs integer into 2 bytes (Little Endian)
        uuid_bytes = bytes([uuid & 0xFF, (uuid >> 8) & 0xFF])
        self.add_field(0x03, uuid_bytes)

    def build(self):
        """Return the final bytearray ready for the radio"""
        return self._data

# --- APP LOGIC ---
ble = bluetooth.BLE()
ble.active(True)

# 1. CREATE BUILDER
builder = BeaconEngine()

# 2. CONFIGURE PACKET
builder.add_name("Pico-Elite")
builder.add_service_uuid16(0x181A) # Environmental Sensing Service

# 3. SHOOT
payload = builder.build()
print(f"Generated Raw Bytes: {payload}")
ble.gap_advertise(100000, payload)

while True:
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Mixing 16-bit and 128-bit UUIDs**: You cannot simply "extend" a long service ID onto a short one without changing the Type code (`0x03` vs `0x07`).
*   **Buffer Persistence**: If you use the same class instance to build multiple different beacons, ensure you clear the `self._data` array between builds, or the packets will merge and crash.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1564**: Transmission Power. By default, the Pico shouts at 0dBm (Max). This wastes battery. We can lower it to -20dBm for a "Whisper" beacon that only works when very close.

## 1. Project 1564: Professional BLE Environment Service Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Transition from simple broadcasting to interactive data exchange by implementing a **BLE GATT Service**, allowing a smartphone to proactively "Read" sensor data and receive "Notifications" when environmental conditions change.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **GATT (Generic Attribute Profile)**: The hierarchy that defines the "Services" (containers) and "Characteristics" (data points) of a BLE device.
*   **UUID (Universally Unique Identifier)**: Standard 16-bit or 128-bit codes that label services (e.g., `0x181A` for Environmental Sensing).
*   **Notifications (Push)**: A mechanism where the Pico W automatically sends data to the phone only when a value changes, saving battery on the phone side.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | GATT Server Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_define_service`** (Create container)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_add_characteristic`** (Add data point)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_notify_value`** (Trigger push update)
*   **from Sensors, drag `read_onboard_temp`** (Data source)

### 7. Variables
*   **temp_service**: Service Object (The GATT container).
*   **temp_characteristic**: Characteristic Object (The readable value).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Schema Phase**
1.  **Define the Structure**:
    *   Initialize `aioble`. 
    *   **Snap** a `Service` block with UUID `0x181A`.
2.  **Add the Data Point**:
    *   **Snap** a `Characteristic` block inside the service.
    *   Set properties to `read=True` and `notify=True`.
    *   Set the UUID to `0x2A6E` (Temperature).

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **The Sampling Loop**:
    *   Read the Pico's internal temperature.
    *   **Snap** the `characteristic.write()` block to update the local value.
4.  **The Notification Logic**:
    *   If the temperature changes by >0.1C, **snap** the `characteristic.notify()` block.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Connect and Read**:
    *   Use nRF Connect. Connect to the device.
    *   Expand the "Environmental Sensing" service and click the "Read" (down arrow) icon.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W registers the GATT database with the radio.
2.  **Process**: The radio starts advertising as a "Peripheral" (Connectable).
3.  **Action**: A smartphone connects. The Pico stops advertising and enters a "Session".
4.  **Result**: The phone "Subscribes" to the temperature characteristic. Every time the Pico moves, the phone screen updates instantly.
5.  **Output**: "Service: ESS Active | State: Connected | Client: iPhone_User".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth
import random
import struct

# --- GATT DEFINITION ---
# Environmental Sensing Service (0x181A)
TEMP_SERVICE_UUID = bluetooth.UUID(0x181A)
# Temperature Characteristic (0x2A6E)
TEMP_CHAR_UUID = bluetooth.UUID(0x2A6E)

# 1. SETUP SERVICE & CHARACTERISTIC
temp_service = aioble.Service(TEMP_SERVICE_UUID)
temp_char = aioble.Characteristic(
    temp_service, TEMP_CHAR_UUID, read=True, notify=True
)

def encode_temp(temp):
    """BLE Temperature is usually 100x Celsius in 16-bit signed int"""
    return struct.pack("<h", int(temp * 100))

async def sensor_task():
    """Simulate sensor and update GATT database"""
    current_temp = 25.0
    while True:
        # Create a small drift
        current_temp += random.uniform(-0.1, 0.1)
        print(f"[GATT] Internal Value: {current_temp:.2f}C")
        
        # Update the local database value
        temp_char.write(encode_temp(current_temp))
        
        # Send notification to any connected client
        temp_char.notify(encode_temp(current_temp))
        
        await asyncio.sleep(2)

async def peripheral_task():
    """Manage connections"""
    while True:
        try:
            print("[BLE] Waiting for connection...")
            # Advertise as connectable device
            connection = await aioble.advertise(
                250_000,
                name="Pico-Sensor",
                services=[TEMP_SERVICE_UUID],
                appearance=0x0300, # Thermometer
            )
            print(f"[BLE] Connected to {connection.device}")
            # Wait for disconnection
            await connection.disconnected()
            print("[BLE] Disconnected")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[BLE] Connection error: {e}")

async def main():
    # Register services
    # (In aioble, services are registered when the first one is created)
    await asyncio.gather(sensor_task(), peripheral_task())

# START
asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Properties Mismatch**: Attempting to "Read" a characteristic that was defined with only `notify=True`. Ensure the properties in your code match what you expect the app to do.
*   **Byte Ordering**: Standard BLE uses "Little Endian" (least significant byte first). If you send `0x0A 0x01` instead of `0x01 0x0A`, the temperature will show up as a huge, incorrect number on the phone.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1565**: Protect your sensor data from unauthorized snooping by adding **Secure Pairing** and encryption to the connection.
