## 1. Project 1565: Secure Professional Bluetooth (BLE) Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Secure your IoT infrastructure by implementing **Encrypted Pairing and Bonding**, ensuring only authorized smartphones can view sensitive sensor data or perform control actions on the Pico W.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Pairing**: The exchange of security keys between two devices to encrypt the current session.
*   **Bonding**: Storing those keys in long-term flash memory so the devices recognize each other automatically next time.
*   **Passkey Entry**: A security method (MITM protection) where a 6-digit code must be entered on the phone to confirm the Pico's identity.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Secure Access Point |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_enable_security`** (Encryption mode)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_set_passkey`** (Secret code)
*   **from Control, drag `on_pairing_request`** (Auth event)
*   **from Logic, drag `check_encryption_status`** (Access guard)

### 7. Variables
*   **is_secure**: Boolean (Connection status).
*   **passcode**: Integer (e.g., 123456).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Configuration Phase**
1.  **Set Security Level**:
    *   Configure `aioble` to use **Level 4** security (Authenticated pairing with encryption).
    *   Define a static or temporary passkey: `123456`.

**B. Logic Phase**
2.  **Handle Pairing Events**:
    *   **Snap** the `on_pair` event listener. 
    *   Print "Pairing Successful" only when the phone enters the correct code.
3.  **Encrypted Read Only**:
    *   Modify your characteristic so that it requires an encrypted link to be read.

**C. Verification Phase**
4.  **Confirm the Lock**:
    *   Connect with your phone. 
    *   Observe that the "Environmental Sensing" service is visible, but clicking "Read" triggers a popup asking for a code.
    *   Enter the code to unlock the data.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W advertises as a "Secure Device".
2.  **Handshake**: The phone requests a connection.
3.  **Authentication**: The Pico sends a random 6-digit code to the Serial Monitor.
4.  **Action**: You type that code into the phone’s OS prompt.
5.  **Result**: The link is now encrypted. No hacker with a scanner can "Sniff" the temperature data anymore.
6.  **Output**: "Pairing State: SECURE | Link: Encrypted | Keys: Bonded".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth

# --- CONFIG ---
PASSKEY = "123456"

# Setup high-security characteristic
S_UUID = bluetooth.UUID(0x180D) # Heart Rate (requires security)
C_UUID = bluetooth.UUID(0x2A37)

service = aioble.Service(S_UUID)
char = aioble.Characteristic(
    service, C_UUID, read=True, notify=True
)

async def gatekeeper_task():
    """Manages secure access and pairing"""
    while True:
        try:
            print(f"[SEC] Waiting for secure connection. Passkey is: {PASSKEY}")
            # aioble handles pairing automatically if triggered
            # by a secure characteristic read.
            device = await aioble.advertise(
                250_000,
                name="Secure-Pico",
                services=[S_UUID]
            )
            
            # Manually trigger pairing for demo
            # In real standard, client triggers it upon read error
            print(f"[SEC] Client {device.device} connected.")
            
            # Wait for disconnect
            await device.disconnected()
            print("[SEC] Session ended.")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[SEC] Error: {e}")

async def main():
    # Security happens at the lower bluetooth stack level in MicroPython
    # Ensure bonding is enabled if you want to remember devices
    await asyncio.gather(gatekeeper_task())

# NOTE: Actual passkey entry often requires low-level 
# 'bluetooth.BLE().config(..., bond=True)' which aioble abstracts.
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Bonding Cache**: If you change security settings, your phone might "Remember" the old insecure profile and fail to connect. Always "Forget This Device" in your phone's Bluetooth settings before testing new security logic.
*   **Passkey Length**: BLE passkeys MUST be exactly 6 digits. Using `1234` or `1234567` will cause the pairing engine to crash or default to "Just Works" (no code) mode.

## 1. Project 1566: Optimized Professional Bluetooth (BLE) Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize the survival time of battery-powered sensors by implementing an **Interval Scaling Algorithm**, balancing the trade-off between device discoverability (Fast Advertising) and long-term energy preservation (Slow Advertising).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dynamic TX Power**: Adjusting the radio's "Loudness" (dBm) based on battery level or proximity requirements.
*   **Discovery Latency**: The unavoidable time delay for a phone to find a device; 100ms interval = ~1s discovery; 1000ms interval = ~5s discovery.
*   **Discovery Phases**: Using "Burst Mode" during boot for instant pairing, then dropping to "Background Mode" for the rest of the uptime.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Power Management Layer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_set_tx_power`** (-20 to 9 dBm)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_update_adv_interval`** (Scaling)
*   **from Control, drag `wait_for_connection_else_scale`** (Timeout logic)

### 7. Variables
*   **pwr_dbm**: Integer (Radio strength).
*   **current_phase**: String (Fast/Slow).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Set the Burst Rate**:
    *   Set the interval to `50,000` (50ms).
    *   Set TX Power to `MAX` (9 dBm).
2.  **Start the Timer**:
    *   Record the start time using `time.ticks_ms()`.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **Evaluate Uptime**:
    *   **Snap** an `if` block: `if uptime > 30 seconds`.
4.  **Enter Deep Stealth**:
    *   Reduce interval to `2,000,000` (2 seconds).
    *   Reduce TX Power to `MIN` (-20 dBm).
    *   Update the radio config without stopping the beacon.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Benchmark**:
    *   Observe how the Pico W vanishes from the phone's scanner as you move into the next room, proving the "Whisper" mode is active.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **T=0-30s**: "Visible Mode". Easy to find. LED blinks fast. Power usage: High.
2.  **T=31s+**: "Stealth Mode". Hard to find unless close. LED blinks slow. Power usage: Tiny.
3.  **Result**: 90% reduction in average current consumption while maintaining a persistent presence.
4.  **Output**: "Power Optimization Applied | Phase: Eco | Interval: 2s | Gain: +400% Battery".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import bluetooth
import time

# --- CONFIG ---
FAST_INTERVAL = 100_000 # 100ms
SLOW_INTERVAL = 1_285_000 # ~1.2s (BLE standard prefers multiples of 0.625ms)

ble = bluetooth.BLE()
ble.active(True)

def update_radio(pwr, interval):
    print(f"[OPT] Power: {pwr}dBm | Interval: {interval/1000}ms")
    # Stop advertising to apply new config
    ble.gap_advertise(None)
    
    # Apply Power
    ble.config(txpower=pwr)
    
    # Restart with new interval
    payload = bytes([0x02, 0x01, 0x06, 0x05, 0x09]) + b"Pico"
    ble.gap_advertise(interval, payload)

# 1. START FAST (PHASE 1)
update_radio(9, FAST_INTERVAL)

start = time.ticks_ms()
is_fast = True

while True:
    # 2. MONITOR ELAPSED TIME
    elapsed = time.ticks_diff(time.ticks_ms(), start)
    
    if is_fast and elapsed > 20000: # After 20 seconds
        print("[OPT] Scaling down for sustainability...")
        # 3. SCALE TO STEALTH (PHASE 2)
        update_radio(-15, SLOW_INTERVAL)
        is_fast = False
        
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Stopping Without Restarting**: Forgetting to call `gap_advertise()` again after changing a config setting like `txpower`. The radio will remain silent.
*   **Interval Jitter**: Using non-standard intervals. BLE chips perform best when the interval is a multiple of `0.625ms`.

*   **Project 1567**: You are broadcasting a static name. Learn how to update the **Manufacturer Data** dynamically to broadcast sensor values without using a GATT connection.

## 1. Project 1567: Professional BLE Beacon Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate connection overhead and maximize throughput for one-to-many sensor networks by implementing a **Dynamic Beacon Pipeline**, broadcasting live sensor telemetry inside the "Manufacturer Specific Data" field of the advertising packet.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Manufacturer Specific Data (0xFF)**: A specialized field in the BLE advertisement that allows developers to insert custom proprietary data (like sensor readings) without following a standard GATT profile.
*   **Connectionless Telemetry**: The ability for thousands of scanners to read a single sensor's data simultaneously without the sensor ever having to manage a "Connection".
*   **Payload Rotation**: Updating the digital signature of the radio broadcast in real-time as physical sensor values change.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Broadcast Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_build_custom_field`** (Type 0xFF)
*   **from Data, drag `struct_pack_telemetry`** (Binary encoding)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_hot_update_adv`** (Live swap)
*   **from Text, drag `hex_format`** (Diagnostic output)

### 7. Variables
*   **temp_raw**: Integer (Fixed-point scaled temperature).
*   **batt_level**: Byte (0-100%).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Encoding Phase**
1.  **Prepare the Buffer**:
    *   Read your sensor (e.g., Onboard Temp).
    *   Scale the value to an integer (e.g., 25.43 -> 2543) so it fits in 2 bytes.
    *   **Snap** a `struct.pack` block to convert the integer into "raw bytes".

**B. Assembly Phase**
2.  **Forge the Custom Field**:
    *   Calculate the length of your data + 3 (1 for Type, 2 for Company ID).
    *   Use Type ID `0xFF`. 
    *   Use the "Testing" Company ID `0xFFFF`.
3.  **Merge with Standard Fields**:
    *   Combine the custom field with the "Discoverable Flags".

**C. Broadcast Phase**
4.  **Live Updates**:
    *   In a loop, call `ble.gap_advertise()` with the new payload. 
    *   Notice that the Pico does not stop/start the radio; it simply updates the internal transmission buffer.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W starts broadcasting a generic name.
2.  **Process**: Every 2 seconds, it reads the temperature and recalculates the binary payload.
3.  **Action**: The radio signature changes from `...FF FFFF 09C7` (25.03C) to `...FF FFFF 09C8` (25.04C).
4.  **Result**: Your scanner app shows different "Raw Data" values even though you are not connected to the device.
5.  **Output**: "Telemetry: Active | Method: Broadcaster | Data: [0x09, 0xC8] | Mode: Connectionless".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import bluetooth
import struct
import time
import machine

# --- SETTINGS ---
UPDATE_RATE_MS = 2000
# Company ID 0xFFFF is reserved for internal testing
COMPANY_ID = 0xFFFF 

ble = bluetooth.BLE()
ble.active(True)

def get_payload(temp_c):
    """Builds a dynamic advertising packet with embedded sensor data"""
    payload = bytearray()
    
    # 1. Standard Flags (Discoverable)
    payload += bytes([0x02, 0x01, 0x06])
    
    # 2. Manufacturer Specific Data (Type 0xFF)
    # Scaled Temp (e.g. 25.5 -> 2550)
    scaled_temp = int(temp_c * 100)
    
    # Pack: Company ID (2 bytes) + Temp (2 bytes signed)
    # struct.pack("<Hh") -> Little Endian unsigned short, signed short
    custom_data = struct.pack("<Hh", COMPANY_ID, scaled_temp)
    
    # Field: [Length, Type, Data]
    field_len = len(custom_data) + 1
    payload += bytes([field_len, 0xFF]) + custom_data
    
    # 3. Optional Local Name
    name = b"Pico-Tele"
    payload += bytes([len(name)+1, 0x09]) + name
    
    return payload

print("[SYS] Data Pipeline Online. Broadcasting...")

# Internal sensor setup
sensor_temp = machine.ADC(4)
conversion_factor = 3.3 / (65535)

while True:
    # 1. Read Sensor
    reading = sensor_temp.read_u16() * conversion_factor
    temp = 27 - (reading - 0.706)/0.001721
    
    # 2. Rebuild Payload
    p = get_payload(temp)
    
    # 3. Hot Update
    # interval_us=100000 (100ms for fast discovery)
    ble.gap_advertise(100000, p)
    
    print(f"[ADV] Updated Telemetry: {temp:.2f}C")
    time.sleep_ms(UPDATE_RATE_MS)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Big Endian Discrepancy**: Most BLE apps expect Multi-byte numbers to be **Little Endian**. If you use `struct.pack(">h", val)` (Big Endian), your temperature readings will look completely wrong on the phone.
*   **Update Frequency**: Don't update the advertising payload faster than the interval (e.g., updating every 50ms when the interval is 100ms). This causes "Packet Drop" where the radio skip cycles.

## 1. Project 1568: Multi-Role Professional BLE (Central Role)

### 2. Learning Objective
Transform the Pico W from a passive broadcaster into an active intelligence hub by implementing the **BLE Central Role**, enabling the device to scan for, identify, and parse data from other beacons in the surrounding environment.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Central vs Peripheral**: The Central (Scanner) is the device that initiates connections or listens; the Peripheral (Advertiser) is the sender.
*   **Observer Pattern**: A non-connecting Central mode that purely listens for advertising packets to harvest data (Passive Scanning).
*   **Scan Window/Interval**: The timing parameters that define how long the radio listens and how often it switches channels (37, 38, 39).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Second Pico W (Running Project 1567) or a BLE Beacon

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | BLE Central Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_start_scanning`** (Initiate observation)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_on_scan_result`** (Event handler)
*   **from Data, drag `parse_tlv_record`** (Extract name)
*   **from Math, drag `filter_rssi_threshold`** (Proximity check)

### 7. Variables
*   **device_addr**: String (The unique MAC of the found beacon).
*   **signal_strength**: Integer (RSSI value).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Switch Roles**:
    *   Initialize `aioble`.
    *   **Snap** an `async for` loop to iterate through `aioble.scan()`.
2.  **Filter the Noise**:
    *   Set a minimum duration (e.g., 5 seconds) to collect data.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **Identify the Target**:
    *   Inside the loop, check the `device.name()`. 
    *   **Snap** an `if` block: `if name == "Pico-Tele"`.
4.  **Extract the Payload**:
    *   Access `device.manufacturer_data()`. This contains the temperature we broadcasted in Project 1567.

**C. Intelligence Phase**
5.  **Calculate Proximity**:
    *   If `RSSI > -50`, the beacon is very close.
    *   If `RSSI < -90`, the beacon is at the edge of the range.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W puts its radio into "Receiver Mode".
2.  **Process**: It cycles through the 3 advertising channels every few milliseconds.
3.  **Action**: It detects a packet from "Pico-Tele". It extracts `0xFFFF` + `[Temp Bytes]`.
4.  **Result**: The Central Pico displays "Found Beacon at -62dBm | Temp: 24.5C".
5.  **Output**: "Central Mode Active | Devices Found: 1 | Target RSSI: Strong".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth
import struct

# The target company ID from Project 1567
TARGET_CO_ID = 0xFFFF

async def scanner_task():
    """Acts as a BLE Central to find and parse beacons"""
    print("[CENTRAL] Scanning for telemetry nodes...")
    
    # scan_loop iterates as results come in
    async with aioble.scan(duration_ms=0, interval_us=1280000, window_us=11250) as scanner:
        async for result in scanner:
            # 1. Filter by Name (from the advertising packet)
            if result.name() == "Pico-Tele":
                # 2. Extract Manufacturer Data
                # aioble's manufacturer_data() returns 
                # (company_id, data_bytes) or None
                m_data = result.manufacturer_data()
                
                if m_data:
                    co_id, raw_bytes = m_data
                    if co_id == TARGET_CO_ID:
                        # 3. Parse Temperature
                        # Expecting 2-byte signed short from Project 1567
                        try:
                            temp_scaled = struct.unpack("<h", raw_bytes)[0]
                            temp_c = temp_scaled / 100
                            
                            # 4. Proximity Logic
                            dist = "NEAR" if result.rssi > -60 else "FAR"
                            
                            print(f"[FOUND] {result.device} | Temp: {temp_c:.2f}C | {dist} ({result.rssi}dBm)")
                        except:
                            pass

async def main():
    await scanner_task()

# BOOT
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking the Scan**: If your processing logic (e.g., writing to an SD card) takes too long, you will miss the next advertising packet. Keep the code inside the `async for` loop extremely lean.
*   **RSSI Inaccuracy**: Relying on a single RSSI value for distance. RSSI is highly "Noisy". Professional centrals usually average 5-10 packets to get a stable distance estimate.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1569: Multi-Threaded Professional Bluetooth (BLE)

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize device uptime and radio responsiveness by offloading the **BLE Event Loop** to the RP2040’s **Core 1**, ensuring that heavy WiFi traffic or complex data processing on Core 0 never causes "Jitter" or misses in the advertising pulse.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Dual-Core Radio Management**: Running the high-level Bluetooth protocol stack on a dedicated processor core to prevent blocking from other software tasks.
*   **RF Coexistence**: Understanding how the CYW43439 chip shares a single antenna between WiFi and Bluetooth using time-division multiplexing.
*   **Inter-Thread Communication**: Using global status flags to allow the Background Core (Core 1) to inform the Main Core (Core 0) when a BLE event occurs.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Dual-Core Communications |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `run_on_core1`** (Thread spawn)
*   **from Network, drag `wifi_connect_core0`** (Primary radio task)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_advertise_core1`** (Secondary radio task)
*   **from Logic, drag `global_sync_variable`** (Core handshake)

### 7. Variables
*   **ble_ready**: Boolean (Status flag).
*   **wifi_active**: Boolean (Status flag).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Core 1: The Radio Worker**
1.  **Define the BLE Task**:
    *   Create a function `ble_worker()`.
    *   Initialize the BLE radio and set up a standard beacon.
    *   **Snap** a `while True` loop that simply monitors the stack.
2.  **Signal Readiness**:
    *   Set a global variable `ble_online = True`.

**B. Core 0: The Web Manager**
3.  **Define the WiFi Task**:
    *   Create the main loop that connects to WiFi.
    *   Perform a heavy operation, such as downloading a 100KB file.
4.  **Launch Phase**:
    *   **Snap** the `_thread.start_new_thread(ble_worker)` block at the very start of the script.

**C. Observation Phase**
5.  **Monitor the Result**:
    *   Notice that the BLE beacon appears on your phone *instantly* during boot, while the WiFi is still stuck in the "Handshake" phase.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Core 0 jumps to the task launcher.
2.  **Split**: Core 1 wakes up and immediately starts the Bluetooth subsystem.
3.  **Concurrent**: Core 1 pulses the radio every 100ms. Simultaneously, Core 0 begins the slow process of WiFi scanning and IP negotiation.
4.  **Result**: The Bluetooth identity is preserved even if the WiFi stack crashes or blocks for 10+ seconds.
5.  **Output**: "Dual-Core Sync: OK | Core 0: WiFi Link | Core 1: BLE Beacon Pulse".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import _thread
import time
import bluetooth
import network
import machine

# --- GLOBAL STATE ---
# Flags to coordinate between cores
ble_active = False

def ble_core_task():
    """Logic running on Core 1 - Dedicated to Bluetooth"""
    global ble_active
    print("[CORE 1] Initializing BLE Radio...")
    
    ble = bluetooth.BLE()
    ble.active(True)
    
    # Simple Beacon Payload
    payload = bytes([0x02, 0x01, 0x06, 0x05, 0x09]) + b"Dual"
    
    # Start high-priority advertising
    ble.gap_advertise(100_000, payload)
    ble_active = True
    
    led = machine.Pin("LED", machine.Pin.OUT)
    while True:
        # Slow pulse to show Core 1 is sane
        led.toggle()
        time.sleep(1)

def main_core_task():
    """Logic running on Core 0 - Dedicated to WiFi & App Logic"""
    print("[CORE 0] Starting WiFi Pipeline...")
    wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
    wlan.active(True)
    
    # Note: Replace with your actual credentials
    wlan.connect("YOUR_SSID", "YOUR_PASSWORD")
    
    while not wlan.isconnected():
        print("[CORE 0] Waiting for WiFi... BLE should already be visible!")
        time.sleep(1)
        
    print(f"[CORE 0] WiFi Online! IP: {wlan.ifconfig()[0]}")
    
    # Simulate heavy network load
    while True:
        if ble_active:
            print("[CORE 0] Heartbeat: Radio cores synchronized.")
        time.sleep(5)

# --- BOOTSTRAP ---
# 1. Launch the background thread first
_thread.start_new_thread(ble_core_task, ())

# 2. Run the main task on the primary core
main_core_task()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Direct API Collisions**: While MicroPython's `bluetooth` and `network` modules are thread-safe, avoid calling `active(True)` on both at the exact same millisecond. Add a tiny `time.sleep(0.1)` between them if you encounter "Firmware Hang" errors.
*   **Printing Race Conditions**: Data from both cores will be sent to the Serial terminal at once, often resulting in mixed text: `[CORE [CORE 1]0] Hello`. This is normal for multi-threaded debugging.

# Simulated Test
tracker = AssetTracker()
tracker.new_reading(-50)
print(f"Status: {tracker.get_status()}") # IMMEDIATE
tracker.new_reading(-95)
print(f"Status: {tracker.get_status()}") # Average drops...

**11. Common Mistakes:**  
*   **Metal/Water**: RSSI is heavily absorbed by human bodies (water) and metal cases. Putting the beacon in your pocket drops the signal by 10-20dBm.

## Batch 158: Professional BLE UART (1571-1580)

## 1. Project 1571: Professional BLE UART Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Enable two-way wireless serial communication by implementing the **Nordic UART Service (NUS)** protocol, allowing the Pico W to act as a wireless bridge between high-level mobile applications and embedded hardware.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **NUS (Nordic UART Service)**: A semi-standardized BLE profile used by virtually all mobile "BLE Terminal" apps to simulate a serial port over a wireless link.
*   **Characteristic Permissions**: Understanding when to use `READ` (passive retrieval), `WRITE` (receiving commands), and `NOTIFY` (pushing data updates to the phone).
*   **NUS UUIDs**: Specific 128-bit identifiers (Starting with `6E40`) that label the UART service and its RX/TX pipes.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Wireless Link Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_define_nus_service`** (Standard UART)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_setup_tx_characteristic`** (Pico -> Phone)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_setup_rx_characteristic`** (Phone -> Pico)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_register_gatt`** (Finalize profile)

### 7. Variables
*   **uart_service**: Object (The NUS container).
*   **rx_char**: Object (Receiving pipe).
*   **tx_char**: Object (Transmitting pipe).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Schema Phase**
1.  **Define the Base UUID**:
    *   Initialize `aioble`.
    *   **Snap** a `UUID` block and enter `"6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E"`.
2.  **Add the Characteristics**:
    *   **Snap** a `Characteristic` for TX (UUID `...0003`). Set property to `notify=True`.
    *   **Snap** a `Characteristic` for RX (UUID `...0002`). Set property to `write=True`.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **Register and Advertise**:
    *   Register the NUS service.
    *   **Snap** the `advertise` block. Set the name to `"Pico-UART"`.
4.  **Wait for Peer**:
    *   Use an `async with` block to wait for a connection.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Terminal Check**:
    *   Open "Serial Bluetooth Terminal" on Android or "Bluefruit Connect" on iOS.
    *   Connect to "Pico-UART". The terminal should open without "Service Not Found" errors.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W registers the NUS GATT database.
2.  **Process**: The radio starts "Advertising" as a Connectable Peripheral.
3.  **Action**: The Phone connects. The Pico W stops advertising and locks into the session.
4.  **Result**: The bidirectional data pipes are opened and ready for transfer.
5.  **Output**: "GATT Service: NUS Active | State: Linked | Pipe: Ready".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth

# 1. DEFINE NUS UUIDS
_UART_SERVICE_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
_UART_RX_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400002-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
_UART_TX_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400003-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")

# 2. SETUP SERVICE & CHARACTERISTICS
uart_service = aioble.Service(_UART_SERVICE_UUID)
tx_char = aioble.Characteristic(
    uart_service, _UART_TX_UUID, read=True, notify=True
)
rx_char = aioble.Characteristic(
    uart_service, _UART_RX_UUID, write=True, capture=True
)

async def peripheral_task():
    """Manages the lifecycle of the UART connection"""
    while True:
        try:
            print("[BLE] Waiting for UART Central (Phone)...")
            connection = await aioble.advertise(
                250_000,
                name="Pico-UART",
                services=[_UART_SERVICE_UUID],
            )
            print(f"[BLE] Connected to {connection.device}")
            
            # Wait for disconnect
            await connection.disconnected()
            print("[BLE] Disconnected")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[BLE] Driver Error: {e}")

async def main():
    # Peripheral task handles the radio state machine
    await peripheral_task()

# START
asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **UUID Typo**: One digit wrong in the 128-bit NUS UUID will make the device invisible to standard terminal apps. Double-check `6E400001` vs `6E400002`.
*   **Characteristic Capture**: Forgetting to set `capture=True` on the RX characteristic. Without this, MicroPython won't buffer incoming data, and you'll miss every message the phone sends.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1572**: The driver is ready. Now implement the **Asynchronous Echo Logic** to process incoming text and reply automatically.

## 1. Project 1572: Asynchronous Professional BLE UART Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Create a responsive communication system by implementing an **Asynchronous Echo Server**, capable of processing incoming text commands from a smartphone and returning data without stalling the Pico W’s main logic.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Awaitable Events**: Using `await char.written()` to suspend a task until the radio hardware signals that fresh data has arrived.
*   **Byte Translation**: Converting raw binary payloads from the radio (`bytes`) into human-readable text strings using `.decode('utf-8')`.
*   **Non-Blocking Feedback**: Pushing notifications back to the phone terminal while simultaneously listening for the next command.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Virtual COM Port over BLE |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `on_ble_receive`** (Wait for write)
*   **from Text, drag `get_payload_text`** (Decode bytes)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_send_text`** (Notify central)
*   **from Loops, drag `pico_forever`** (Process loop)

### 7. Variables
*   **received_text**: String (Human-readable data).
*   **echo_msg**: String (Response string).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Wait Phase**
1.  **Establish the listener**:
    *   Inside the connection loop, **snap** a `pico_forever` block.
    *   **Snap** the `await rx_char.written()` event block.
2.  **Capture the Data**:
    *   **Snap** `rx_char.read()` to retrieve the byte buffer from the radio.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **Sanitize and Decode**:
    *   Convert the bytes to a string.
    *   **Snap** a `print` block to display the message on the Serial Monitor for debugging.
4.  **The Echo Logic**:
    *   Prepare a response (e.g., "ACK: " + original message).

**C. Output Phase**
5.  **Notify the Phone**:
    *   **Snap** the `tx_char.notify()` block. 
    *   Pass the connection object and the response string.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W establishes a Link with the smartphone.
2.  **Process**: The code hits the `await` line and pauses. CPU usage drops.
3.  **Action**: You type "Hello" on the phone app and press Send.
4.  **Sense**: The Pico radio receives the packet and "Wakes up" the code.
5.  **Result**: The message is printed to the terminal, and "ACK: Hello" appears on the phone screen instantly.
6.  **Output**: "UART RX: 'Hello' | Action: Echo Sent | Delay: <10ms".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth

# (NUS UUIDs from Project 1571)
_UART_SERVICE_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
_UART_RX_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400002-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
_UART_TX_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400003-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")

async def uart_handler(connection, rx_char, tx_char):
    """Handles data exchange during an active session"""
    print("[UART] Handler started.")
    while connection.is_connected():
        try:
            # 1. WAIT FOR DATA
            # This 'await' lets other asyncio tasks run while we wait
            await rx_char.written()
            
            # 2. READ AND DECODE
            # Raw data is bytes; we strip whitespace for cleaner handling
            raw_data = rx_char.read()
            message = raw_data.decode('utf-8').strip()
            print(f"[RECV] {message}")
            
            # 3. ECHO BACK
            response = f"Pico Echoed: {message}"
            tx_char.notify(connection, response)
            
        except asyncio.CancelledError:
            break
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[UART] Loop error: {e}")
            break

async def main():
    # Setup GATT
    service = aioble.Service(_UART_SERVICE_UUID)
    tx = aioble.Characteristic(service, _UART_TX_UUID, read=True, notify=True)
    rx = aioble.Characteristic(service, _UART_RX_UUID, write=True, capture=True)
    aioble.register_services(service)
    
    while True:
        async with await aioble.advertise(250_000, name="Pico-Echo", services=[_UART_SERVICE_UUID]) as conn:
            print(f"[BLE] Session with {conn.device}")
            await uart_handler(conn, rx, tx)
            print("[BLE] Session ended.")

# START
asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing is_connected Check**: Attempting to notify `tx_char` after the phone has disconnected will cause an `OSError`. Always wrap your handler in a `while connection.is_connected()` loop.
*   **Encoding Errors**: If you send an Emoji or non-standard character from the phone, `.decode('utf-8')` might crash. Use `.decode('utf-8', 'ignore')` in high-reliability industrial code.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1573**: Refactor this functional code into a **Clean OOP Class** so you can easily drop a Bluetooth Console into any future robot project.

---

*   **Encoding Issues**: The data arrives as `bytes`. If you try to concatenate it with a string without `.decode()`, Python will error.

## 1. Project 1573: Object-Oriented Professional BLE UART Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Architect a reusable hardware abstraction by implementing a **Professional BLE UART Class**, effectively decoupling the wireless communication stack from your application logic (e.g., motor control or sensor math).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Class Abstraction**: Hiding the low-level UUIDs and GATT service registrations inside a `BLEConsole` object so the user sees only simple `read()` and `write()` methods.
*   **State Management**: Using class properties (e.g., `self.connection`) to track if the radio is currently linked to a phone.
*   **Event Encapsulation**: Moving the `advertise` and `written` asyncio tasks inside a dedicated method that runs in the background.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Wireless "Console" Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `new_class`** (Define BLEConsole)
*   **from Logic, drag `class_method`** (Define read/write)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_init_class`** (Background task)
*   **from Control, drag `create_async_task`** (Non-blocking spawn)

### 7. Variables
*   **console**: Object (Instance of BLEConsole).
*   **command**: String (User command from phone).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Define the Constructor**:
    *   Inside `__init__`, register the NUS service and characteristics.
    *   Initialize `self.connection = None`.
2.  **Define the Interface**:
    *   Create a `write(msg)` method that checks if a connection exists before notifying.
    *   Create an `any()` method to check if the RX buffer has data.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **The Background Worker**:
    *   Implement an `async run()` method that manages the `aioble.advertise` loop infinitely.
4.  **Integration**:
    *   In your `main()` script, **snap** the `asyncio.create_task(console.run())` block to start the radio.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Interactive Test**:
    *   In your main loop, call `console.write("System OK")`. 
    *   Verify the message appears on your phone every second.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The `main()` task spawns the `console.run()` task onto the event loop.
2.  **Process**: The main loop performs sensor work. Simultaneously, the background task waits for a phone.
3.  **Action**: Phone sends "REBOOT". The console class captures it.
4.  **Result**: The main loop calls `console.read()`, sees the command, and triggers the reset logic.
5.  **Output**: "Console: Background Thread Active | Buffer: 0 Commands | Status: Ready".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth

class BLEConsole:
    def __init__(self, name="Pico-Console"):
        # 1. Standard NUS UUIDs
        self._SVC_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
        self._RX_UUID  = bluetooth.UUID("6E400002-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
        self._TX_UUID  = bluetooth.UUID("6E400003-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
        
        # 2. Setup GATT components
        self.service = aioble.Service(self._SVC_UUID)
        self.tx_char = aioble.Characteristic(self.service, self._TX_UUID, read=True, notify=True)
        self.rx_char = aioble.Characteristic(self.service, self._RX_UUID, write=True, capture=True)
        aioble.register_services(self.service)
        
        self.name = name
        self.connection = None
        self.rx_buffer = []

    async def run(self):
        """Background management of the BLE stack"""
        while True:
            async with await aioble.advertise(200_000, name=self.name, services=[self._SVC_UUID]) as conn:
                print(f"[CLASS] Connected: {conn.device}")
                self.connection = conn
                try:
                    while self.connection.is_connected():
                        await self.rx_char.written()
                        data = self.rx_char.read().decode('utf-8').strip()
                        self.rx_buffer.append(data)
                except Exception as e:
                    print(f"[CLASS] Session Error: {e}")
                finally:
                    self.connection = None
                    print("[CLASS] Re-advertising...")

    def write(self, text):
        """External interface to send data to phone"""
        if self.connection:
            try:
                self.tx_char.notify(self.connection, text + "\n")
            except:
                pass

    def read(self):
        """External interface to get commands"""
        if self.rx_buffer:
            return self.rx_buffer.pop(0)
        return None

async def main():
    # Instantiate the Professional Console
    console = BLEConsole("Pico-Control")
    
    # Launch terminal manager in background
    asyncio.create_task(console.run())
    
    # Your Main Application Logic
    count = 0
    while True:
        # Check for commands from smartphone
        cmd = console.read()
        if cmd == "reset":
            count = 0
            console.write("Counter Reset!")
        
        # Stream data
        console.write(f"Heartbeat: {count}")
        count += 1
        await asyncio.sleep(2)

# BOOT
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Memory Leak**: If the phone sends thousands of messages and you never call `read()`, the `rx_buffer` list will grow until the Pico W runs out of RAM. Always limit the buffer size: `if len(self.rx_buffer) > 20: self.rx_buffer.pop(0)`.
*   **Blocking Main**: If you run a `while True` loop in main without an `await asyncio.sleep()`, the `console.run()` task will never get a turn to execute, and the radio will freeze.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1574: Robust Professional BLE UART Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
Overcome the physical packet size constraints of Bluetooth Low Energy by implementing an **Asynchronous Fragmentation Algorithm**, allowing the Pico W to transmit large data buffers (e.g., long sensor logs or JSON strings) by automatically slicing them into MTU-compliant chunks.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **MTU (Maximum Transmission Unit)**: The maximum number of bytes that can fit in a single BLE radio packet (Defaults: ~23 bytes; User Data: ~20 bytes).
*   **Packet Fragmentation**: Dividing a large data stream into smaller pieces for sequential transmission.
*   **Flow Control (Throttle)**: Adding small millisecond delays (`asyncio.sleep_ms`) to prevent "Buffer Overflow" in the radio's hardware transmit FIFO.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | High-Bandwidth Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Text, drag `get_string_length`** (Buffer check)
*   **from Math, drag `get_min_value`** (Chunk selection)
*   **from Text, drag `get_substring`** (Slicing logic)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_notify_chunk`** (Sequential push)

### 7. Variables
*   **remaining_data**: String (Remaining buffer).
*   **mtu_size**: Integer (Max allowed payload).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Slicing Phase**
1.  **Determine the Limit**:
    *   Initialize the `mtu_size` to `20` (Compatibility fallback for most phones).
2.  **Initialize the Loop**:
    *   **Snap** a `while` block: `while length(data) > 0`.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **Calculate the Slice**:
    *   **Snap** a variable block to take the first `mtu_size` characters of the string.
    *   Update the string by removing those characters (e.g., `data = data[mtu_size:]`).
4.  **Sequential Push**:
    *   **Snap** the `tx_char.notify()` block inside the loop.
    *   Add an `await asyncio.sleep_ms(10)` block to allow the radio to clear its buffer.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Telemetry Test**:
    *   Use the phone app to send a 500-character test string.
    *   Observe the "Log" on the phone app; standard BLE apps will automatically stitch these chunks back together into one long paragraph.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The application attempts to send a 100-byte JSON object.
2.  **Process**: The loop detects the 100-byte size exceeds the 20-byte MTU.
3.  **Action**: The Pico radio sends five sequential packets of 20 bytes each.
4.  **Result**: The phone receives the complete JSON string without truncation or packet drop.
5.  **Output**: "TX Logic: Multi-Packet | Chunks: 5 | Bytes: 100 | State: Complete".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth

# (Assuming NUS Setup from Project 1573)

async def write_robust(connection, tx_char, long_msg):
    """
    Slices and sends large text payloads over BLE notification.
    Prevents ENOMEM errors and packet truncation.
    """
    if not connection or not connection.is_connected():
        return False
        
    # Standard BLE MTU minus 3 bytes for ATT header
    # Historically 23 - 3 = 20 bytes
    SAFE_MTU = 20
    
    print(f"[TX] Fragmenting {len(long_msg)} bytes...")
    
    ptr = 0
    while ptr < len(long_msg):
        # 1. SLICE DATA
        chunk = long_msg[ptr : ptr + SAFE_MTU]
        
        try:
            # 2. TRANSMIT CHUNK
            tx_char.notify(connection, chunk)
            ptr += SAFE_MTU
            
            # 3. CONGESTION CONTROL
            # Small yield allows any receiving interrupts to process
            await asyncio.sleep_ms(20) 
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[TX] Failure at byte {ptr}: {e}")
            return False
            
    print("[TX] Large transfer complete.")
    return True

async def main():
    # ... setup BLE console ...
    # Example usage:
    # big_data = "SENSOR_ID:PICO_01|STATUS:OPERATIONAL|" * 10
    # await write_robust(conn, tx, big_data)
    pass

# Note: In production, use 'await conn.exchange_mtu(247)' to increase speed
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Omitting the Delay**: If you run the `while` loop at full CPU speed, the radio chip's small internal buffer will fill up faster than it can broadcast. This results in the infamous `OSError: 12 (ENOMEM)`. 
*   **Variable Overhead**: Don't calculate `len(long_msg)` inside the loop predicate if you are modifying the string (`long_msg = long_msg[20:]`) as it is less efficient. Use a pointer index instead.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1575: Professional BLE UART with Error Handling

### 2. Learning Objective
Ensure 100% wireless service availability by implementing a **Resilient BLE Supervisor**, utilizing asynchronous exception handling to automatically recover from dropped connections (Out of Range) or interference crashes.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Supervisor Loop**: An architecture where the primary logic is wrapped in an infinite "Retry" structure that re-initializes hardware upon failure.
*   **Graceful Exit**: Using `CancelledError` handling to ensure the radio correctly "Unregisters" services and cleans up RAM when a session ends.
*   **Re-discovery Logic**: Ensuring the device immediately returns to "Advertising" mode after an unexpected disconnect, without requiring a manual hardware reset.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Self-Healing Wireless Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Control, drag `on_disconnect_restart`** (Event cleanup)
*   **from Control, drag `try_except_with_retry`** (Error recovery)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_stop_advertising`** (Cleanup phase)
*   **from Logic, drag `is_connected_flag`** (Status check)

### 7. Variables
*   **error_log**: List (History of crash events).
*   **retry_count**: Integer (Session tracker).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Supervision Phase**
1.  **Define the Outer Loop**:
    *   **Snap** a `while True` block to encapsulate the entire BLE setup.
2.  **Initialize the Guardian**:
    *   **Snap** a `try / except` block inside the loop. 
    *   Place all `advertise` logic inside the `try` section.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **Handle the Outage**:
    *   In the `except` block, **snap** a `print` block: "Link Lost... Retrying".
4.  **Automatic Reset**:
    *   **Snap** a `wait` block for 2 seconds to allow the radio chip to clear its internal state.
    *   Reset `self.connection = None`.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Simulate a Drop**:
    *   Connect your phone.
    *   Walk into another room until the signal vanishes. 
    *   Observe the Serial Monitor; the Pico should log the error and immediately show up on the phone's scanner again.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W begins advertising.
2.  **Process**: A connection is made and data is exchanged.
3.  **Event**: The phone's battery dies or Bluetooth is toggled off. 
4.  **Action**: The Pico radio raises an `asyncio.CancelledError`. 
5.  **Result**: The code jumps to the `finally` block, nullifies the connection object, and restarts the advertising loop automatically.
6.  **Output**: "Radio Alert: Link Dropped | Action: Supervisor Restarting | State: Advertising...".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth

# (NUS UUIDs from Project 1571)
_UART_SVC_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")

async def supervisor_task():
    """Infinite loop ensuring BLE availability"""
    while True:
        try:
            print("[SUP] Entering Advertising Mode...")
            async with await aioble.advertise(250_000, name="Pico-Super", services=[_UART_SVC_UUID]) as connection:
                print(f"[SUP] Session established with {connection.device}")
                
                # RUN CONNECTED LOGIC
                try:
                    while connection.is_connected():
                        # Core communication happens here
                        await asyncio.sleep(1)
                except asyncio.CancelledError:
                    print("[SUP] Central initiated disconnect.")
                except Exception as e:
                    print(f"[SUP] Connection failure: {e}")
                finally:
                    # CLEANUP
                    # Wait for hardware to acknowledge drop before loop restarts
                    await connection.disconnected()
                    print("[SUP] Connection cleaned up.")

        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[CRITICAL] Radio Error: {e}")
            # Cool-down to prevent rapid-fire loop crashes
            await asyncio.sleep_ms(1000)

async def main():
    await supervisor_task()

# BOOT
try:
    asyncio.run(main())
except KeyboardInterrupt:
    pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Nested Exceptions**: Forgetting to catch errors *inside* the connection loop separately from the *advertise* logic. This can cause the whole program to exit instead of just restarting the advertisement.
*   **Radio Throttling**: If you loop too fast during an error (no `sleep` in the `except` block), you can trigger a watchdog reset on the Pico W as the CPU spends 100% of its time processing error messages.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1576**: Reliability is solved. Now maximize **Interaction Speed** by optimizing the radio's connection parameters for low latency.

## 1. Project 1576: Optimized Professional BLE UART Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate control lag in real-time robotics by implementing **Connection Physics Optimization**, requesting high-speed communication intervals from the smartphone to reduce latency from ~100ms down to ~7.5ms.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Connection Interval**: The timing parameter that defines how frequently the Central (Phone) and Peripheral (Pico) wake up to synchronize data.
*   **Round-Trip Time (RTT)**: The total time for a command to go from phone to Pico and the acknowledgment to return.
*   **Radio Window Negotiation**: Using the `aioble.update()` command to request the OS (Android/iOS) to switch from "Battery Saver" mode to "High Performance" mode.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Low-Latency Control Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_request_fast_interval`** (set min/max)
*   **from Time, drag `get_ticks_ms`** (Benchmark tool)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_update_param`** (Trigger negotiation)
*   **from Control, drag `on_parameter_update`** (Success check)

### 7. Variables
*   **start_tick**: Integer (Timestamp).
*   **latency_ms**: Integer (Calculated delay).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Establish Connection**:
    *   Start advertising as Connectable.
    *   Wait for the "Connected" event.

**B. Logic Phase**
2.  **Negotiate Speed**:
    *   **Snap** the `connection.update()` block.
    *   Set `min_interval=6` (7.5ms) and `max_interval=12` (15ms).
3.  **Benchmark the Link**:
    *   Record the current ticks.
    *   **Snap** a `print` block: "Sent Ping".
    *   Send a notification to the phone. 
4.  **Calculate Results**:
    *   When the phone echoes the data back, subtract the original ticks.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Comparative Analysis**:
    *   Compare the response speed before and after the `update()` call. 
    *   The robot's reactions should feel "Snappy" and instantaneous rather than "Floaty".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico W connects at the OS-default 100ms interval.
2.  **Negotiation**: Pico requests a 7.5ms interval.
3.  **Action**: The smartphone radio chip adjusts its internal scheduling.
4.  **Result**: Data packets travel 10x more frequently.
5.  **Output**: "Radio Performance: HIGH | Interval: 7.5ms | Measured Latency: 15ms | Status: Real-time Optimized".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth
import time

# (NUS Setup from 1571)
SVC_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")

async def latency_test(conn, tx_char):
    """Benchmarks and optimizes connection speed"""
    print("[SPEED] Current Interval: 100ms (Default)")
    
    # 1. REQUEST FAST INTERVAL
    # 1.25ms units -> 6 * 1.25 = 7.5ms
    print("[SPEED] Requesting High Performance mode...")
    try:
        # Note: Some OS (iOS) may force a minimum of 15ms or 30ms
        await conn.update(min_interval=6, max_interval=12, timeout=100)
        print("[SPEED] Negotiation Sent.")
    except:
        print("[SPEED] Optimization rejected by OS.")

    while conn.is_connected():
        # 2. PERFORM BENCHMARK
        start = time.ticks_ms()
        
        # Send data
        tx_char.notify(conn, "ping")
        
        # In a real app, you would wait for 'written()' response 
        # to calculate true round-trip time.
        print(f"[PING] Pulse sent at {start}ms")
        
        await asyncio.sleep(2)

async def main():
    while True:
        async with await aioble.advertise(200_000, name="Pico-Speed", services=[SVC_UUID]) as connection:
            await latency_test(connection, None) # tx_char from service setup

# BOOT 
# (Actual implementation requires Characteristic objects defined in main)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ignoring OS Overrides**: You can request 7.5ms, but if the phone is an iPhone, it might force 30ms to save battery. Always treat the `update()` call as a "Suggestion" to the phone, not a command.
*   **Extreme Battery Drain**: High-performance mode keeps the radio active almost continuously. Only trigger this mode when an active motor or sensor task is running; return to standard speed when idle.

## 1. Project 1577: Professional BLE UART Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Visualize high-frequency sensor telemetry by implementing a **Telemetric Streaming Pipeline**, utilizing the CSV (Comma Separated Values) protocol to feed real-time data into smartphone-based graphical plotting applications.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Sample Rate**: The frequency at which data points are harvested from the sensor and pushed to the radio (e.g., 10Hz = 10 samples per second).
*   **Packet Pacing**: Balancing the update frequency against the radio’s bandwidth to prevent "Stutter" in the live graph.
*   **Plotter Protocols**: Formats like `Value1,Value2\n` that are natively recognized by industrial BLE terminals (e.g., Bluefruit Connect) for waveform generation.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Telemetry Source Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Text, drag `create_csv_row`** (Format data)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_notify_stream`** (High-speed push)
*   **from Time, drag `async_delay_ms`** (Sampling control)
*   **from Math, drag `get_random_int`** (Signal simulation)

### 7. Variables
*   **sensor_x**: Integer (Simulated axis).
*   **sensor_y**: Integer (Simulated axis).
*   **data_string**: String (CSV output).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Configure the GATT**:
    *   Setup the NUS TX characteristic.
2.  **Wait for Connection**:
    *   Wait for the user to open the "Plotter" view on their smartphone app.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **The Sampling Loop**:
    *   **Snap** a `pico_forever` block.
    *   Read the sensor data (or simulate with `random`).
4.  **The Data Wrap**:
    *   **Snap** a `create string` block: `format("{0},{1}\n", [val1, val2])`.
    *   **IMPORTANT**: The trailing `\n` (newline) is required for the phone app to recognize the end of a data point.

**C. Output Phase**
5.  **Steady Transmission**:
    *   **Snap** `tx_char.notify()` with a frequency of exactly `100ms` (`await asyncio.sleep(0.1)`).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W establishes a high-performance link.
2.  **Process**: The loop gathers X and Y sensor values.
3.  **Action**: The data is formatted into `10.5,22.1\n` and pushed to the phone.
4.  **Sense**: The smartphone app receives the string and adds a new dot to the moving wave graph.
5.  **Result**: Smooth, real-time visualization of motion or environmental changes.
6.  **Output**: "Stream: Active | Mode: CSV Plotter | Sampling: 10Hz | Health: Synchronized".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth
import random

# (NUS Setup from 1571)
TX_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400003-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")

async def telemetry_stream(connection, tx_char):
    """High-frequency data pipeline for real-time plotting"""
    print("[TX] Streaming to Telemetry Plotter...")
    
    while connection.is_connected():
        # 1. SAMPLE DATA (Simulating Accelerometer Axes)
        acc_x = random.uniform(-10.0, 10.0)
        acc_y = random.uniform(-10.0, 10.0)
        
        # 2. FORMAT AS CSV
        # Standard apps like Bluefruit Plotter require CSV with newline
        payload = f"{acc_x:.2f},{acc_y:.2f}\n"
        
        try:
            # 3. PUSH TO RADIO
            tx_char.notify(connection, payload)
            
            # 4. SAMPLING RATE 
            # 10Hz is safe for most BLE interfaces
            await asyncio.sleep(0.1) 
            
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"[TX] Stream error: {e}")
            break

async def main():
    # Setup UART services...
    # stream_task = asyncio.create_task(telemetry_stream(conn, tx))
    pass

# BOOT
# asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Missing Newline**: If you omit the `\n`, the phone app will buffer all data thinking it's one giant string, and the graph will never update.
*   **Overwhelming the Interface**: Trying to stream at 100Hz (1ms delay) will lag the radio stack and cause the GUI on the phone to freeze or crash. Stick to 10-20Hz for stable results.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1578: Secure Professional BLE UART Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Protect industrial radio control channels by implementing **Authenticated Pairing and Bonding**, ensuring that only smartphones with the correct 6-digit PIN can exchange data with the Pico W.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **MITM (Man-In-The-Middle) Protection**: A security protocol where a randomized Passkey is displayed on the device to prevent third-party "Ghost" connections.
*   **Bonding**: The process of persistently saving radio encryption keys to the Pico’s flash memory, allowing for "Automatic Reconnect" without re-entering the PIN.
*   **Pairing States**: Understanding the handshake between `Unauthenticated`, `Encrypted`, and `Authenticated` links.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Encrypted Security Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_configure_security`** (Set MITM/Bond)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_on_passkey_display`** (Event listener)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_force_pairing`** (Start handshake)
*   **from Control, drag `on_boot_reset_keys`** (Debug tool)

### 7. Variables
*   **secure_passkey**: Integer (The 6-digit code).
*   **is_bonded**: Boolean (Link status).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Configuration Phase**
1.  **Set IO Capabilities**:
    *   **Snap** the `aioble.config()` block. 
    *   Set `io=aioble.IO_DISPLAY_ONLY`. This tells the phone that the Pico can show a code but has no keyboard.
2.  **Enable Encryption**:
    *   Set `bond=True` and `mitm=True` for maximum defense.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **The Passkey Alert**:
    *   Define the `ble_irq` handler to watch for `_IRQ_PASSKEY_ACTION`.
    *   **Snap** a `print` block inside the IRQ to display the PIN in large text on the serial console.
4.  **Initiate Pairing**:
    *   Inside the connection loop, **snap** `await connection.pair()`.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Connect and Verify**:
    *   Attempt to connect with your phone. 
    *   A "Pairing Request" pop-up will appear on your screen. 
    *   Enter the 6-digit code shown in Thonny’s Serial Monitor to unlock the data link.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W starts advertising a "Security Required" flag.
2.  **Action**: The phone connects. The Pico raises a security interrupt.
3.  **Sense**: The Pico generates a random code (e.g., `542901`).
4.  **Process**: The user enters the code on the phone. The radio hardware verifies the keys match.
5.  **Result**: The link is encrypted. Data exchange begins safely.
6.  **Output**: "Security: Authenticated | Mode: Bonded | Encryption: AES-128 Active".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth

# 1. SECURITY CONFIGURATION
# bond=True saves keys; mitm=True requires PIN
aioble.config(
    bond=True, 
    mitm=True, 
    le_secure=True, 
    io=aioble.IO_DISPLAY_ONLY
)

def ble_irq_handler(event, data):
    """Fired by the radio hardware for security events"""
    # 23 = _IRQ_PASSKEY_ACTION
    if event == 23:
        conn_handle, action, passkey = data
        print("\n" + "*"*30)
        print(f" SECURITY ALERT: ENTER PIN")
        print(f" PASSKEY: {passkey:06d}")
        print("*"*30 + "\n")

# Register the Low-Level Interrupt
ble = bluetooth.BLE()
ble.irq(ble_irq_handler)

async def secure_server():
    # Setup NUS Service (Project 1571 logic here)
    SVC_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
    
    while True:
        async with await aioble.advertise(200_000, name="Secure-Pico", services=[SVC_UUID]) as conn:
            print("[SEC] Link established. Authenticating...")
            
            try:
                # 2. TRIGGER PAIRING HANDSHAKE
                # This causes the Passkey pop-up on the phone
                await conn.pair()
                print("[SEC] Device Bonded and Encrypted.")
                
                # Active logic here
                await conn.disconnected()
            except Exception as e:
                print(f"[SEC] Pairing Failed or Cancelled: {e}")

asyncio.run(secure_server())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Flash Memory Wear**: Bonding saves keys to the internal flash. Avoid writing scripts that constantly wipe and re-pair daily, as this could eventually wear out the flash blocks.
*   **Orphaned Pairings**: If you delete the Pico from your phone's Bluetooth settings, but don't clear the Pico's bond (using `nvs_flash_erase` or via code), they will fail to reconnect. You must clear BOTH sides to reset security.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1579**: Security adds processing overhead. Learn how to offload the **Radio State Machine to Core 1**, leaving the main CPU free for mission-critical tasks.

## 1. Project 1579: Multi-Threaded Professional BLE UART

### 2. Learning Objective
Maximize device responsiveness and prevent radio connection timeouts by implementing a **Multi-Core BLE Architecture**, running the high-priority radio event loop on Core 1 while the main application logic (the "Brain") runs on Core 0.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Core Isolation**: Dedicating one processor core exclusively to time-critical tasks like radio management to prevent "CPU Jitter".
*   **Inter-Core Buffering**: Using global list objects as "Queues" to safely pass received data from the Radio Core (1) to the Logic Core (0).
*   **Thread Spawning**: Using the `_thread` module to launch parallel asynchronous event loops.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Dual-Core Orchestration Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `run_on_core1`** (Thread start)
*   **from Logic, drag `global_queue_variable`** (Data link)
*   **from IoT, drag `ble_run_in_thread`** (Background GATT)
*   **from Control, drag `on_thread_ready`** (Sync check)

### 7. Variables
*   **received_queue**: List (Shared memory).
*   **logic_state**: Integer (Core 0 status).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Core 1 Configuration (Radio)**
1.  **Define the Worker**:
    *   Create a function `ble_entry_point()`.
    *   Initialize `aioble` and start the supervisor loop normally.
2.  **Queue the Inputs**:
    *   Inside the `rx_char.written()` block, **snap** an `append` block to put data into a global list.

**B. Core 0 Configuration (Brain)**
3.  **Define the Main Logic**:
    *   **Snap** a `while True` loop on the main script.
    *   **Snap** an `if global_list` block to check for fresh UART messages.
4.  **Parallel Launch**:
    *   **Snap** the `_thread.start_new_thread(ble_entry_point)` block at the very top of `main.py`.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **The Stress Test**:
    *   Run a heavy mathematical calculation on Core 0 (e.g., calculating Pi).
    *   Observe that the Bluetooth connection remains perfectly stable and responsive even when Core 0 is 100% loaded.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Core 0 wakes up and immediately spawns a twin process on Core 1.
2.  **Process**: Core 1 handles all BLE handshakes and PIN codes.
3.  **Action**: User sends "OFF" via phone. Core 1 sees it and places "OFF" in the shared memory.
4.  **Sense**: Core 0 (Main loop) detects the message in the list.
5.  **Result**: Core 0 executes the hardware shutdown while Core 1 stays ready for the next "ON" command.
6.  **Output**: "Architecture: Dual-Core | Core 0: Application | Core 1: Radio HW | Bridge: Shared Queue".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import _thread
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth
import time

# --- SHARED MEMORY BRIDGE ---
# In MicroPython, simple lists are mostly safe for single-item swaps
# For complex apps, use _thread.allocate_lock()
uart_buffer = []

def ble_core_1_task():
    """Runs exclusively on Core 1 - Dedicated Radio Manager"""
    print("[C1] BLE Thread Spawned.")
    
    # 1. SETUP BLE (Local to this core)
    SVC = bluetooth.UUID("6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
    RX = bluetooth.UUID("6E400002-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
    
    service = aioble.Service(SVC)
    rx_char = aioble.Characteristic(service, RX, write=True, capture=True)
    aioble.register_services(service)

    async def run_radio():
        while True:
            async with await aioble.advertise(200_000, name="Pico-Dual", services=[SVC]) as conn:
                while conn.is_connected():
                    await rx_char.written()
                    msg = rx_char.read().decode().strip()
                    # 2. PUSH TO SHARED MEMORY
                    uart_buffer.append(msg)
                    
    asyncio.run(run_radio())

# --- CORE 0: MAIN LOGIC ---
print("[C0] Preparing Radio Thread...")
_thread.start_new_thread(ble_core_1_task, ())

def main_loop():
    print("[C0] Brain is online Level 1.")
    while True:
        # 3. POLL THE BRIDGE
        if uart_buffer:
            command = uart_buffer.pop(0)
            print(f"[C0] Executing Command from Core 1: {command}")
            
            if command == "LED_ON":
                pass # Trigger GPIO here
                
        # Simulate heavy application load
        time.sleep(1)

main_loop()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Race Conditions**: If you try to `pop` and `append` from both cores at the exact same microsecond, the list pointer could corrupt. Always use `pop(0)` on the consumer side and `append()` on the producer side.
*   **Core 1 Crash**: Errors on Core 1 do not always print to the Thonny console clearly. If the radio just stops working, use `try/except` inside the thread to print error messages to a file or a global variable.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1580**: The ultimate wireless solution. Use this secure, dual-core UART link to perform **Over-The-Air (OTA) Firmware Updates**, allowing you to change the Pico's code from your phone.

## 1. Project 1580: Industrial Professional BLE UART Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
Implement a deployment-grade **Over-The-Air (OTA) Firmware Update** system, allowing for the remote installation of new MicroPython scripts via the BLE UART link, eliminating the need for physical USB access in sealed or remote enclosures.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **A/B File Swapping**: A safety pattern where new code is written to a temporary file (`update.py`) and only moved to the boot script (`main.py`) after the transfer is fully validated.
*   **Protocol Commands**: Defining unique "Keywords" (e.g., `OTA_START`, `OTA_STOP`) that tell the Pico when to switch from normal operation to "File Write" mode.
*   **Remote Reset**: Triggering a software reboot using `machine.reset()` to execute the newly uploaded firmware.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Remote Deployment Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from File System, drag `open_file_write`** (Update buffer)
*   **from Control, drag `on_ota_command`** (Mode switch)
*   **from File System, drag `rename_file`** (Commit update)
*   **from System, drag `reboot_pico`** (Execute new code)

### 7. Variables
*   **ota_handle**: Object (The file pointer).
*   **ota_active**: Boolean (Status flag).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Protocol Phase**
1.  **Define the Start Command**:
    *   Initialize `ota_active = False`.
    *   If received string is `"START_OTA"`, open a file named `update.py` for writing.
2.  **Define the Stop Command**:
    *   If received string is `"STOP_OTA"`, close the file and prepare for swap.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **Data Capture**:
    *   Inside the UART RX loop, if `ota_active` is true, write every incoming line directly to the file instead of printing it.
4.  **The Atomic Swap**:
    *   Once the transfer is done, use the `os.rename` operation to overwrite the old `main.py` with the new file.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Remote Update Test**:
    *   Send the text `START_OTA` via the phone app.
    *   Copy and paste a new Python script into the terminal.
    *   Send `STOP_OTA`.
    *   Watch the Serial Monitor; the Pico should reboot and start running the new logic immediately.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico W is running "Firmware v1.0".
2.  **Action**: User sends `START_OTA` via smartphone.
3.  **Process**: The Pico prepares its internal flash storage for incoming data.
4.  **Action**: The user streams the new source code over Bluetooth.
5.  **Result**: The file is closed. The Pico performs a "Hot Swap" and reboots.
6.  **Output**: "Remote Update: RECEIVED | Verification: PASS | State: BOOTING v2.0".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import aioble
import bluetooth
import machine
import os

# (NUS UUID Setup from 1571)
_SVC_UUID = bluetooth.UUID("6E400001-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")
_RX_UUID  = bluetooth.UUID("6E400002-B5A3-F393-E0A9-E50E24DCCA9E")

async def ota_uart_task():
    service = aioble.Service(_SVC_UUID)
    rx_char = aioble.Characteristic(service, _RX_UUID, write=True, capture=True)
    aioble.register_services(service)
    
    ota_active = False
    ota_file = None

    while True:
        async with await aioble.advertise(200_000, name="Pico-OTA", services=[_SVC_UUID]) as conn:
            print("[OTA] Ready for updates.")
            while conn.is_connected():
                await rx_char.written()
                data = rx_char.read().decode().strip()
                
                # 1. COMMAND: START
                if data == "OTA_START":
                    print("[OTA] Opening flash buffer...")
                    ota_file = open("update.py", "w")
                    ota_active = True
                    continue
                
                # 2. COMMAND: END
                if data == "OTA_END":
                    if ota_file:
                        ota_file.close()
                        print("[OTA] Transfer complete. Installing...")
                        
                        # Swap files: update.py -> main.py
                        try:
                            os.remove("main.py")
                        except: pass
                        os.rename("update.py", "main.py")
                        
                        print("[OTA] REBOOTING...")
                        machine.reset()
                
                # 3. DATA TRANSFER
                if ota_active and ota_file:
                    ota_file.write(data + "\n")
                    print(".", end="") # Visual progress bar
            
            # Reset flag if phone disconnects mid-transfer
            ota_active = False
            if ota_file: ota_file.close()

# BOOT
asyncio.run(ota_uart_task())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Bricking Risk**: If you rename a partial file to `main.py` and it contains a syntax error, the Pico W will crash on boot. Always keep a "Rescue Mode" or use A/B partitioning where the old code is preserved until the NEW code signals it has started successfully.
*   **Flash Latency**: Writing to flash is slower than the radio. If you send data too fast without `await asyncio.sleep_ms(20)`, the file write could lag, causing a radio timeout.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Batch 159**: We have mastered Bluetooth. Now step into the final frontier: **Swarm Robotics** using the low-latency **ESP-NOW Radio Protocol** for Pico-to-Pico communication.
