## Batch 159: Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio (1581-1590)

## 1. Project 1581: Professional ESP-NOW Radio Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
Break free from the constraints of WiFi Routers and Bluetooth pairing by implementing **ESP-NOW**, a connectionless WiFi-based protocol that allows two Raspberry Pi Pico W units to communicate directly with ultra-low latency and a range of up to 200 meters.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Connectionless Communication**: Unlike standard WiFi, ESP-NOW does not require a handshake or a router; it "shouts" data directly into the air for other Picos to hear.
*   **MAC Address Identity**: Using the unique permanent hardware ID of each Pico W as its "Street Address" for targeted delivery.
*   **Broadcast Mode**: Sending a message to every Pico in range simultaneously using the universal address `FF:FF:FF:FF:FF:FF`.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W (Node A)** | Internal | Wireless Transmitter |
| **Pico W (Node B)** | Internal | Wireless Receiver |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_esp_now`** (Radio activation)
*   **from Network, drag `add_peer_broadcast`** (Open channel)
*   **from Network, drag `send_esp_now_message`** (Trigger transmission)
*   **from Control, drag `on_message_received`** (Event listener)

### 7. Variables
*   **radio**: Object (The ESP-NOW instance).
*   **payload**: String (The message content).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Schema Phase (Initialization)**
1.  **Activate WiFi Radio**:
    *   Initialize the WLAN in `network.STA_IF` mode.
    *   Set `active(True)`. Note: Do **not** call `connect()`.
2.  **Initialize ESP-NOW**:
    *   Create the `espnow.ESPNow()` object.
    *   Call `active(True)`.

**B. Logic Phase (Broadcast)**
3.  **The Universal Peer**:
    *   To send a message to *everyone*, you must first "Add a Peer".
    *   Use the broadcast MAC address: `b'\xff\xff\xff\xff\xff\xff'`.
4.  **The Transmission Loop**:
    *   Inside a `while True` loop, use the `send()` block to dispatch a "Hello World" packet every 1000ms.

**C. Verification Phase (Receiver)**
5.  **Monitor the Waves**:
    *   On the second Pico, run the listening script.
    *   Use `radio.any()` to check if data is waiting.
    *   Wait for the output: `From [MAC]: Hello World`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico A powers up its WiFi antenna in "Raw Mode".
2.  **Process**: Pico A registers the global "Everyone" address.
3.  **Action**: Every second, a raw WiFi packet is injected into the air.
4.  **Sense**: Pico B filters the air for ESP-NOW packets and captures them.
5.  **Result**: Instant P2P communication without a router.
6.  **Output**: "Radio: ONLINE | Protocol: ESP-NOW | Scope: BROADCAST".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import espnow
import time

# 1. Hardware Radio Setup
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
wlan.active(True)

# 2. ESP-NOW Driver Setup
radio = espnow.ESPNow()
radio.active(True)

# 3. Add the BROADCAST Address (Everyone)
# \xff is hex for 255. Six bytes of 255 = Broadcast MAC.
everyone = b'\xff\xff\xff\xff\xff\xff'
radio.add_peer(everyone)

print("Pico Radio Station is LIVE.")

while True:
    # 4. DISPATCH DATA
    print("Broadcasting Ping...")
    radio.send(everyone, "PING_SIGNAL")
    
    # Check for replies
    if radio.any():
        host, msg = radio.recv()
        if msg:
            print(f"Heard from {host.hex()}: {msg.decode()}")
            
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **WLAN Inactive**: If you don't call `wlan.active(True)` first, the ESP-NOW driver will crash because the physical hardware isn't powered on.
*   **Duplicate Peers**: Trying to `add_peer()` the same address twice will cause an `OSError`. Always wrap your Peer discovery in a `try/except` block or check before adding.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1582**: Broadcast is "shouting". Now learn how to "whisper" to one specific Pico using **Unicast** and manage responses **Asynchronously** to keep your CPU free.

---

## 1. Project 1582: Asynchronous Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
Upgrade your radio link to handle multiple data streams simultaneously using **Unicast Communication** (Point-to-Point) and an **Asynchronous Receiver** that processes incoming packets without blocking the Pico's main code loop.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Unicast vs Broadcast**: Sending a message to one specific MAC address instead of everyone, reducing network noise.
*   **Event-Driven Radio**: Using `asyncio` to "wait" for a packet in the background while performing other tasks (like blinking an LED).
*   **ACK (Acknowledgment)**: Verifying that the target Pico actually received the message.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Local Node** | Internal | Station 1 |
| **Remote Node** | Internal | Station 2 |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `wait_for_esp_now`** (Async listener)
*   **from Control, drag `create_task`** (Parallel processing)
*   **from Network, drag `get_mac_address`** (Identify self)

### 7. Variables
*   **other_pico**: Byte String (The target's MAC).
*   **status_flag**: Integer (Process state).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Handshake Phase**
1.  **Retrieve MAC Addresses**:
    *   Run `print(network.WLAN().config('mac').hex())` on both Picos.
    *   Copy the result from Pico B into the script of Pico A.
2.  **Add Target**:
    *   Use `radio.add_peer(TARGET_MAC)` to "lock on" to the other device.

**B. Logic Phase (Async)**
3.  **The Receiver Task**:
    *   Create an `async def listener()` function.
    *   Inside a `while True`, use `radio.irecv()` or `await radio.any()`.
    *   When data arrives, print it and immediately send back an `"OK"` signal.
4.  **The Main Loop**:
    *   Run `asyncio.create_task(listener())` to keep the radio ear open 24/7.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Bidirectional Test**:
    *   Pico A sends "CMD_ON".
    *   Pico B receives it (in the background) and replies "OK".
    *   Pico A receives "OK".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes the radio and starts a background "Ear" process.
2.  **Action**: The foreground "Brain" decides to send a command.
3.  **Process**: The radio hardware targets only the specific MAC address of the peer.
4.  **Sense**: The peer's "Ear" detects a targeted packet and wakes up the responder logic.
5.  **Result**: Low-latency command/response and zero interference with other code tasks.
6.  **Output**: "Target: LOCKED | Mode: ASYNC | Response: BI-DIRECTIONAL".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import espnow
import uasyncio as asyncio

# Configuration - FILL IN MAC FROM YOUR PICO!
REMOTE_PICO = b'\x28\xcd\xc1\x00\x11\x22' 

class AsyncRadio:
    def __init__(self):
        wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
        wlan.active(True)
        self.e = espnow.ESPNow()
        self.e.active(True)
        try:
            self.e.add_peer(REMOTE_PICO)
        except: pass

    async def listener(self):
        print("[RADIO] Listening for targeted messages...")
        while True:
            if self.e.any():
                host, msg = self.e.recv()
                if msg:
                    data = msg.decode()
                    print(f"[RX] From {host.hex()}: {data}")
                    # Auto-Responder
                    if data == "PING":
                        self.e.send(host, "PONG")
            await asyncio.sleep(0.01) # Yield to other tasks

    async def sender(self):
        while True:
            print("[TX] Sending Unicast PING...")
            try:
                self.e.send(REMOTE_PICO, "PING")
            except Exception as e:
                print(f"[ERR] Failed to send: {e}")
            await asyncio.sleep(2)

async def main():
    radio_system = AsyncRadio()
    # Run both tasks simultaneously
    asyncio.create_task(radio_system.listener())
    asyncio.create_task(radio_system.sender())
    
    # Simulate main application logic
    while True:
        await asyncio.sleep(1)

asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Sleep**: If you use `time.sleep()` instead of `await asyncio.sleep()`, the entire ESP-NOW receiver will "freeze" during the sleep duration, potentially causing buffer overflows.
*   **Unregistered Peer**: You **cannot** send a unicast message to a MAC address until you have called `add_peer()` for that specific address first.

## 1. Project 1583: Object-Oriented Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio Class

### 2. Learning Objective
Transition from raw scripts to scalable engineering by encapsulating radio logic into an **Object-Oriented Radio Class**. You will create a `PicoMesh` class that handles peer management, error logging, and data packing automatically, allowing you to focus on your application's "what" instead of the radio's "how".

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Encapsulation**: Hiding the complex `network` and `espnow` setup inside a class to keep your main script clean.
*   **Data Serialization**: Using `struct` or `json` to prepare complex variables (integers, floats, booleans) for transmission as a single byte-bundle.
*   **Stateful Peers**: Maintaining a list of "Known Friends" within the class memory.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Local Node** | Internal | Encapsulated Logic Controller |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `Define Class`** (Object structure)
*   **from Network, drag `Radio Constructor`** (Setup inside init)
*   **from Data, drag `Pack Struct`** (Binary serialization)

### 7. Variables
*   **mesh_node**: Instance (The custom class).
*   **peer_list**: List (Registry of MACs).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Structural Phase**
1.  **Define the Constructor (`__init__`)**:
    *   Inside the class, initialize WLAN and ESP-NOW.
    *   Create an internal registry list `self.peers = []`.
2.  **Define Method `register(mac)`**:
    *   Add logic to `add_peer()` the MAC and store it in the internal list for tracking.

**B. Logic Phase**
3.  **Define Method `publish(msg)`**:
    *   Loop through the internal `self.peers` list and send the message to every registered friend.
4.  **Define Method `read()`**:
    *   Wrap `recv()` in a cleaner interface that strips metadata and returns only the message string.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Interface Test**:
    *   Instantiate `node = PicoMesh()`.
    *   Call `node.register(MAC_B)`.
    *   Call `node.publish("Class Test")`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The class is born, automatically waking up the WiFi drivers in the background.
2.  **Process**: The developer registers friends without touching low-level network blocks.
3.  **Action**: `node.publish()` is called.
4.  **Logic**: The class handles the loop, the `send()` triggers, and any error catch-all.
5.  **Result**: Modular, readable code that can be easily moved between projects.
6.  **Output**: "Status: OOP ACTIVE | Peers: REGISTERED | Interface: CLEAN".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import espnow
import struct

class PicoMeshNode:
    def __init__(self, name="Pico"):
        self.name = name
        # 1. Setup Drivers
        self.wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF)
        self.wlan.active(True)
        self.e = espnow.ESPNow()
        self.e.active(True)
        self.peers = []
        
        print(f"[{self.name}] Radio Class Initialized.")
        print(f"[{self.name}] My MAC: {self.wlan.config('mac').hex()}")

    def register_friend(self, mac_bytes):
        """Adds a peer safely and tracks them"""
        try:
            self.e.add_peer(mac_bytes)
            self.peers.append(mac_bytes)
            print(f"Registered Peer: {mac_bytes.hex()}")
        except:
            pass # Already registered

    def announce(self, text):
        """Send a string message to every registered peer"""
        payload = text.encode()
        for p in self.peers:
            try:
                self.e.send(p, payload)
            except Exception as err:
                print(f"Send Error to {p.hex()}: {err}")

    def listen(self):
        """Polls for messages and returns (decoded_msg, sender_mac)"""
        if self.e.any():
            host, msg = self.e.recv()
            if msg:
                return msg.decode(), host
        return None, None

# --- Application Layer ---
node = PicoMeshNode(name="SensorCore")

# Define our peer target (Use your specific MAC here)
FRIEND_MAC = b'\x28\xcd\xc1\x00\x11\x22'
node.register_friend(FRIEND_MAC)

# Periodic Action simulation
import time
while True:
    node.announce("LOG:STATUS_OK")
    
    # Check receiver ear
    msg, sender = node.listen()
    if msg:
        print(f"Data from {sender.hex()}: {msg}")
        
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Variable Scope**: If you don't save the WLAN object as `self.wlan` (using `self`), it might be garbage collected by MicroPython to save memory, causing the radio to shut down unexpectedly.
*   **Memory Overhead**: Classes consume more RAM than raw scripts. On a standard Pico W (without SPIRAM), keep your class definitions slim to avoid `MemoryError`.

*   **Endianness**: Using `<` (Little Endian) on Pico is standard. If talking to a Big Endian server, use `>`.  
*   **Buffer Limit**: ESP-NOW max payload is ~250 bytes. Keep your custom packet small!

"Ack/Retry". Send message. Wait for Ack. If timeout, resend. Max 3 retries.

**Hardware Requirements:**
*   2 Picos

**Expected Behavior:**
*   Reliable delivery transport.

**Solution:**

def send_reliable(msg):
    for i in range(3):
        e.send(peer, msg)
        if wait_for_ack(timeout=100ms):
            return True
    return False
``

## 1. Project 1584: Robust Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
Eliminate data loss in wireless environments by implementing a **Reliable Delivery Protocol** for ESP-NOW. You will build a "Stop-and-Wait ARQ" (Automatic Repeat Request) system that automatically retries failed transmissions and uses Acknowledgment (ACK) packets to confirm success.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **ACK Handshake**: A small return packet sent by the receiver to say "I got it!".
*   **Exponential Retries**: Attempting to resend a message up to 3 or 5 times before declaring a network failure.
*   **Aync Timeouts**: Using `asyncio.wait_for` to prevent a script from hanging forever if a peer disappears.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Node A** | Internal | Station with ACK logic |
| **Node B** | Internal | Station with Auto-Responder |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Control, drag `Loop with Timeout`** (Retry logic)
*   **from Logic, drag `Event Wait`** (ACK detection)
*   **from IoT, drag `send_esp_now_ack`** (Verification signal)

### 7. Variables
*   **max_tries**: Integer (Limit of attempts).
*   **ack_flag**: Boolean (Success status).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Transmission Phase (Sender)**
1.  **Define the Reliable Sender**:
    *   Create a loop that runs 3 times.
    *   Inside the loop, send the message and then **wait** for a global "ACK" variable to become true.
    *   If 200ms pass without an ACK, `continue` to the next loop iteration.

**B. Logic Phase (Receiver)**
2.  **Define the Auto-Responder**:
    *   When any packet is received, immediately use `radio.send(host, "ACK")`.
    *   Note: Always send the ACK *before* processing the message to ensure the sender can unblock as fast as possible.

**C. Verification Phase**
3.  **Stress Test**:
    *   Cover one Pico's antenna with your hand or move them apart.
    *   Observe the Serial Monitor: The Pico should print `Retry 1...`, `Retry 2...`, then eventually `Delivered!` once the signal clears.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico A sends a "CMD:LOAD" signal to Pico B.
2.  **Process**: Pico A enters a "Waiting" state, pausing its next action.
3.  **Sense**: Pico B captures the signal and instantly fires back an "ACK" byte.
4.  **Result**: Pico A hears the ACK, marks the task as DONE, and moves to the next line.
5.  **Output**: "Protocol: RELIABLE ARQ | Latency: 12ms | Reliability: 99.9%".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
import espnow
import network

# Setup
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
e = espnow.ESPNow(); e.active(True)
PEER_MAC = b'\x28\xcd\xc1\x00\x11\x22'
try: e.add_peer(PEER_MAC)
except: pass

# Global Event to signal ACK arrival
ack_event = asyncio.Event()

async def reliable_send(msg, retries=5):
    """Sends a message and waits for ACK before returning"""
    for i in range(retries):
        print(f"[TX] Attempt {i+1}: Sending {msg}...")
        e.send(PEER_MAC, msg)
        
        try:
            # Wait 250ms for the ACK event
            await asyncio.wait_for(ack_event.wait(), 0.25)
            print("[INFO] ACK Received. Message delivered.")
            ack_event.clear()
            return True
        except asyncio.TimeoutError:
            print("[WARN] ACK Timeout. Retrying...")
            
    print("[ERROR] Failed to deliver after max retries.")
    return False

async def listener_task():
    """Handles incoming ACKs and regular messages"""
    while True:
        if e.any():
            host, msg = e.recv()
            if msg == b'ACK':
                ack_event.set()
            else:
                print(f"[RX] Message: {msg.decode()}")
                # Respond with ACK
                e.send(host, b'ACK')
        await asyncio.sleep(0.01)

async def main():
    asyncio.create_task(listener_task())
    await asyncio.sleep(1)
    
    # Test our robustness
    success = await reliable_send("TRIGGER_EVENT")
    if success:
        print("System synchronized.")

asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **ACK Storms**: If your receiver sends an "ACK" to an "ACK", you will create an infinite loop of radio signals. Ensure the receiver only sends an ACK when the message content is *not* "ACK".
*   **Blocking Main**: If you run the Sender loop without `asyncio.sleep` or `wait_for`, the receiver task might never get time to process the incoming ACK, causing a "False Timeout".

## 1. Project 1585: Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio with Error Handling

### 2. Learning Objective
Engineer a self-healing radio link that survives environmental interference by implementing **Global Channel Hopping**. If the radio link becomes "noisy" on Channel 1 (e.g., due to a nearby Router), both Picos will automatically jump to Channel 6 or 11 to find clear air.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Frequency Agility**: The ability of a radio system to switch between the 13 available WiFi channels to avoid jamming.
*   **Sync Sequence**: Both Picos must follow a pre-agreed channel list (e.g., `[1, 6, 11]`) so they don't get lost on different frequencies.
*   **Heartbeat Loss**: Using the absence of data as a trigger to start "Searching" for the peer.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Adaptive Frequency Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `set_wifi_channel`** (Frequency switch)
*   **from Time, drag `timestamp`** (Drift check)
*   **from Control, drag `on_heartbeat_fail`** (Healing trigger)

### 7. Variables
*   **channel_index**: Integer (Pointer to list).
*   **last_seen**: Float (Time of last packet).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Configuration Phase**
1.  **Define the Pre-Agreed List**:
    *   Create a variable `CHANNELS = [1, 6, 11]` (the three non-overlapping channels).
2.  **Define the Hop Logic**:
    *   Create a function `hop()`. Use `wlan.config(channel=...)` to change the frequency.

**B. Logic Phase (The Search)**
3.  **The Watchdog**:
    *   Inside the main loop, check `time.time() - last_seen`.
    *   If more than 5 seconds pass without hearing the partner, call `hop()`.
4.  **The Sender**:
    *   If `reliable_send` (from 1584) fails all 5 retries, call `hop()` immediately and try again.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Interference Test**:
    *   Manually change Channel on ONLY one Pico.
    *   Observe the logs. Within 5-10 seconds, the second Pico should "chase" the first one through the channels until they reconnect.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Both units are talking clearly on Channel 1.
2.  **Event**: Interference occurs. No ACKs are arriving.
3.  **Process**: Pico A (Sender) hops to Ch 6. It shouts "Hello?". No one is there.
4.  **Sense**: Pico B (Receiver) realizes it's been silent for 5s. It hops to Ch 6.
5.  **Result**: Pico A whispers "Hello?", Pico B responds "ACK!".
6.  **Output**: "Connectivity: RESTORED | Channel: 6 | Mode: AUTO-RECOVERY".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import espnow
import time

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
e = espnow.ESPNow(); e.active(True)
e.add_peer(b'\xff'*6) # Broadcast for initial search

CHANNELS = [1, 6, 11]
chan_idx = 0
last_packet_time = time.time()

def set_channel(idx):
    ch = CHANNELS[idx]
    print(f"[RADIO] Jumping to Channel {ch}...")
    wlan.config(channel=ch)

while True:
    # 1. Receiver Logic
    if e.any():
        host, msg = e.recv()
        last_packet_time = time.time()
        print(f"Got: {msg}")

    # 2. SEFL-HEALING WATCHDOG
    # If silence for 5 seconds, assume we are on wrong channel
    if time.time() - last_packet_time > 5:
        print("[WARN] Link lost. Scanning frequencies...")
        chan_idx = (chan_idx + 1) % len(CHANNELS)
        set_channel(chan_idx)
        last_packet_time = time.time() # Reset timer for this new channel
        
    # 3. Sender Heartbeat
    try:
        e.send(b'\xff'*6, "BEACON")
    except: pass
    
    time.sleep(0.5)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Hopping too fast**: If you change channels every 100ms, you will likely skip over the partner while they are sending. Keep "Search time" at least 1-2 seconds per channel.
*   **Firmware Mismatch**: On some older MicroPython versions, you must call `e.active(False)` and then `True` after changing a channel for the settings to stick.

## 1. Project 1586: Optimized Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
Optimize your wireless network for high-density environments using **TDMA (Time Division Multiple Access)**. You will learn how to synchronize the "clocks" of multiple Picos so they take turns speaking in specific time slots, preventing radio collisions and "shouting matches".

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Time Division**: Splitting one second into "Slots" (e.g., 0-500ms for Pico A, 500-1000ms for Pico B).
*   **Master Sync**: One Pico acting as the "Conductor" to keep everyone's timers aligned.
*   **Modulo Slot Logic**: Using mathematical `time % slot_count` to decide whose turn it is.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W (3+ recommended to see the "orderly" queue)
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Node 0 (Master)** | Internal | Synchoronization Leader |
| **Node 1 (Slave)** | Internal | Scheduled Responder |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Time, drag `ticks_ms`** (High-res timer)
*   **from Math, drag `modulo %`** (Slot calculator)
*   **from Control, drag `wait_for_next_slot`** (Precise timing)

### 7. Variables
*   **my_slot_id**: Integer (0 or 1).
*   **cycle_ms**: 1000 (Total time for one full round).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Synchronication Phase**
1.  **Set the ID**:
    *   Assign `MY_ID = 0` on first Pico, `MY_ID = 1` on the second.
2.  **Define the Slot Size**:
    *   Set `SLOT_TIME = 500`ms. (Total cycle = 1000ms).

**B. Logic Phase (The Schedule)**
3.  **The Turn Calculator**:
    *   Calculate `current_cycle_pos = time.ticks_ms() % 1000`.
    *   `if current_cycle_pos < 500` and `MY_ID == 0`: It's my turn.
    *   `if current_cycle_pos >= 500` and `MY_ID == 1`: It's my turn.
4.  **Quiet Mode**:
    *   If it is NOT your turn, your code **must** stay in Receiver Mode and never call `send()`.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **Traffic Analysis**:
    *   Watch two Picos side-by-side.
    *   Observe that they alternate their LEDs or print statements perfectly, even if they are both trying to send data as fast as possible.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: All Picos check the millisecond timer of the CPU.
2.  **Process**: Pico 0 sees it is in the 0-499ms window.
3.  **Action**: Pico 0 sends 10 packets of data. Pico 1 remains silent.
4.  **Sync**: Time hits 500ms.
5.  **Action**: Pico 0 stops. Pico 1 starts sending its status.
6.  **Output**: "Efficiency: 100% | Collisions: 0 | Capacity: N-Nodes".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import time
import espnow
import network

# CONFIGURATION
MY_ID = 0           # Node 0 or Node 1
TOTAL_NODES = 2
SLOT_MS = 500       # Each node gets 0.5 seconds
CYCLE_MS = TOTAL_NODES * SLOT_MS

wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
e = espnow.ESPNow(); e.active(True)
e.add_peer(b'\xff'*6)

print(f"Node {MY_ID} Ready. Cycle: {CYCLE_MS}ms")

while True:
    now = time.ticks_ms()
    # Which slot are we currently in?
    current_slot = (now // SLOT_MS) % TOTAL_NODES
    
    if current_slot == MY_ID:
        # --- TRANSMIT WINDOW ---
        print(f"[TURN] Node {MY_ID} is speaking...")
        try:
            e.send(b'\xff'*6, f"DATA_FROM_{MY_ID}")
        except: pass
        
        # Calculate time to end of slot to avoid 'double-sending'
        time.sleep(0.4) # Sleep for most of my slot to give CPU a break
        
    else:
        # --- RECEIVE WINDOW ---
        if e.any():
            host, msg = e.recv()
            print(f"[RECV] Heard {msg.decode()}")
        time.sleep_ms(10) # Fast poll while listening
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Clock Drift**: Internal clocks are not perfect. After 30 minutes, they might drift by 100ms and start colliding. **Fix**: Have Node 0 send a "SYNC" signal every 10 seconds to reset everyone's timer.
*   **Gap time**: Always leave a "Guard Gap" of 10-20ms between slots to allow for radio propagation and CPU processing delay.

## 1. Project 1587: Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
Build a high-throughput **Wireless Telemetry Pipeline** for streaming complex sensor data between Picos. You will learn to bypass the overhead of strings and JSON by using **Binary Serialization** with `struct`, allowing for faster updates and lower memory usage.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Binary Packing**: Converting variables (Floats, Ints) directly into a memory buffer instead of converting them to text first.
*   **Payload Structuring**: Designing a fixed-length radio frame (e.g., 2 Floats = 8 Bytes).
*   **Sample Rate Optimization**: Achieving update frequencies of 50Hz+ for real-time motor/display feedback.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Collector Node** | Internal | Sensor Data Producer |
| **Display Node** | Internal | Telemetry Consumer |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `Pack Binary`** (Float packing)
*   **from Data, drag `Unpack Binary`** (Float extraction)
*   **from IoT, drag `esp_now_stream`** (High-speed radio)

### 7. Variables
*   **temp_sensor**: Float (Simulated/Physical).
*   **packet_buffer**: Bytes (Binary data).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Producer Phase (Sender)**
1.  **Read the Sensors**:
    *   Initialize variables for `temperature` and `humidity`.
2.  **Pack the Buffer**:
    *   Use `struct.pack("<ff", temp, hum)`. The `"ff"` tells the Pico to treat both as 4-byte floats. The `<` ensures Little Endian.
3.  **Dispatch**:
    *   Send the 8-byte buffer to the peer's MAC address.

**B. Consumer Phase (Receiver)**
4.  **Listen and Validate**:
    *   When a packet arrives, check `len(msg)`. If it is exactly 8 bytes, proceed.
5.  **Unpack the Data**:
    *   Use `struct.unpack("<ff", msg)` to restore the two original float values.

**C. Verification Phase**
6.  **Real-Time Monitor**:
    *   Watch the Receiver. Even if the sensors move by 0.01 units, the value should update on the terminal screen instantly.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Pico A samples the environment.
2.  **Process**: The CPU converts the high-precision decimal into raw binary ones and zeros.
3.  **Action**: The radio sends a tiny 8-byte frame (highly efficient).
4.  **Sense**: Pico B receives the bits and translates them back into a human-readable float.
5.  **Result**: High-speed, high-precision telemetry with no "text parsing" lag.
6.  **Output**: "Stream: ACTIVE | Resolution: 32-bit Float | Frequency: 20Hz".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import network
import espnow
import struct
import random
import time

# Hardware Setup
w0 = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); w0.active(True)
e = espnow.ESPNow(); e.active(True)
PEER = b'\x28\xcd\xc1\x00\x11\x22'
try: e.add_peer(PEER)
except: pass

def run_pipeline():
    print("Wireless Data Pipeline: STARTING")
    while True:
        # 1. GENERATE DATA
        t = 22.5 + random.uniform(-1, 1)
        h = 45.0 + random.uniform(-5, 5)
        
        # 2. PACK TO BINARY (2x Floats = 8 Bytes)
        try:
            payload = struct.pack("<ff", t, h)
            e.send(PEER, payload)
        except Exception as err:
            print(f"Pipeline Clogged: {err}")
            
        # 3. FAST SAMPLE RATE (50ms)
        time.sleep(0.05)

# --- RECEIVER VERSION (Uncomment to use on second Pico) ---
# def run_display():
#     while True:
#         if e.any():
#             host, msg = e.recv()
#             if len(msg) == 8: # Validation
#                 temp, hum = struct.unpack("<ff", msg)
#                 print(f"TELEMETRY -> Temp: {temp:.2f}C | Hum: {hum:.1f}%")
#         time.sleep(0.01)

run_pipeline()
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Endian Mismatch**: If you use `<` (Little Endian) on the sender but no prefix (or `>`) on the receiver, the numbers will come out as complete gibberish (e.g., `22.5` becomes `1.23e+30`). Always use `<` for Pico-to-Pico.
*   **Buffer Size**: If you send 12 bytes but try to unpack "ff" (8 bytes), it will cause an `Exception`. Use `try/except` for robustness.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1588**: This pipeline is open for anyone to sniff. Learn to wrap your data in **AES Encryption** for military-grade security.

## 1. Project 1588: Secure Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
Protect your wireless communication from eavesdropping and hijacking by implementing **Application-Layer AES-128 Encryption**. You will learn to use the hardware-accelerated `ucryptolib` to encrypt your ESP-NOW packets with a shared secret key.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Symmetric Encryption**: Both Picos share a 16-byte "Master Key".
*   **Initialization Vector (IV)**: A random 16-byte prefix that ensures the same command (e.g., "STOP") looks different every time it is sent.
*   **CTR Mode (Counter)**: A fast stream cipher mode ideal for low-power wireless packets.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Secure Pico** | Internal | Encrypted Channel Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `AES Encrypt`** (ucryptolib init)
*   **from System, drag `Get Random Bytes`** (IV generation)
*   **from Data, drag `Slicing`** (Header removal)

### 7. Variables
*   **master_key**: 16-byte Secret.
*   **cipher_text**: Bytes (Gibberish).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Encryption Phase (Sender)**
1.  **Define the Key**:
    *   Set `KEY = b'1234567890123456'` (Exactly 16 characters).
2.  **Generate the IV**:
    *   Use `os.urandom(16)` to create a fresh IV for *every* packet.
3.  **Wrap the Data**:
    *   Initialize `aes(KEY, 6, IV)` (Mode 6 = CTR).
    *   Encrypt the payload. Send `IV + CIPHERTEXT`.

**B. Decryption Phase (Receiver)**
4.  **Extract the IV**:
    *   The first 16 bytes represent the IV. The rest is the secret data.
5.  **Unwrap**:
    *   Initialize `aes(KEY, 6, extracted_IV)`.
    *   Decrypt and convert back to a string.

**C. Verification Phase**
6.  **The Sniffer Test**:
    *   Look at the serial output on a regular Pico receiver without the key. It should only see unreadable binary noise.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: User clicks "Open Door" in the app.
2.  **Process**: The Pico generates a random salt (IV) and scrambles the text into a 32-byte block.
3.  **Action**: The scrambled noise is sent over the air.
4.  **Sense**: The authorized receiver uses the IV and Key to unscramble the code.
5.  **Result**: Secure control that cannot be replayed or read by attackers.
6.  **Output**: "Security: AES-128 | Mode: CTR | Status: ENCRYPTED".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import ucryptolib
import os
import network
import espnow

# MUST BE 16 BYTES!
MASTER_KEY = b'Antigravity_Safe'

class SecureRadio:
    def __init__(self, key):
        self.key = key
        self.wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); self.wlan.active(True)
        self.e = espnow.ESPNow(); self.e.active(True)

    def encrypt_and_send(self, peer, plaintext):
        iv = os.urandom(16)
        # Mode 6 = CTR (No padding needed)
        cipher = ucryptolib.aes(self.key, 6, iv)
        ciphertext = cipher.encrypt(plaintext)
        # We MUST send the IV along with the data so receiver can decrypt
        self.e.send(peer, iv + ciphertext)

    def decrypt_received(self, raw_msg):
        if len(raw_msg) < 17: return None
        iv = raw_msg[:16]
        data = raw_msg[16:]
        cipher = ucryptolib.aes(self.key, 6, iv)
        return cipher.decrypt(data)

# Usage
sec = SecureRadio(MASTER_KEY)
PEER = b'\x28\xcd\xc1\x00\x11\x22'
try: sec.e.add_peer(PEER)
except: pass

print("Shields up. Sending secure command...")
sec.encrypt_and_send(PEER, "ACTIVATE_SECURITY_ALARM")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Reusing IV**: If you use a static IV, an attacker can detect patterns (Replay Attacks). Always use `urandom`.
*   **Data Length**: CTR mode handles any length, but CBC mode (Mode 2) requires the data to be a multiple of 16 bytes. Stick to CTR for radio packets.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1589**: Encryption adds processing delay. Move your radio listener to **Core 1 (Multi-Threading)** to ensure encryption/decryption doesn't slow down your robot's sensors on Core 0.

## 1. Project 1589: Multi-Threaded Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio

### 2. Learning Objective
Achieve maximum system reliability by implementing a **Multi-Core Radio Hub**. You will offload the ESP-NOW receiver and polling logic to Core 1, allowing Core 0 (the Main Brain) to handle complex PID motor control or calculations without ever dropping a wireless packet.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Core Affinity**: Assigning the high-speed "Radio Ear" to the second CPU core.
*   **Thread Safety**: Using `_thread.allocate_lock()` (Mutex) to prevent two cores from trying to read/write the same memory list at once.
*   **Producer-Consumer Pattern**: Core 1 "Produces" messages; Core 0 "Consumes" them.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   2x Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   2x Micro USB Cables

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Dual-Core Orchestration Node |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `Run Thread`** (_thread implementation)
*   **from Logic, drag `Acquire Lock`** (Memory protection)
*   **from Data, drag `Global List`** (The Bridge)

### 7. Variables
*   **inbox**: List (The shared queue).
*   **mem_lock**: Object (The mutex).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Core 1 Phase (The Listener)**
1.  **Define the Radio Loop**:
    *   Create `radio_thread()`. Inside, run `while True`.
    *   Listen for `e.recv()`. When data arrives, **lock** the inbox, `append` the message, and **unlock**.
2.  **Spawn**:
    *   Use `_thread.start_new_thread(radio_thread, ())`.

**B. Core 0 Phase (The Brain)**
3.  **Process Work**:
    *   Inside the main loop, perform a heavy task (e.g., `time.sleep(2)` simulation).
4.  **Check the Box**:
    *   After the task, **lock** the inbox, `pop` all messages, and **unlock**.

**C. Verification Phase**
5.  **The Delay Test**:
    *   Send 5 pulses from the remote Pico while the Local Pico is "Sleeping" (in its 2s heavy task).
    *   Observe that the Local Pico still remembers and processes all 5 pulses immediately after waking up.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Core 0 wakes up Core 1 and tells it to "Listen 24/7".
2.  **Action**: Core 0 begins a long, complex calculation.
3.  **Process**: A wireless command arrives. Core 1 captures it and stores it in a safe list.
4.  **Sense**: Core 0 finishes its work and looks at the list.
5.  **Result**: Zero packet loss even during heavy CPU load.
6.  **Output**: "Platform: DUAL-CORE | Core 1: IO-WAITER | Core 0: LOGIC-EXEC".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import _thread
import time
import espnow
import network

# 1. Setup Drivers
wlan = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); wlan.active(True)
e = espnow.ESPNow(); e.active(True)
e.add_peer(b'\xff'*6)

# 2. Shared Memory Infrastructure
inbox = []
lock = _thread.allocate_lock()

def radio_core_one():
    """EXCLUSIVELY RUNS ON CORE 1"""
    print("[C1] Radio Daemon LIVE.")
    while True:
        if e.any():
            host, msg = e.recv()
            if msg:
                with lock:
                    inbox.append((host, msg))
        time.sleep_ms(10) # Prevent Core 1 from overheating

# 3. Launch Thread
_thread.start_new_thread(radio_core_one, ())

# --- CORE 0: MAIN APP ---
while True:
    print("[C0] Performing heavy calculation...")
    time.sleep(3) # Imagine this is 3 seconds of AI or PID data
    
    # Check if Core 1 picked up any mail
    with lock:
        while inbox:
            sender, data = inbox.pop(0)
            print(f"[C0] Processing late message from {sender.hex()}: {data}")

    print("[C0] Cycle complete.")
```

**11. Common Mistakes:**  
*   **Memory Race**: Modifying a global list from two cores without `lock` can corrupt the list structure (though rare in simple appends, it's best practice).  
*   **Printing**: Printing from Core 1 can crash the IDE connection. Try to only print from Core 0.  

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Project 1590**: The Grand Finale. A **Swarm Logic**. Five Picos turn on. They vote. One becomes the "Leader" and flashes Blue. The others become "Followers" and flash Green. If the Leader dies, they re-elect.

## 1. Project 1590: Industrial Professional Pico-to-Pico Radio Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
Orchestrate a self-organizing network by implementing a **Leader Election (Bully Algorithm)** for swarm robotics. You will learn how to make multiple Picos "vote" on who is in charge, allowing the swarm to maintain a single "Master" even if the original leader is powered off or disconnected.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Decentralized Consensus**: Making decisions without a central computer; every node has equal "voting rights".
*   **Heartbeat Monitoring**: Periodically checking if the Leader is still sending "I am alive" signals.
*   **State Machine Logic**: Switching between `ELECTION`, `LEADER`, and `FOLLOWER` states based on network events.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   3x Raspberry Pi Pico W (Standard Picos)
*   3x RGB LEDs (Optional, for visual status)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico W** | Internal | Swarm Node (Agent) |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Control, drag `Switch Case (States)`** (Finite State Machine)
*   **from System, drag `Random Integer`** (Unique ID generation)
*   **from Network, drag `ESP-NOW Heartbeat`** (Liveness check)

### 7. Variables
*   **my_rank**: Integer (Unique random ID).
*   **current_leader**: Integer (The ID to follow).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**A. Initialization Phase**
1.  **Generate Identity**:
    *   Set `MY_ID = random.randint(1, 10000)`.
2.  **Define the States**:
    *   `STATE_ELECTION = 0`, `STATE_LEADER = 1`, `STATE_FOLLOWER = 2`.

**B. Peer Logic (The Election)**
3.  **The Bully Pattern**:
    *   Every node shouts its ID into the air: `CLAIM:1234`.
    *   If a node hears a number *higher* than its own, it admits defeat and becomes a **Follower**.
    *   If 3 seconds pass and no one has shouted a higher number, the node declares itself **Leader**.

**C. Maintenance Phase**
4.  **Leaders Duty**:
    *   The Leader must shout `PULSE` every 500ms to keep the followers "occupied".
5.  **Followers Duty**:
    *   If a Follower doesn't hear a `PULSE` for 5 seconds, it assumes the leader "died" and restarts the `ELECTION` state.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Three Picos turn on. They all start in `ELECTION` mode.
2.  **Process**: Node A (ID 50), Node B (ID 99), Node C (ID 12).
3.  **Action**: Node B shouts 99. Node A and C see it, realize 99 > their IDs, and stop shouting.
4.  **Sense**: Node B shouts for 3 seconds, hears no higher ID.
5.  **Result**: Node B becomes Leader. Node A and C become Followers.
6.  **Output**: "Swarm: STABLE | Leader: ID 99 | Follower Count: 2".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import time
import random
import espnow
import network

# 1. Identity Setup
MY_ID = random.randint(1, 10000)
print(f"[NODE] Online. My ID is {MY_ID}")

# 2. State Definitions
ELECTION, LEADER, FOLLOWER = 0, 1, 2
state = ELECTION
current_leader = -1
last_pulse = time.time()

# 3. Radio Setup
w = network.WLAN(network.STA_IF); w.active(True)
e = espnow.ESPNow(); e.active(True)
BCAST = b'\xff' * 6
try: e.add_peer(BCAST)
except: pass

def process_radio():
    global state, current_leader, last_pulse
    while e.any():
        _, msg = e.recv()
        try:
            cmd, r_id = msg.decode().split(':')
            r_id = int(r_id)
            
            if cmd == "CLAIM":
                if r_id > MY_ID:
                    print(f"[EVENT] Yielding to higher ID: {r_id}")
                    state = FOLLOWER
                    current_leader = r_id
                    
            elif cmd == "PULSE":
                if r_id >= MY_ID:
                    state = FOLLOWER
                    current_leader = r_id
                    last_pulse = time.time()
        except: pass

while True:
    process_radio()
    
    if state == ELECTION:
        print("[STATE] Conducting Election...")
        e.send(BCAST, f"CLAIM:{MY_ID}")
        
        # If no one higher seen for 3s, promote self
        if time.time() - last_pulse > 3:
            print("[STATE] I am the new LEADER!")
            state = LEADER
            current_leader = MY_ID
            
    elif state == LEADER:
        # Maintenance Pulse
        e.send(BCAST, f"PULSE:{MY_ID}")
        time.sleep(0.5)
        
    elif state == FOLLOWER:
        # Watchdog: If Leader disappears for 5s, revolt
        if time.time() - last_pulse > 5:
            print("[ALERT] Leader lost. Starting new election!")
            state = ELECTION
            last_pulse = time.time()
            
    time.sleep(0.1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Small ID Ranges**: If you use `random(0, 5)`, two Picos might get the same ID, causing them to fight for leadership forever. Use a large range (1-10,000).
*   **Blocking Sleep**: If you use `time.sleep(5)` in your follower code, you won't hear any pulses that happen *during* that sleep, causing you to think the leader is dead when they are actually alive.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Batch 160**: You have mastered the air (Wireless). Now step into the world of industrial machinery and wired reliability with **Modbus RTU over RS-485**.

**11. Common Mistakes:**  
*   **No Random**: If everyone has ID=0, they loop forever. Ensure IDs are unique.  
*   **Network Congestion**: In a real swarm of 50, broadcast storms kill the network. Use "Random Backoff" (sleep random ms) before sending CLAIM.  

**12. Try This Next:**  
*   **Batch 160**: We have conquered Wireless (WiFi, BLE, ESP-NOW). Now let's return to the wired world of heavy industry. **Modbus RTU (RS-485)**. The language of Factories.

## Batch 160: Professional Modbus Industrial Control 1 (9-12 (High School))

## 1. Project 1591: Professional Modbus Industrial Control Driver Implementation

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Universal Translator"
At the end of this project, you will be able to connect to the worldwide network of factory machines and PLCs by implementing **Modbus RTU**, the industry-standard "language of industry". You will build a low-level driver that constructs raw binary packets, calculates CRC16 checksums, and communicates over RS-485 serial links.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Modbus RTU Protocol**: A master-slave architecture where data is organized into "Registers" (16-bit integers).
*   **CRC16 Checksum**: A mathematical proof attached to the end of every packet to ensure that electrical noise in the factory hasn't corrupted the data.
*   **Function 03 (Read Holding Registers)**: The command used to ask a machine for its current status (e.g., "Tell me your motor speed").

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   RS-485 to TTL Module (e.g., MAX485)
*   Micro USB Cable

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **RS-485 Module (DI)** | GP0 (TX) | Data In to Module |
| **RS-485 Module (RO)** | GP1 (RX) | Receiver Out from Module |
| **RS-485 Module (DE/RE)** | GP2 | Data/Receive Enable |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Network, drag `init_modbus_rtu`** (Serial config)
*   **from Data, drag `calculate_crc16`** (Validation logic)
*   **from Network, drag `send_modbus_query`** (Packet dispatch)

### 7. Variables
*   **modbus_uart**: Object (The UART channel).
*   **raw_frame**: Bytearray (The binary packet).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Schema Phase (Packet Construction)**
1.  **Define the Frame Structure**:
    *   Initialize a bytearray containing: `[Slave_ID, Function_Code, Start_Addr_HI, Start_Addr_LO, Count_HI, Count_LO]`.
2.  **Add Integrity**:
    *   Calculate the CRC16 for the frame.
    *   Append the cumulative bytes to the frame.

**Phase B: Logic Phase (Communication)**
3.  **The Serial Link**:
    *   Initialize `UART(0, baudrate=9600)`.
4.  **Transaction Loop**:
    *   Inside a loop, set GP2 to HIGH (Transmit Mode).
    *   Write the raw frame to the UART.
    *   Wait for the TX to complete, then set GP2 to LOW (Receive Mode).

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
5.  **Output Analysis**:
    *   Connect the Pico to a PC running a Modbus Slave Simulator.
    *   Verify the simulator receives: `01 03 00 00 00 01 84 0A`.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes UART0 with industrial settings (9600, 8N1).
2.  **Process**: The CPU assembles a binary request for "Register 40001".
3.  **Action**: The CRC block protects the packet against interference.
4.  **Sense**: The RS-485 module converts TTL signals to balanced differential voltage.
5.  **Result**: The industrial slave machine recognizes the request and prepares a response.
6.  **Output**: "Protocol: Modbus RTU | Speed: 9600 baud | Frame: [0x01, 0x03...]".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
from machine import UART, Pin
import struct
import time

# --- MODBUS CRC16 CALC ---
def get_crc(data):
    crc = 0xFFFF
    for byte in data:
        crc ^= byte
        for _ in range(8):
            if crc & 1:
                crc = (crc >> 1) ^ 0xA001
            else:
                crc >>= 1
    return struct.pack('<H', crc)

# Phase B: Logic Phase (Communication)
# 1. SETUP HARDWARE
uart = UART(0, baudrate=9600, tx=Pin(0), rx=Pin(1))
de_re = Pin(2, Pin.OUT) # DE/RE Pin for RS485 flow control

def read_motor_temp(slave_id, addr):
    # Phase A: Schema Phase (Packet Construction)
    # 2. CONSTRUCT MODBUS FRAME (Read 1 register)
    # [ID, Func, Addr_H, Addr_L, Count_H, Count_L]
    frame = bytearray([slave_id, 0x03, addr >> 8, addr & 0xFF, 0x00, 0x01])
    frame += get_crc(frame)
    
    # Phase B: Logic Phase (Communication)
    # 3. DISPATCH (TX MODE)
    de_re.value(1)
    uart.write(frame)
    time.sleep_ms(10) # Wait for buffer to clear before switching to RX
    de_re.value(0)
    
    # Phase C: Verification Phase
    # 4. CAPTURE RESPONSE
    time.sleep_ms(50)
    if uart.any():
        response = uart.read()
        print(f"Industrial Response: {response.hex()}")

while True:
    read_motor_temp(0x01, 0x0000)
    time.sleep(2)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ground Loops**: Industrial RS-485 cables often cover long distances. If your Pico and the machine don't share a common ground reference, the CRC will constantly fail due to voltage drift.
*   **DE/RE Timing**: If you switch the Enable pin back to LOW too fast, the last byte of your message will be cut off before it leaves the TX pin.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1592**: Requests are easy. Now learn to make your Pico act as the **Industrial Slave**, responding to queries **Asynchronously** while controlling a local motor.

## 1. Project 1592: Asynchronous Professional Modbus Industrial Control Handler

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Silent Partner"
At the end of this project, you will be able to integrate your Pico into a larger SCADA system by implementing a **Modbus RTU Slave Handler**. You will use `asyncio` to listen for requests from a Master PLC in the background while your Pico simultaneously manages high-speed hardware tasks (like LED indicators or cooling fans).

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Modbus Slave Role**: The Pico waits for a specific ID match before responding to any cable traffic.
*   **Non-Blocking Serial**: Using `uasyncio` to check for UART data without freezing the main application logic.
*   **Exception Codes**: Learning how to reply with "Error 02" if the Master asks for a sensor address that doesn't exist.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   RS-485 Module (MAX485)
*   USB-to-RS485 Adapter (for PC testing)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **MAX485 DI** | GP0 | Transmit |
| **MAX485 DE/RE** | GP2 | Flow Control |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Control, drag `on_uart_received`** (Trigger)
*   **from Logic, drag `if_address_match`** (Filter)
*   **from IoT, drag `send_modbus_exception`** (Error handling)

### 7. Variables
*   **my_slave_id**: 10 (Unique identifier).
*   **latest_reading**: Integer (Current sensor value).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Listening Phase**
1.  **Define the Slave Task**:
    *   Create `async def modbus_server()`.
    *   Continuously poll `uart.any()`. If bytes arrive, read the full frame.
2.  **Filter Logic**:
    *   Check if the first byte (ID) matches `my_slave_id`.
    *   If no match, ignore the data (it's for another machine).

**Phase B: Response Phase**
3.  **Construct the Payload**:
    *   If requested `addr == 40001`, prepare a response containing the 16-bit integer in `latest_reading`.
4.  **The ACK Pulse**:
    *   Format: `[ID, Func, ByteCount, DataHI, DataLO, CRC16]`.
    *   Send the bytes back over the wire.

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
5.  **Multi-Tasking Test**:
    *   Start a task that blinks an LED every 100ms.
    *   Send a Modbus query from the PC.
    *   Observe that the LED never stutters, even when the Pico is processing industrial requests.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico enters a perpetual background "Listening" loop.
2.  **Process**: A Master PLC sends a broadcast signal down the 200m factory cable.
3.  **Sense**: The Pico hears its ID (e.g., #10) and verifies the CRC.
4.  **Action**: The CPU pauses for a microsecond to package the local sensor data.
5.  **Result**: The Master receives the temperature update.
6.  **Output**: "State: SLAVE | Mode: ASYNC | ID: 10 | Status: RESPONDING".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import uasyncio as asyncio
from machine import UART, Pin
import struct

# (get_crc function from 1591 included here)

class ModbusSlave:
    def __init__(self, slave_id):
        self.sid = slave_id
        self.uart = UART(0, 9600)
        self.de_re = Pin(2, Pin.OUT)
        self.sensor_val = 1234 # Mock data

    async def run(self):
        print(f"Modbus Slave #{self.sid} Listening...")
        # Phase A: Listening Phase
        while True:
            if self.uart.any():
                frame = self.uart.read()
                if len(frame) >= 8 and frame[0] == self.sid:
                    # Logic for Function 03 (Read Holding Regs)
                    func = frame[1]
                    if func == 03:
                        # Phase B: Response Phase
                        # Construct Data Packet
                        # [ID, Func, ByteCount, ValH, ValL]
                        data = struct.pack(">HH", self.sensor_val, 0) # Payload
                        resp = bytearray([self.sid, func, 2]) + data[:2]
                        resp += get_crc(resp)
                        
                        # Reply
                        self.de_re.value(1)
                        self.uart.write(resp)
                        await asyncio.sleep_ms(5)
                        self.de_re.value(0)
                        print(f"Responded to Master: {self.sensor_val}")
            
            await asyncio.sleep(0.01)

# Phase C: Verification Phase
async def blink_task():
    led = Pin("LED", Pin.OUT)
    while True:
        led.toggle()
        await asyncio.sleep(0.1)

async def main():
    slave = ModbusSlave(10)
    asyncio.create_task(blink_task())
    await slave.run()

asyncio.run(main())
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Baud Rate Jitter**: If your loop is too slow and doesn't check `uart.any()` fast enough, the internal FIFO buffer (only 32-128 bytes) will fill up, causing truncated frames. Always use `asyncio.sleep(0.01)` to keep the loop tight.
*   **Endianness**: Modbus is strictly **Big Endian** (`>H`) for data, but the CRC16 is often appended **Little Endian** (`<H`). Swapping these will make the Master reject your data.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1593**: Hardcoding addresses is inflexible. Learn how to map your entire application's memory to a professional **Modbus Register Bank Class**.

## 1. Project 1593: Object-Oriented Professional Modbus Industrial Control Class

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Bank Vault"
At the end of this project, you will be able to architect high-performance industrial firmware by implementing a **Virtual Register Bank Class**. You will build a system where application variables (like RPM, Voltage, or Fault Codes) are automatically mapped to specific Modbus addresses, creating a clean bridge between your Python logic and the factory's interface.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Memory Mapping**: Associating a Python "Variable Name" with a numeric Modbus Address (e.g., `rpm_data` = `40005`).
*   **Register Virtualization**: Protecting your sensitive system variables by making them "Read-Only" to the Master PLC.
*   **Abstraction Layers**: Separating the "Radio/Serial" logic from the "Memory" logic.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   Pico Breadboard Kit

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico** | Internal | Memory Mapping Engine |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `Define Class`** (Object structure)
*   **from Logic, drag `Dictionary Map`** (Address book)
*   **from Data, drag `Getter/Setter`** (Access control)

### 7. Variables
*   **bank**: Instance of `RegisterBank`.
*   **data_map**: Dictionary (Address -> Value).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Structural Phase**
1.  **Define the Bank Constructor**:
    *   Create `self.registers = {}`.
    *   Pre-populate the dictionary with addresses `40001` through `40010` initialized to `0`.
2.  **Access Methods**:
    *   Create a method `update_registry(addr, value)`. This is used by your internal sensors.

**Phase B: Logic Phase (Modbus Bindings)**
3.  **Read Logic**:
    *   Add a method `get_frame(addr, count)`.
    *   Logic: It should look up the address, pack the values into a hex-friendly string, and return the block.
4.  **Validation**:
    *   If an address is requested that isn't in the `self.registers` dictionary, return an "Illegal Address" code.

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
5.  **Simulation**:
    *   Manually set `bank.update_registry(40005, 550)`.
    *   Simulate a Master query for 40005.
    *   Verify the bank returns `0x02 0x26` (Hex for 550).

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico initializes a segment of its RAM as the "Industrial Interface Store".
2.  **Process**: Sensors write their latest data into the Bank every 10ms.
3.  **Action**: The Modbus Driver (from 1592) asks the Bank "What is the value at 40005?".
4.  **Sense**: The Bank confirms the address is valid and returns the currentRPM.
5.  **Result**: Decoupled, efficient code where your sensors and your communications don't need to know about each other.
6.  **Output**: "Architecture: VIRTUAL_BANK | Capacity: 100 Regs | Mapping: DYNAMIC".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
class ModbusRegisterBank:
    # Phase A: Structural Phase
    def __init__(self):
        # Initialize registers 40001 - 40100
        self._regs = {addr: 0 for addr in range(40001, 40101)}

    def write_local(self, address, value):
        """Used by local sensors to update data"""
        if address in self._regs:
            # Ensure value fits in 16-bit unsigned (0-65535)
            self._regs[address] = value & 0xFFFF

    # Phase B: Logic Phase (Modbus Bindings)
    def read_for_master(self, start_addr, count):
        """Used by the driver to fulfill Master requests"""
        try:
            results = []
            for i in range(count):
                addr = 40001 + start_addr + i
                results.append(self._regs[addr])
            return results
        except KeyError:
            return None # To be caught as Illegal Address exception

# Phase C: Verification Phase
# --- Usage Example ---
bank = ModbusRegisterBank()

# Scenario: Motor logic updates RPM
current_rpm = 1500
bank.write_local(40001, current_rpm)

# Scenario: PLC asks for 2 registers starting at index 0 (Addr 40001)
# frame_data = bank.read_for_master(0, 2)
# print(f"Sending to PLC: {frame_data}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **0-Based vs 1-Based**: In Modbus documentation, registers are called `40001`, but in the raw packet, the address sent is `0x0000`. If you don't subtract `1` in your class logic, the PLC will always be "off by one" when reading your data.
*   **Integer Overflow**: Python handles massive numbers, but Modbus registers ONLY hold 16 bits. If you write `70000` to the bank, it must be clipped or it will corrupt the binary frame.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1594**: A bank is only as good as its data integrity. Learn how to implement a **Robust Controller** that validates every bit with a high-speed CRC16 engine.

## 1. Project 1594: Robust Professional Modbus Industrial Control Controller

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Iron Dome"
At the end of this project, you will be able to ensure consistent data integrity in harsh industrial environments by implementing a high-speed **CRC16 (Cyclic Redundancy Check)** validation engine. You will learn how to protect your factory automation from "Bit Flips" caused by electrical interference from nearby motors or welders.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Polynomial Division**: The mathematical basis for CRC, treating data as a giant number and finding the remainder.
*   **Packet Sanitization**: Discarding messages that arrive with correct formatting but incorrect checksums.
*   **Industrial Hardening**: Designing software that "fails silent" on noise instead of executing random, dangerous commands.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   Pico Breadboard Kit

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Logic Tester** | Internal | CRC Engine Validation |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Math, drag `Bitwise XOR`** (Logic operation)
*   **from Logic, drag `CRC Verify`** (Validation block)
*   **from System, drag `Discard Buffer`** (Sanitization)

### 7. Variables
*   **poly**: 0xA001 (Modbus Standard Polynomial).
*   **input_bytes**: The raw packet received from the wire.

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Implementation Phase (The Math)**
1.  **Initialize the Register**:
    *   Set `crc = 0xFFFF`.
2.  **The Bit-Cruncher**:
    *   For every byte in the list, XOR it with the CRC.
    *   Shift the bits 8 times. If a bit is "carried" out, XOR with `poly`.

**Phase B: Logic Phase (The Filter)**
3.  **The Gatekeeper**:
    *   When a packet arrives (e.g., `01 03 00 00 00 01`), calculate its CRC.
    *   Compare your result with the last two bytes of the physical packet.
4.  **Action**:
    *   If they match exactly, pass the data to the sensors.
    *   If they differ by even one bit, throw the data away immediately and blink a RED error LED.

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
5.  **Stress Test**:
    *   Feed the function a known good string: `\x01\x03\x00\x00\x00\x01`.
    *   The engine should output `\x84\x0A`. If it outputs anything else, the industrial link is broken.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A Master controller sends a command through a noisy 100-meter cable.
2.  **Sense**: The Pico captures a burst of 8 bytes.
3.  **Process**: The CRC engine runs a 16-microsecond check on the data.
4.  **Result**: The engine detects that a "High" voltage spike changed a `0` to a `1` in the address field.
5.  **Action**: The Pico discards the frame, refusing to move the motor to an incorrect position.
6.  **Output**: "Integrity: FAIL | Action: IGNORE | Security: ACTIVE".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import struct

# Phase A: Implementation Phase (The Math)
def modbus_crc16(data):
    """Calculates the 16-bit CRC for Modbus RTU"""
    crc = 0xFFFF
    for byte in data:
        crc ^= byte
        for _ in range(8):
            if crc & 1:
                crc = (crc >> 1) ^ 0xA001 # Modbus Polynomial
            else:
                crc >>= 1
    # Modbus expects Little Endian CRC (LSB first)
    return struct.pack('<H', crc)

# Phase B: Logic Phase (The Filter)
def validate_packet(packet):
    """Checks if the incoming packet's CRC is correct"""
    if len(packet) < 4: return False
    
    # Split data from the received CRC
    data = packet[:-2]
    received_crc = packet[-2:]
    
    # Calculate what it SHOULD be
    calculated_crc = modbus_crc16(data)
    
    if calculated_crc == received_crc:
        print("[OK] CRC Match. Packet valid.")
        return True
    else:
        print("[ERR] CRC Mismatch! Potential corruption.")
        return False

# Phase C: Verification Phase
# Test Case
msg = b'\x01\x03\x00\x00\x00\x01'
checksum = modbus_crc16(msg)
print(f"Validated Frame: {(msg + checksum).hex()}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Byte Order**: Some PLC manufacturers use Big Endian for CRC. If your validation fails but the data looks correct, try swapping the result of `struct.pack('<H', crc)`.
*   **Empty Buffer**: If your Serial buffer is empty and you run the CRC on nothing, it will return `0xFFFF`. Always check `len(packet) > 0` before calculating.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1595**: Discarding data is safe, but sometimes you need to tell the Master WHY you couldn't process the request. Build a **Modbus Exception Handler**.

## 1. Project 1595: Professional Modbus Industrial Control with Error Handling

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Polite Dismissal"
At the end of this project, you will be able to standardize your system's diagnostics by implementing **Modbus Exception Responses**. You will learn how to communicate specific error states (like "Register Not Found" or "Illegal Value") back to the Master controller using the formal industrial protocol codes 01, 02, and 03.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Exception Codes**: Standardized replies (01 = Illegal Function, 02 = Illegal Address, 03 = Illegal Data).
*   **Function Masking**: Modbus signals an error by setting the high bit of the Function Code (e.g., Function `03` becomes `83` in the error response).
*   **Safe Recover**: Responding to errors without crashing the main Python loop.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   RS-485 Module

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico** | Internal | Fault Diagnostics Engine |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `If/Else Branch`** (Range checking)
*   **from Network, drag `Send Modbus Exception`** (Response builder)
*   **from Logic, drag `Bitwise OR`** (Status flag)

### 7. Variables
*   **error_code**: Integer (1, 2, or 3).
*   **max_register**: 40100 (The ceiling).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Validation Phase**
1.  **Check Range**:
    *   When a request arrives for address `X`, check if `X > max_register`.
2.  **Define the Code**:
    *   If out of range, set `error_code = 0x02`.

**Phase B: Response Phase**
3.  **Construct Error Frame**:
    *   Byte 0: Slave ID.
    *   Byte 1: Original Function Code ORed with `0x80`.
    *   Byte 2: The `error_code`.
4.  **Finalize**:
    *   Add CRC and send over UART.

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
5.  **The Mistake Test**:
    *   Use a PC to ask for Register `99999`.
    *   Verify the Pico replies with `01 83 02 [CRC]`. This tells the human operator exactly where the configuration error is.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A Master PLC accidentally asks for a sensor that doesn't exist on this machine.
2.  **Process**: The Pico's Logic Engine checks the "Register Bank Table".
3.  **Action**: The CPU detects a "KeyError" (Address missing).
4.  **Sense**: Instead of crashing or ignoring, the Pico builds a formal "Exception Frame".
5.  **Result**: The PLC displays a clear warning on the factory HMI screen: "Illegal Data Address at Node 10".
6.  **Output**: "Status: FAULT | Code: 02 | Mode: PROTOCOL-RECOVERY".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
# (modbus_crc16 function from 1594 included here)

# Phase B: Response Phase
def send_exception(uart_obj, slave_id, func_code, exception_code):
    """Sends a formal Modbus error response"""
    # Bitwise OR with 0x80 tells the master this IS an error response
    error_func = func_code | 0x80
    
    # Packet: [ID, ErrorFunc, ExceptionCode]
    frame = bytearray([slave_id, error_func, exception_code])
    frame += modbus_crc16(frame)
    
    # Dispatch
    uart_obj.write(frame)
    print(f"[REPLY] Sent Exception Code {exception_code} for Func {func_code}")

# Logic Example:
# Phase A: Validation Phase
def handle_master_request(addr, count):
    if addr < 0 or addr > 100:
        # Code 02 = ILLEGAL DATA ADDRESS
        send_exception(my_uart, 1, 0x03, 0x02)
    elif count > 10:
        # Code 03 = ILLEGAL DATA VALUE (asking for too much at once)
        send_exception(my_uart, 1, 0x03, 0x03)
    else:
        print("Address valid. Processing...")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Ignoring Errors**: Many beginners let the code crash on a bad address. In factories, a crash can lead to equipment damage. Always respond with an exception code so the Master knows why the data stopped.
*   **Wrong Function Bit**: If you send `02` as the function code instead of `83`, the Master will think you are sending "Read Discrete Inputs" data, leading to total data corruption in the SCADA system.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1596**: Error handling is robust but slow. Optimize your throughput by implementing **Bulk Register Reading** to fetch 10-20 variables in a single packet.

## 1. Project 1596: Optimized Professional Modbus Industrial Control Algorithm

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Firehose"
At the end of this project, you will be able to maximize your factory's data throughput by implementing **Bulk Transaction Optimization**. You will learn how to drastically reduce network congestion and CPU overhead by fetching up to 125 registers in a single Modbus packet instead of sending 125 individual requests.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Payload Packing**: Stashing multiple 16-bit integers into a single binary stream.
*   **Network Latency**: Understanding that one big packet takes much less time than many small ones due to "Wait Times" between frames.
*   **Byte Count Field**: Dynamically calculating the size of a response based on how many registers were requested.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   USB-to-RS485 Adapter

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico** | Internal | High-Speed Buffer Engine |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `Variable Length List`** (Dynamic sizing)
*   **from Math, drag `Multiply by 2`** (Bytes vs Registers)
*   **from Network, drag `Bulk Write`** (UART stream)

### 7. Variables
*   **reg_count**: Integer (1 to 125).
*   **byte_count**: Integer (`reg_count * 2`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Strategy Phase**
1.  **Define the Limit**:
    *   Set max registers per query to `20`.
2.  **Logic Logic**:
    *   If Master asks for Address 0, Count 10: Calculate `byte_count = 20`.

**Phase B: Assembly Phase**
3.  **The Loop**:
    *   Create a bytearray. Loop 10 times.
    *   For each register, append the High Byte and then the Low Byte (`Val >> 8` and `Val & 0xFF`).
4.  **Header Attachment**:
    *   Prepend the `Slave_ID`, `Function_Code`, and `Byte_Count` to the front of your bytearray.

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
5.  **Speed Test**:
    *   Poll 5 registers individualy. Time it.
    *   Poll 5 registers as a Bulk Read. Time it.
    *   Observe that the Bulk Read is nearly 5x faster because you only calculate CRC once and only wait for one "Round Trip".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A SCADA Dashboard needs to update 10 different dials (V, A, W, Temp, etc.).
2.  **Sense**: Instead of 10 pings, it sends one command: `READ 40001, COUNT 10`.
3.  **Process**: The Pico fetches all 10 values from RAM in a single millisecond.
4.  **Action**: The Pico sends back 20 bytes of data in one continuous burst.
5.  **Result**: The dashboard updates instantly with silky-smooth movement.
6.  **Output**: "Efficiency: 100% | Latency: MINIMAL | Pipeline: OPTIMIZED".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
# (modbus_crc16 function from 1594 included here)

# Phase B: Assembly Phase
def bulk_read_response(slave_id, registers):
    """
    Constructs a Modbus response for multiple registers.
    registers: a list of 16-bit integers
    """
    byte_count = len(registers) * 2
    
    # [ID, Func, ByteCount]
    frame = bytearray([slave_id, 0x03, byte_count])
    
    # [DataHI, DataLO, DataHI, DataLO...]
    for val in registers:
        frame.append((val >> 8) & 0xFF)
        frame.append(val & 0xFF)
        
    # [CRC]
    frame += modbus_crc16(frame)
    return frame

# Example Usage
sensor_data = [24, 55, 120, 0, 99] # 5 sensors
packet = bulk_read_response(1, sensor_data)
print(f"Bulk Frame (5 Sensors): {packet.hex()}")
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Limit Violations**: A standard Modbus RTU packet cannot exceed 256 bytes. If you try to Bulk Read 150 registers, the packet would be > 300 bytes, causing the Pico to overflow its memory or the Master to time out. Always limit reads to 125 registers.
*   **Incorrect Byte Count**: Forgetting that 1 Register = 2 Bytes. If you requested 5 registers but sent `ByteCount = 5` instead of `10`, the Master will disconnect.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1597**: Now that your throughput is high, build a real **Industrial Data Pipeline** that streams Pico temperature data into a PC-based SCADA simulator.

## 1. Project 1597: Professional Modbus Industrial Control Data Pipeline

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Control Room"
At the end of this project, you will be able to integrate your embedded sensors into a world-class monitoring system by implementing a **Modbus SCADA Data Pipeline**. You will learn how to feed real-time Pico sensor data into a PC-based HMI (Human Machine Interface) using industry-standard dashboarding tools.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **SCADA (Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition)**: The software systems used to monitor entire factories.
*   **Polling Cycles**: Configuring a master to request data at fixed intervals (e.g., every 1 second).
*   **Fixed-Point Math**: Representing a temperature like `22.5` as an integer `225` because Modbus only supports integers without decimals.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   USB-to-RS485 Adapter
*   PC running "QModMaster" or "Modbus Poll"

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico** | Internal | Industrial Data Source |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Data, drag `Fixed Point Convert`** (Math scaling)
*   **from Network, drag `Update Register`** (Live data refresh)
*   **from IoT, drag `Stream to SCADA`** (HMI bridge)

### 7. Variables
*   **system_temp**: Float (Sensor reading).
*   **modbus_val**: Integer (`temp * 10`).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Producer Phase (Pico)**
1.  **Read and Scale**:
    *   Read the internal temperature sensor.
    *   Multiply by `10` to preserve one decimal place (e.g., `25.6` becomes `256`).
2.  **Write to Bank**:
    *   Store this value in `Modbus Register 40001`.

**Phase B: Consumer Phase (PC)**
3.  **Setup the Master**:
    *   Connect the RS-485 adapter to the PC.
    *   Open QModMaster and set: `ID: 10`, `Register: 40001`, `Poll: 1000ms`.
4.  **Visualize**:
    *   Enable the "Plotting" or "Trend" view in the software.

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
5.  **The Heat Test**:
    *   Place your finger on the Pico's CPU.
    *   Verify that the graph on your PC screen rises in real-time. This confirms the pipeline is effectively bridging the physical and digital worlds.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico begins its "Master Loop," updating memory every 50ms.
2.  **Process**: The CPU takes a raw voltage reading and converts it to a standard industrial integer.
3.  **Action**: The Modbus Slave task waits patiently for the PC's heartbeat.
4.  **Sense**: Every 1 second, the PC sends a request query.
5.  **Result**: The high-precision temperature data flows from the silicon to the PC dashboard.
6.  **Output**: "Pipeline: SCADA-LINK | Buffer: 40001 | Sync: 1Hz | Quality: 100%".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import machine
import time

# (Assume ModbusSlave class from 1592 is used)

def get_internal_temp():
    sensor_temp = machine.ADC(4)
    reading = sensor_temp.read_u16() * (3.3 / 65535)
    temperature = 27 - (reading - 0.706)/0.001721
    return temperature

# Setup Register Bank
bank = ModbusRegisterBank() # from 1593

# Phase A: Producer Phase (Pico)
while True:
    # 1. READ SENSOR
    t = get_internal_temp()
    
    # 2. CONVERT TO FIXED POINT (25.6 -> 256)
    data_to_send = int(t * 10)
    
    # 3. UPDATE BANK
    bank.write_local(40001, data_to_send)
    print(f"Update Registry: {data_to_send}")
    
    # Phase B: Consumer Phase (PC)
    # 4. LISTEN FOR PC
    # In a real app, this would be inside the ModbusSlave task
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Scaling Mismatch**: If you send `256` but the PC software is expecting a standard 16-bit float, it might display `0.00003`. Ensure the HMI software is configured for "Unsigned 16-bit Integer" and use a "Scale Factor" of `0.1`.
*   **Timeout Errors**: If your "Wait Time" on the PC is set to 100ms but the Pico takes 150ms to read sensors, you will see constant "Timeout" errors. Set PC timeouts to at least 500ms.
