## 1. Project 1598: Secure Professional Modbus Industrial Control Interface

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Security Guard"
At the end of this project, you will be able to enforce strict security and safety boundaries in your firmware by implementing **Register Access Control**. You will learn how to divide your Modbus memory into "Read-Only" (Safe) and "Read-Write" (Command) zones, ensuring a remote operator cannot accidentally overwrite critical system parameters or factory safety limits.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Permission Masking**: Using a list or dictionary to keep track of which registers are "Locked" to the outside world.
*   **Function 06 (Write Single Register)**: The command used by operators to change settings remotely.
*   **Unauthorized Access Rejection**: Automatically firing back Exception Code 03 if someone tries to write to a locked sensor address.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   RS-485 Module

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Pico** | Internal | Access Permission Engine |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Logic, drag `In List?`** (Permission check)
*   **from Control, drag `Rejection Branch`** (Security logic)
*   **from Data, drag `Constant List`** (The Blacklist)

### 7. Variables
*   **locked_regs**: List `[40001, 40002]`.
*   **incoming_addr**: Integer (The master's target).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Configuration Phase**
1.  **Define the Policy**:
    *   Create a list: `READ_ONLY = [40001, 40002]` (Sensor registers).
    *   Registers like `40010` (Target Speed) are NOT in the list.

**Phase B: Logic Phase (The Guard)**
2.  **Intercept the Write**:
    *   Inside the Modbus handler, check if the `Function == 0x06`.
3.  **Evaluate Permission**:
    *   If `target_addr` is in `READ_ONLY`:
        *   Trigger `send_exception(0x01, 0x06, 0x03)`.
    *   Else:
        *   Update the register value and send an ACK.

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
4.  **Security Penetration Test**:
    *   Use a PC to try and write "999" to the Temperature sensor (40001).
    *   Verify that the write is rejected and the original temperature reading remains unchanged and safe.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: A rogue or poorly configured PC tries to overwrite the Pico's "Read-Only" safety limits.
2.  **Sense**: The Pico's UART captures a "Write Request" to a protected area.
3.  **Process**: The Access Manager checks the `READ_ONLY` list.
4.  **Action**: The CPU blocks the write and generates a formal "Illegal Data" warning.
5.  **Result**: Critical system variables remain untouched, preventing potential hardware damage.
6.  **Output**: "Access: DENIED | Address: 40001 | Security: ENFORCED".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
# (Assume send_exception and ModbusRegisterBank are defined)

# Phase A: Configuration Phase
READ_ONLY_REGISTERS = [40001, 40002, 40003]

# Phase B: Logic Phase (The Guard)
def handle_write_command(addr, value):
    """
    Decides whether to allow a remote Master to change a local variable.
    """
    # 1. CHECK PERMISSIONS
    if addr in READ_ONLY_REGISTERS:
        print(f"[SEC] Unauthorized write attempt to {addr} BLOCKED.")
        # Return Exception 03 (Illegal Data Value/Address for writing)
        return (False, 0x03)
    
    # 2. ALLOWED
    print(f"[SEC] Config change allowed: {addr} = {value}")
    # Update local memory
    # bank.write_local(addr, value)
    return (True, None)

# Phase C: Verification Phase
# Usage Simulation
success, error = handle_write_command(40001, 500)
if not success:
    # send_exception_to_uart(error)
    pass
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **The "Write Everything" Trap**: Beginners often give the Master full access to all memory. This is dangerous. Sensors (Input) should ALWAYS be Read-Only. Only Motors/Setpoints (Actuators) should be Writable.
*   **Off-By-One Logic**: Remember that the PLC writes to `40005`, but the packet says `0x0004`. Your permission list must account for this offset.

### 12. Try This Next
*   **Project 1599**: Modbus RTU is limited to a single cable. Expand your horizons by building a **Modbus Gateway** that bridges data between RS-485 (Serial) and WiFi (TCP).

## 1. Project 1599: Multi-Threaded Professional Modbus Industrial Control

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Bridge"
At the end of this project, you will be able to upgrade your factory's connectivity by implementing a **Modbus RTU-to-TCP Gateway**. You will learn to use both cores of the Pico W to bridge two different worlds: Core 0 handles the high-speed Serial (RS-485) machine traffic, while Core 1 manages the Network (WiFi/Socket) traffic from the internet.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **Bridge Architecture**: Relaying a command from one protocol (TCP/IP) to another (Serial) in real-time.
*   **Core Distribution**: Giving each protocol its own dedicated CPU so they don't fight for timing.
*   **Socket Programming**: Accepting raw binary connections over WiFi Port 502 (The Modbus TCP Standard).

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico W
*   RS-485 Module
*   WiFi Access Point

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Serial Machine** | UART0 | RS-485 Slave |
| **PC Manager** | WiFi | Modbus TCP Master |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from System, drag `Initialize Core 1`** (Thread spawn)
*   **from Network, drag `Listen on Port 502`** (Socket init)
*   **from Logic, drag `Message Relay`** (Buffer swap)

### 7. Variables
*   **tcp_socket**: The listening port.
*   **serial_buffer**: Bytes (Relay payload).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Core 1 Phase (The Internet Ear)**
1.  **Socket Setup**:
    *   Connect to WiFi.
    *   Create a socket and `bind` to Port 502.
2.  **The Waiter**:
    *   Listen for a PC connection. Once connected, read 12 bytes (Modbus TCP header).
    *   Strip the TCP header and put the Modbus command into a shared "Bridge Queue".

**Phase B: Core 0 Phase (The Factory Mouth)**
3.  **The Translator**:
    *   Check the Bridge Queue.
    *   If a command is there, add a CRC16 and send it out the GP0/GP1 UART pins to the old machines.
4.  **The Return Path**:
    *   Wait 50ms for the serial response. Send the machines' answer back to Core 1.

**Phase C: Verification Phase**
5.  **Remote Control Test**:
    *   Use a Smartphone App (like ModBus Monitor) on the same WiFi.
    *   Connect to the Pico's IP address.
    *   Verify you can control the RS-485 machine from your phone through the Pico bridge.

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: Two specialized tasks begin on separate CPUs.
2.  **Sense**: A modern Cloud server sends a command over WiFi to stop a motor.
3.  **Process**: Core 1 captures the WiFi packet and translates it for the legacy serial machine.
4.  **Action**: Core 0 whispers the command over the RS-485 wires.
5.  **Sense**: The machine stops and confirms.
6.  **Result**: A 40-year-old machine is now "Cloud Connected" via the Pico W Gateway.

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import _thread
import socket
import network
import time
from machine import UART

# (Assume CRC and UART logic from 1591)

# Phase A: Core 1 Phase (The Internet Ear)
def tcp_gateway_core_1():
    """Manages the WiFi / Internet side of things"""
    s = socket.socket()
    s.bind(('', 502))
    s.listen(1)
    print("Gateway TCP Server Ready on Port 502")
    
    while True:
        conn, addr = s.accept()
        data = conn.recv(128)
        if data:
            # Modbus TCP has a 6-byte header
            # We relay the payload to Core 0 (Simplified global bridge)
            print(f"Relaying TCP request from {addr}")
            # ... Relay Logic ...
        conn.close()

# Start the WiFi listener on the second core
_thread.start_new_thread(tcp_gateway_core_1, ())

# Phase B: Core 0 Phase (The Factory Mouth)
# Core 0 Main Loop
while True:
    # Handle the Serial machine side
    # if tcp_bridge_available:
    #    send_to_serial_machine()
    time.sleep(1)
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Blocking Sockets**: If you use `s.setblocking(True)`, the Pico will freeze while waiting for a connection. Always use a short timeout or `select` to keep the bridge alive.
*   **MTU Size**: Modbus TCP packets can be slightly larger. Ensure your buffers are at least 300 bytes.

### 12. Try This Next

## 1. Project 1600: Industrial Professional Modbus Industrial Control Solution

### 2. Learning Objective
"The Brain"
At the end of this project, you will be able to reach the summit of industrial engineering by building a **Virtual PLC (Programmable Logic Controller)**. You will learn to implement "Scan-Cycle" logic, where your Pico continuously evaluates a set of rules (e.g., "If Pressure > 100, Open Valve") while responding to remote master commands simultaneously.

### 3. Concepts Introduced
*   **PLC Scan Cycle**: The three-stage process: 1. Read Inputs, 2. Execute Logic, 3. Update Outputs.
*   **Autonomous Logic**: Deciding to act locally without waiting for a command from a PC.
*   **Systemic Safety**: Implementing a "Watchdog" that resets the system if the PLC loop becomes unresponsive.

### 4. Hardware Required
*   Raspberry Pi Pico
*   Potentiometer (The Sensor)
*   1x Relay Module (The Actuator)

### 5. Wiring / Interfaces
| Component | Pico Pin | Notes |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Relay IN** | GP15 | Factory Output |
| **Potentiometer** | GP26 (ADC) | Factory Input |

### 6. Blocks Used
*   **from Control, drag `Loop Scan Cycle`** (Industrial timing)
*   **from Logic, drag `Execute Logic Table`** (Rule engine)
*   **from IoT, drag `HMI Sync`** (Status report)

### 7. Variables
*   **pressure_reg**: 40001 (Input).
*   **pump_state_reg**: 40005 (Output).

### 8. Step-by-Step Guide

**Phase A: Input Phase**
1.  **Scan Sensors**:
    *   Read GP26. Scale the 0-65535 value to 0-300 PSI.
    *   Write this to `bank register 40001`.

**Phase B: Logic Phase (The Rule)**
2.  **Evaluate Boolean**:
    *   Check `if bank.read(40001) > 200`:
        *   Set `bank register 40005 = 0` (Pump OFF).
    *   Else:
        *   Maintain current state.

**Phase C: Output Phase**
3.  **Apply to Hardware**:
    *   Update GP15 based on the value in `register 40005`.

**Phase D: Verification Phase**
4.  **Commissioning Test**:
    *   Turn the potentiometer past the "200 PSI" mark.
    *   Verify the relay clicks OFF immediately.
    *   Verify the PC dashboard simultaneously updates to show "PRESSURE: HIGH | STATUS: CRITICAL".

### 9. Execution Flow
1.  **Start**: The Pico PLC enters its infinite "Scan Loop."
2.  **Read**: The CPU samples the analog environment and populates the Modbus memory.
3.  **Logic**: The Rule Engine compares current data against safety thresholds.
4.  **Action**: The Pico decides to shut down the pump to prevent a simulated explosion.
5.  **Communicate**: The Modbus Slave task informs the distant operator of the automatic shutdown.
6.  **Output**: "Mode: PLC | Logic: FAIL-SAFE | Cycle Time: 2ms | State: SECURE".

### 10. Generated Code
```python
import time
from machine import ADC, Pin

# (Assume ModbusRegisterBank class is available)
regs = ModbusRegisterBank()
pump_relay = Pin(15, Pin.OUT)
pressure_adc = ADC(26)

def plc_scan_cycle():
    # Phase A: Input Phase
    # 1. READ INPUTS
    raw_p = pressure_adc.read_u16()
    pressure_psi = int(raw_p * (300 / 65535))
    regs.write_local(40001, pressure_psi)

    # Phase B: Logic Phase (The Rule)
    # 2. EVALUATE LOGIC
    # Rule: If pressure exceeds 250 PSI, Force Pump OFF
    if pressure_psi > 250:
        regs.write_local(40005, 0)
        print("[PLC] SAFETY TRIPPED: Pressure Too High!")
    
    # Phase C: Output Phase
    # 3. UPDATE OUTPUTS
    # The actuator follows the register state
    pump_relay.value(regs.read_for_master(4, 1)[0])

# Main Industrial Loop
while True:
    start_time = time.ticks_ms()
    
    plc_scan_cycle()
    
    # Ensure cycle time is consistent (approx 100ms)
    elapsed = time.ticks_diff(time.ticks_ms(), start_time)
    time.sleep_ms(max(0, 100 - elapsed))
```

### 11. Common Mistakes
*   **Unbounded Loops**: If your PLC logic has a `while True` inside it, your Modbus comms will never run, and your factory will lose communication. Always keep the scan cycle non-blocking.
*   **Floating State**: Ensure your logic accounts for the "Hysteresis". If you flip the pump ON/OFF exactly at 250, it will "chatter" (rapidly flip) if the pressure is 249.9. Use a buffer range.
